# Daily Summary: 2025-06-21

### Using BDF schemes in the temporal integration of POD-ROM methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14543v1)
- **Authors**: Bosco García-Archilla, Alicia García-Mascaraque, Julia Novo
- **Abstract**: In this paper we consider the numerical approximation of a semilinear reaction-diffusion model problem (PDEs) by means of reduced order methods (ROMs) based on proper orthogonal decomposition (POD). We focus on the time integration of the fully discrete reduced order model. Most of the analysis in the literature has been carried out for the implicit Euler method as time integrator. We integrate in time the reduced order model with the BDF-q time stepping ($1\le q\le 5$) and prove optimal rate of convergence of order $q$ in time. Our set of snapshots is obtained from finite element approximations to the original model problem computed at different times. These finite element approximations can be obtained with any time integrator. The POD method is based on first order difference quotients of the snapshots. The reason for doing this is twofold. On the one hand, the use of difference quotients allow us to provide pointwise-in-time error bounds. On the other, the use of difference quotients is essential to get the expected rate $q$ in time since we apply that the BDF-q time stepping, $1\le q\le 5$, can be written as a linear combination of first order difference quotients.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper analyzes the numerical approximation of semilinear reaction-diffusion equations using reduced-order methods (ROMs) based on Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (POD). The main focus is on time integration using Backward Differentiation Formulae (BDF-q) of orders 1 to 5. The authors prove optimal convergence rates in time for these schemes when applied to the reduced-order model. A key feature of their approach is the use of first-order difference quotients of snapshots in the POD basis construction, which enables pointwise-in-time error estimates and allows expressing BDF-q schemes as linear combinations of these difference quotients. Numerical experiments are presented to validate the theoretical results. The authors use  HJ-projections which they argue provide tighter bounds compared to L2-projections and they build upon prior work especially concerning stability conditions for BDF schemes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in extending the analysis of POD-ROMs with BDF schemes to orders higher than 2, specifically up to order 5. While the use of POD-ROMs and BDF schemes individually is well-established, a comprehensive error analysis for higher-order BDF schemes within the POD-ROM context, especially for semilinear problems and with pointwise-in-time estimates, represents a nontrivial contribution. The technique of expressing BDF-q as a linear combination of first-order difference quotients to achieve optimal temporal convergence rates is a valuable contribution. The use of HJ-projections instead of L2 projections, as in previous works, strengthens the results and is a step forward, but as stated by the authors, this analysis can also be carried out for L2 projections.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the widespread use of higher-order time integrators in practical simulations. Providing a rigorous error analysis for these schemes applied to POD-ROMs is crucial for justifying their use and understanding their accuracy. The paper bridges a gap in the literature by providing theoretical guarantees for a wider range of time integration methods. Moreover, pointwise-in-time error estimates are more informative than global error bounds, making the results practically relevant. While the model problem is a semilinear reaction-diffusion equation, the techniques developed are potentially applicable to a broader class of problems. However, the analysis has only been carried out for BDF schemes, which are known to be very effective for stiff problems but are not adequate for, say, oscillatory problems. Also, the study is restricted to BDF schemes of order less or equal than 5; the authors comment that the analysis for the BDF-6 scheme could be carried out, but would be more complicated.

*   **Strengths:** The paper presents a clear and rigorous error analysis. The use of difference quotients is a clever technique for obtaining optimal temporal convergence. The numerical experiments are well-designed and support the theoretical findings. The references are comprehensive, indicating a thorough understanding of the existing literature.

*   **Weaknesses:** The paper's scope is somewhat limited by the specific choice of time integration schemes (BDF) and the model problem (semilinear reaction-diffusion). While the techniques are likely generalizable, the authors don't explicitly discuss the applicability to other types of problems or time integrators (e.g., Runge-Kutta methods). Although the authors mention the BDF-6 method, a full analysis of it is omitted. Also, the analysis requires the problem to be smooth enough, which is not always guaranteed for all physical processes. The reliance on Lipschitz continuity of the nonlinear term is a standard but potentially restrictive assumption. As the authors mention, in practice a same method as in [10, Theorem 2] can be carried out.
    The numerical section is limited as it includes one single problem and one single value of the POD dimension.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging the use of higher-order BDF schemes in POD-ROM simulations with a stronger theoretical justification. The techniques developed for error analysis could be adopted and extended by other researchers.

*   **Justification of the Score:** Taking into account the points made above, the novelty is good but the significance is moderate. The theoretical framework for POD-ROMs with higher-order BDF schemes contributes meaningfully to the field. However, the analysis is not as general as it could be. Also, although the authors mention the BDF-6 method, a full analysis of it is omitted. Moreover, the numerical section is limited as it includes one single problem and one single value of the POD dimension. The methods could be useful to a considerable number of scientists.
    It is a well-written paper with a clear proof.

### DreamLight: Towards Harmonious and Consistent Image Relighting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14549v1)
- **Authors**: Yong Liu, Wenpeng Xiao, Qianqian Wang, Junlin Chen, Shiyin Wang, Yitong Wang, Xinglong Wu, Yansong Tang
- **Abstract**: We introduce a model named DreamLight for universal image relighting in this work, which can seamlessly composite subjects into a new background while maintaining aesthetic uniformity in terms of lighting and color tone. The background can be specified by natural images (image-based relighting) or generated from unlimited text prompts (text-based relighting). Existing studies primarily focus on image-based relighting, while with scant exploration into text-based scenarios. Some works employ intricate disentanglement pipeline designs relying on environment maps to provide relevant information, which grapples with the expensive data cost required for intrinsic decomposition and light source. Other methods take this task as an image translation problem and perform pixel-level transformation with autoencoder architecture. While these methods have achieved decent harmonization effects, they struggle to generate realistic and natural light interaction effects between the foreground and background. To alleviate these challenges, we reorganize the input data into a unified format and leverage the semantic prior provided by the pretrained diffusion model to facilitate the generation of natural results. Moreover, we propose a Position-Guided Light Adapter (PGLA) that condenses light information from different directions in the background into designed light query embeddings, and modulates the foreground with direction-biased masked attention. In addition, we present a post-processing module named Spectral Foreground Fixer (SFF) to adaptively reorganize different frequency components of subject and relighted background, which helps enhance the consistency of foreground appearance. Extensive comparisons and user study demonstrate that our DreamLight achieves remarkable relighting performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DreamLight paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DreamLight, a novel model for universal image relighting. It seamlessly integrates a subject into a new background, maintaining aesthetic consistency in lighting and color, whether the background is specified by an image or generated from a text prompt. DreamLight addresses limitations of existing approaches by using a unified data format and leveraging the semantic prior from pre-trained diffusion models. Key contributions include a Position-Guided Light Adapter (PGLA) that modulates foreground lighting based on background directionality and a Spectral Foreground Fixer (SFF) that enhances foreground consistency.  The authors demonstrate the model's effectiveness through extensive comparisons and user studies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing concepts in a novel way to address the universal image relighting task.

    *   The **unified model for image and text-based relighting** is a valuable contribution. While IC-Light also tackles both types of relighting, DreamLight offers a more integrated solution, and avoids the structural simplifications.
    *   The **Position-Guided Light Adapter (PGLA)** is a well-motivated module that intelligently directs background light to better integrate the foreground.  This is a crucial step towards creating realistic light interactions.
    *   The **Spectral Foreground Fixer (SFF)** addresses a common artifact in diffusion-based compositing, improving subject consistency.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and challenging problem in image composition and editing. The ability to seamlessly relight images with arbitrary backgrounds (specified by images or text) has broad applications in various fields, from virtual reality to film production. The qualitative results provided support the method's ability to achieve more natural and realistic compositions than existing methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Approach:** DreamLight elegantly handles both image and text-based relighting within a single model.
    *   **Effective Modules:** PGLA and SFF contribute meaningfully to improved light interaction and foreground consistency.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides thorough qualitative and quantitative comparisons against several baselines and related works. The user study further validates the effectiveness of the model.
    *   **Data Generation pipeline:** The description of data generation process is a nice to have.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Approach:** While the combination of techniques is novel and effective, the individual components (diffusion models, attention mechanisms, etc.) are not entirely groundbreaking.  The paper builds on established techniques.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper includes some ablation, a more detailed analysis of the relative importance of each component would further strengthen the results and reveal the architectural robustness of DreamLight.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The computational requirements of diffusion-based models can be demanding, which is a limitation that should be acknowledged. While the paper doesn't focus on efficiency, it's a practical consideration for real-world applications.
    *   **Potential Failure Cases:** Like most generative models, DreamLight may struggle with particularly complex or unusual scenes. Discussion on such limitations and potential future directions would strengthen the manuscript.

*   **Justification of the Score:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of image relighting. While it relies on existing techniques, the novel combination of these techniques and the introduction of PGLA and SFF lead to a significant improvement in the quality of relighting results, especially in the context of text-based background generation. The experimental results are convincing and demonstrate the model's superior performance and broad applicability. Therefore, a score of 7 is warranted. The approach isn't revolutionary but offers a valuable advance in practical image editing.

**Score: 7**

### Empirically-Calibrated H100 Node Power Models for Reducing Uncertainty in AI Training Energy Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14551v1)
- **Authors**: Alex C. Newkirk, Jared Fernandez, Jonathan Koomey, Imran Latif, Emma Strubell, Arman Shehabi, Constantine Samaras
- **Abstract**: As AI's energy demand continues to grow, it is critical to enhance the understanding of characteristics of this demand, to improve grid infrastructure planning and environmental assessment. By combining empirical measurements from Brookhaven National Laboratory during AI training on 8-GPU H100 systems with open-source benchmarking data, we develop statistical models relating computational intensity to node-level power consumption. We measure the gap between manufacturer-rated thermal design power (TDP) and actual power demand during AI training. Our analysis reveals that even computationally intensive workloads operate at only 76% of the 10.2 kW TDP rating. Our architecture-specific model, calibrated to floating-point operations, predicts energy consumption with 11.4% mean absolute percentage error, significantly outperforming TDP-based approaches (27-37% error). We identified distinct power signatures between transformer and CNN architectures, with transformers showing characteristic fluctuations that may impact grid stability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing concern about the energy consumption of AI training, especially with large language models.  It presents empirically-calibrated node-level power models to reduce uncertainty in estimating energy consumption during AI training.  The study combines empirical measurements from Brookhaven National Laboratory (BNL) using 8-GPU H100 systems with open-source benchmarking data to develop statistical models linking computational intensity to node power consumption. The analysis highlights that actual power demand during AI training is significantly below the manufacturer-rated Thermal Design Power (TDP), with even computationally intensive workloads operating at only 76% of the TDP. The paper develops an architecture-specific model, calibrated to floating-point operations (FLOPs), that predicts energy consumption with 11.4% Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE), outperforming TDP-based approaches. The research also identifies distinct power signatures for transformer and CNN architectures, noting characteristic fluctuations in transformer architectures that could impact grid stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its empirical approach to modeling AI training power demand using real-world measurements from H100 systems, coupled with open-source benchmarking data. It addresses the gap in accurate node-level power data, which has been a limitation in previous studies that often rely on manufacturer-rated TDP values.  The architecture-specific calibration using FLOPs is also a valuable contribution. However, aspects of this area have been explored previously, this paper addresses the gap with current state-of-the-art hardware.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for infrastructure planning, energy forecasting, and environmental assessments related to AI.  By demonstrating that actual power consumption is lower than TDP, the study challenges commonly held assumptions and provides a more accurate basis for grid capacity planning. Furthermore, the identification of distinct power signatures for different AI architectures contributes to a more nuanced understanding of AI workloads' impact on grid stability. These are practically useful findings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Foundation:** The use of empirical data from real AI training workloads on cutting-edge hardware is a major strength.
    *   **Improved Accuracy:** The developed statistical model significantly outperforms TDP-based approaches in predicting energy consumption.
    *   **Architecture-Specific Insights:** The identification of distinct power signatures contributes to a more nuanced understanding of AI workload characteristics.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have direct relevance for infrastructure planning, energy forecasting, and grid stability management.
    *   Availability of code and data promotes reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a specific hardware configuration (8-GPU H100 DGX) and a limited set of workloads. While valuable, the findings may not generalize to all AI training scenarios or future hardware generations. The SMC nodes have differences to the BNL node, although addressed through choice of statistical techniques there may be differences in observed behavior.
    *   **Model Simplifications:** The statistical models involve simplifications and assumptions (e.g., about component power scaling) that could impact accuracy.
    *   **Data Granularity:** The lack of component-level submetered power data limits the ability to precisely attribute power consumption to specific elements of the system.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research and industry practices by:

    *   Promoting the use of empirical data and FLOPs-based calibration in energy modeling.
    *   Encouraging further investigation of architecture-specific power signatures.
    *   Informing more accurate grid capacity planning and energy forecasting for AI infrastructure.
    *   Providing valuable data for optimizing AI workload scheduling and resource allocation.

*   **Areas for Improvement:**

    *   Expand the dataset to include a wider range of hardware configurations, AI architectures, and training workloads.
    *   Develop more sophisticated models that account for component-level power scaling and workload characteristics.
    *   Conduct long-term monitoring of AI training power demand to capture temporal variations and adapt to hardware advancements.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**
The paper makes a valuable contribution to the growing field of AI energy research. The use of empirical data from a modern AI hardware setup is key to creating a practical energy prediction model for AI workload. The model presented delivers improved accuracy relative to TDP-based predictions. The paper is not entirely novel as other related power models and estimation methods exist but this paper focuses on state-of-the-art hardware with good methodology. While the scope is limited and some simplifications are involved, the findings have significant practical implications for infrastructure planning and energy forecasting. The study's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, earning it a score of 7, reflecting its important, but not exceptional, contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Risk Estimation of Knee Osteoarthritis Progression via Predictive Multi-task Modelling from Efficient Diffusion Model using X-ray Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14560v1)
- **Authors**: David Butler, Adrian Hilton, Gustavo Carneiro
- **Abstract**: Medical imaging plays a crucial role in assessing knee osteoarthritis (OA) risk by enabling early detection and disease monitoring. Recent machine learning methods have improved risk estimation (i.e., predicting the likelihood of disease progression) and predictive modelling (i.e., the forecasting of future outcomes based on current data) using medical images, but clinical adoption remains limited due to their lack of interpretability. Existing approaches that generate future images for risk estimation are complex and impractical. Additionally, previous methods fail to localize anatomical knee landmarks, limiting interpretability. We address these gaps with a new interpretable machine learning method to estimate the risk of knee OA progression via multi-task predictive modelling that classifies future knee OA severity and predicts anatomical knee landmarks from efficiently generated high-quality future images. Such image generation is achieved by leveraging a diffusion model in a class-conditioned latent space to forecast disease progression, offering a visual representation of how particular health conditions may evolve. Applied to the Osteoarthritis Initiative dataset, our approach improves the state-of-the-art (SOTA) by 2\%, achieving an AUC of 0.71 in predicting knee OA progression while offering ~9% faster inference time.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a new interpretable machine learning method for estimating the risk of knee osteoarthritis (OA) progression.  It uses a multi-task predictive model that classifies future knee OA severity and predicts anatomical knee landmarks from efficiently generated high-quality future images. Image generation is achieved using a class-conditioned diffusion model in a latent space. The method aims to improve upon existing approaches by addressing their lack of interpretability and the complexity of generating future images. The experiments on the OAI dataset demonstrate improved state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance in risk estimation (AUC of 0.71) with faster inference time compared to previous methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Improved Interpretability:** A major strength of the paper is its focus on interpretability. By predicting anatomical landmarks along with future OA severity, the method provides clinicians with visual cues about which features contribute to the predicted progression. This addresses a crucial limitation of many existing "black box" machine learning models in medical imaging.
*   **Efficient Image Generation:** The use of a diffusion model in a class-conditioned latent space for image generation is a compelling approach. By working in a latent space, the method achieves efficient generation of high-quality future images, addressing another drawback of previous methods that relied on complex and impractical image generation pipelines. Faster inference is also a significant advantage for real-world applications.
*   **Performance Improvement:** The results on the OAI dataset demonstrate a 2% improvement in AUC over the previous SOTA method. While the improvement itself might seem modest, combined with the enhanced interpretability and efficiency, it presents a worthwhile advancement.
*   **Multi-task learning**: The use of multi-task learning for knee landmark localization and risk estimation, resulting in an improvement in performance, provides a strong approach to solving the problem.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** The performance increase over the existing SOTA might be considered incremental. A 2% AUC improvement, while statistically significant, may not dramatically alter clinical decision-making on its own.
*   **Dependency on Annotations:** The method relies on labeled data, especially class labels and landmark annotations. Although the paper suggests this dependency is not for image generation (just for risk estimation), in the real world, these annotations could be unavailable or inconsistent, limiting the method's generalizability.
*   **Limited Time Horizon:** The method predicts risk over a relatively short time horizon (12 months). While this is useful, longer-term forecasting would be more clinically relevant. The paper acknowledges this limitation and suggests iterative risk estimation for longer forecasts, but doesn't provide concrete results for this claim.
*   **Ablation Study Needs More Depth:** Some aspects of the ablation study are a bit unclear. For example, there needs to be a clearer justification for why image space data provides better performance when upscaling is not employed.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty of the paper lies in combining several aspects: (1) using a diffusion model in latent space for efficient future image generation in the context of OA risk prediction, (2) multi-task prediction of both KL class and anatomical landmarks for enhanced interpretability, and (3) achieving improved SOTA performance and faster inference within a more compact model.
It's significant because it directly addresses key challenges in applying machine learning to medical imaging. The focus on interpretability helps foster trust and acceptance among clinicians. The more efficient and high-quality image generation opens the door to more practical applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by improving interpretability and efficiency in OA risk estimation. However, the improvements over previous methods are relatively incremental, and there are some limitations in the design. However, the interpretability and efficiency aspects of the paper makes a strong argument for higher clinical utility.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV

### AlphaDecay:Module-wise Weight Decay for Heavy-Tailed Balancing in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14562v1)
- **Authors**: Di He, Ajay Jaiswal, Songjun Tu, Li Shen, Ganzhao Yuan, Shiwei Liu, Lu Yin
- **Abstract**: Weight decay is a standard regularization technique for training large language models (LLMs). While it is common to assign a uniform decay rate to every layer, this approach overlooks the structural diversity of LLMs and the varying spectral properties across modules. In this paper, we introduce AlphaDecay, a simple yet effective method that adaptively assigns different weight decay strengths to each module of an LLM. Our approach is guided by Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization (HT-SR) theory, which analyzes the empirical spectral density (ESD) of weight correlation matrices to quantify "heavy-tailedness." Modules exhibiting more pronounced heavy-tailed ESDs, reflecting stronger feature learning, are assigned weaker decay, while modules with lighter-tailed spectra receive stronger decay. Our method leverages tailored weight decay assignments to balance the module-wise differences in spectral properties, leading to improved performance. Extensive pre-training tasks with various model sizes from 60M to 1B demonstrate that AlphaDecay achieves better perplexity and generalization than conventional uniform decay and other adaptive decay baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces AlphaDecay, a novel weight decay scheduling method for training large language models (LLMs).  Instead of applying a uniform weight decay rate across all layers, AlphaDecay adaptively assigns decay strengths to individual modules (e.g., attention heads, MLP layers) based on their "heavy-tailedness," quantified using the empirical spectral density (ESD) of the module's weight correlation matrix. The method is grounded in Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization (HT-SR) theory, which suggests that modules with stronger feature learning (more heavy-tailed ESDs) should receive weaker decay, and vice versa. Experimental results on LLaMa models ranging from 60M to 1B parameters show that AlphaDecay improves perplexity and generalization compared to uniform weight decay and other adaptive decay methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of module-wise adaptive weight decay based on spectral properties is moderately novel. Previous work has explored adaptive weight decay (AWD) and differentiated weight decay for attention and MLP modules, but this paper is the first to tie the adaptation directly to a spectral property (heavy-tailedness) derived from HT-SR theory, and doing this on a module, rather than a layer, level.  While the individual components (HT-SR, adaptive weight decay) are not entirely new, their combination in the context of LLM training, specifically for module-wise regularization, represents a meaningful advance.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the training efficiency and performance of LLMs. The results show consistent gains across different model sizes and weight decay settings. This is crucial for reducing the computational resources needed to train large models. Furthermore, the paper provides insights into the role of weight decay as related to spectral properties, contributing to a deeper understanding of LLM optimization. While the performance improvements are not *enormous*, they are consistent and achieved with a relatively simple modification. The interpretability (linking decay to HT-SR) is also a valuable aspect.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The method is motivated by a solid theoretical framework (HT-SR).
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across multiple model sizes (60M to 1B parameters).
    *   **Consistent Improvements:**  AlphaDecay demonstrates consistent gains compared to baselines across various weight decay settings.
    *   **Interpretability:** The method provides a connection between spectral properties and optimal regularization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incrementality:** While combining existing ideas in a clever way, the core components (adaptive WD, HT-SR) aren't ground-breaking by themselves.
    *   **Modest Performance Gains:** The perplexity reductions are meaningful, but not transformative. A larger performance gap would have dramatically increased the paper's impact.
    *   **Limited Scope:**  The method is evaluated primarily on LLaMa architectures and the C4 dataset. Wider architectural and data set validation would strengthen the results, and experiments on fine-tuning would have been useful.
    *   **Dependence on ESD Calculation**: The method involves computing the ESD, which could be computationally expensive for very large models. The paper doesn't fully address the computational overhead or provide specific optimization techniques.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The paper's ideas could influence future research on LLM regularization techniques.  The module-wise adaptation approach and the link to spectral properties could inspire new methods for better LLM training. Practical adoption of AlphaDecay would depend on its computational cost and integration with existing training pipelines.

**Justification for Score**

Given the points above, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LLM training, however it is an incremental improvement, rather than a conceptual leap. The theoretical grounding in HT-SR is valuable, and the empirical evidence is persuasive, showing consistent benefits across different model sizes. However, the modest gains, the limited scope of evaluation (architecture and dataset) and computational dependence on ESD calculation temper the overall impact.

### Single-Example Learning in a Mixture of GPDMs with Latent Geometries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14563v1)
- **Authors**: Jesse St. Amand, Leonardo Gizzi, Martin A. Giese
- **Abstract**: We present the Gaussian process dynamical mixture model (GPDMM) and show its utility in single-example learning of human motion data. The Gaussian process dynamical model (GPDM) is a form of the Gaussian process latent variable model (GPLVM), but optimized with a hidden Markov model dynamical prior. The GPDMM combines multiple GPDMs in a probabilistic mixture-of-experts framework, utilizing embedded geometric features to allow for diverse sequences to be encoded in a single latent space, enabling the categorization and generation of each sequence class. GPDMs and our mixture model are particularly advantageous in addressing the challenges of modeling human movement in scenarios where data is limited and model interpretability is vital, such as in patient-specific medical applications like prosthesis control. We score the GPDMM on classification accuracy and generative ability in single-example learning, showcase model variations, and benchmark it against LSTMs, VAEs, and transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Gaussian Process Dynamical Mixture Model (GPDMM), a novel approach for single-example learning of human motion. It combines multiple Gaussian Process Dynamical Models (GPDMs) in a mixture-of-experts framework, leveraging embedded geometric features to encode diverse sequences in a single latent space. This allows for both categorization and generation of different motion classes from a very limited dataset (one example per class). The paper demonstrates the GPDMM's performance in classification and generative tasks, compares variations of the model architecture, and benchmarks it against LSTM, VAE, and transformer networks. The results show that GPDMM can achieve competitive performance with far fewer training examples, emphasizing its potential for applications where data scarcity is a problem.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the GPDMM architecture specifically designed for single-example motion learning. While GPDMs and mixture-of-experts models exist, their combination with embedded geometric features and optimization for minimal data scenarios is a significant departure from standard approaches. This design choice is important in addressing the challenges specific to limited datasets, which can often occur in patient-specific and personalized applications. The paper also investigates several initial condition strategies, which are novel in the context of single example learning with GPDMMs.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its applicability to domains where acquiring large datasets is difficult or impossible (e.g., patient-specific medical applications like prosthesis control). The ability to learn and generate motion from a single example is a powerful capability. While neural networks have dominated motion modeling, their reliance on extensive training data often limits their use in such scenarios. GPDMM offers a viable alternative with better data efficiency and greater interpretability (thanks to the latent space representation), which is very important for applications requiring reliability and verifiability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data Efficiency:** The core strength is the model's ability to learn from a single example per class, addressing a critical need in many real-world scenarios.
    *   **Interpretability:** The latent space representation offers interpretability, providing insight into the learned dynamics and enabling visualization of motion categories.
    *   **Strong benchmarks for evaluation:** The paper compares against LSTMs, VAEs, and Transformers with comparable hyperparameters, making its claims well grounded.
    *   **Explicit Focus on Hyperparameter Optimization:** The authors explicitly perform hyperparameter optimization across all competing architectures in a systematic way, increasing confidence in the results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The GPDMM can be relatively complex to implement and optimize, especially with the embedded geometric features. Choosing suitable geometric features may require domain expertise.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While promising, the evaluation is still limited to relatively small datasets and simple human movements. The generalizability to more complex, high-dimensional motion needs further investigation.
    *   **Specific Geometric Priors:** Despite attempting a fairly thorough range of initial condition and geometric strategies, the strong performance may be tied to the specific chosen priors and geometric features.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** GP models are often very sensitive to the choice of kernel and kernel hyperparameters; despite the work in hyperparameter optimization, this could pose a problem on larger datasets.
    *   **Lack of ablation or decomposition of the model and how the initial condition choices influence the results:** It isn't clear how the individual components of GPDMM (geometric features, GPs, mixture of experts) contribute to the overall performance and if some elements are redundant.

*   **Potential Impact:** The GPDMM has the potential to influence research in data-efficient motion modeling, especially in applications requiring personalized models. Its interpretability can make it appealing in domains where transparency is important. It could be particularly useful for assistive technologies, medical diagnostics, and robotics where acquiring large patient or task-specific datasets is difficult. The single-example learning setup can allow researchers to more easily use smaller datasets for testing and development.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    Given the novelty of the architecture combined with the emphasis on single-example learning, the significance for data-scarce application scenarios, and the strengths of experimental design and evaluation, the paper demonstrates an important contribution to the field. However, the weaknesses regarding the implementation complexity, limited scope of evaluation, and potentially sensitive parameter tuning prevent it from achieving a higher score.

### TGDPO: Harnessing Token-Level Reward Guidance for Enhancing Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14574v1)
- **Authors**: Mingkang Zhu, Xi Chen, Zhongdao Wang, Bei Yu, Hengshuang Zhao, Jiaya Jia
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reinforcement learning from human feedback have shown that utilizing fine-grained token-level reward models can substantially enhance the performance of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) in aligning large language models. However, it is challenging to leverage such token-level reward as guidance for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), since DPO is formulated as a sequence-level bandit problem. To address this challenge, this work decomposes the sequence-level PPO into a sequence of token-level proximal policy optimization problems and then frames the problem of token-level PPO with token-level reward guidance, from which closed-form optimal token-level policy and the corresponding token-level reward can be derived. Using the obtained reward and Bradley-Terry model, this work establishes a framework of computable loss functions with token-level reward guidance for DPO, and proposes a practical reward guidance based on the induced DPO reward. This formulation enables different tokens to exhibit varying degrees of deviation from reference policy based on their respective rewards. Experiment results demonstrate that our method achieves substantial performance improvements over DPO, with win rate gains of up to 7.5 points on MT-Bench, 6.2 points on AlpacaEval 2, and 4.3 points on Arena-Hard. Code is available at https://github.com/dvlab-research/TGDPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TGDPO, a novel method for enhancing Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) by incorporating token-level reward guidance. The core idea involves decomposing the sequence-level DPO problem into a series of token-level proximal policy optimization problems. This decomposition allows for the derivation of a closed-form optimal token-level policy and a corresponding token-level reward. By leveraging this reward and the Bradley-Terry model, the authors develop a new computable loss function that enables different tokens to deviate from the reference policy based on their rewards. Experiments on instruction following benchmarks demonstrate that TGDPO outperforms existing DPO methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in its decomposition of sequence-level DPO into a series of token-level PPO problems, enabling the integration of token-level rewards. While the use of token-level rewards in PPO is not entirely new, its application to DPO is a significant advancement. The derivation of the closed-form optimal token-level policy and the computable loss function are also novel contributions. The theoretical approach in Section 4 and the results in Theorem 4.1 and 4.3 appear to be the most innovative parts of this paper. The practical method for implementing TGDPO building on the induced DPO reward, contributes to the usability of this approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in RLHF, namely the sparse reward problem associated with sequence-level optimization. By introducing token-level guidance, TGDPO improves training stability and sample efficiency. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate that TGDPO outperforms existing DPO algorithms on multiple benchmarks (MT-Bench, AlpacaEval 2, Arena-Hard). Its stability in the face of loss convergence and robustness to varying token-level reward quality are practically valuable benefits.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a rigorous theoretical framework for incorporating token-level rewards into DPO.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of TGDPO across various models and benchmarks.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper introduces a practical method for implementing TGDPO and demonstrates its robustness and controllability.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and presents the key concepts clearly.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While the paper provides a clear explanation of the concepts, the mathematical derivations can be complex for readers unfamiliar with RLHF and DPO.
    *   **Dependency on Reward Model:** The method relies on a token-level reward model, which adds an extra step to the process and might introduce bias depending on the reward model's construction. The authors address robustness to reward variations but further exploration of the impact of reward model quality would be useful.
    *   **Limitations of the Approach:** While the approach enhances performance, understanding its limitations would be relevant. Are there scenarios where TGDPO underperforms existing DPO techniques or introduces undesirable outcomes, such as biases in text generation? Are there token level biases that arise from using this reward guidance?

*   **Potential Influence:** TGDPO has the potential to significantly influence the field of RLHF by providing a more efficient and effective approach to aligning large language models with human preferences. This could lead to the development of more powerful and aligned AI systems.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper builds upon existing work in RLHF and DPO, it introduces a novel and well-justified approach for integrating token-level rewards into DPO. The rigorous theoretical foundation and extensive empirical validation provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of TGDPO. The demonstration of robustness and controllability further enhance the practicality of the proposed method. While the reliance on a token-level reward model and a further deeper understanding of the limitations of this approach prevent it from being a truly transformative breakthrough, TGDPO represents a substantial advancement in the field.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GenerationPrograms: Fine-grained Attribution with Executable Programs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14580v1)
- **Authors**: David Wan, Eran Hirsch, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Ido Dagan, Mohit Bansal
- **Abstract**: Recent large language models (LLMs) achieve impressive performance in source-conditioned text generation but often fail to correctly provide fine-grained attributions for their outputs, undermining verifiability and trust. Moreover, existing attribution methods do not explain how and why models leverage the provided source documents to generate their final responses, limiting interpretability. To overcome these challenges, we introduce a modular generation framework, GenerationPrograms, inspired by recent advancements in executable "code agent" architectures. Unlike conventional generation methods that simultaneously generate outputs and attributions or rely on post-hoc attribution, GenerationPrograms decomposes the process into two distinct stages: first, creating an executable program plan composed of modular text operations (such as paraphrasing, compression, and fusion) explicitly tailored to the query, and second, executing these operations following the program's specified instructions to produce the final response. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that GenerationPrograms significantly improves attribution quality at both the document level and sentence level across two long-form question-answering tasks and a multi-document summarization task. We further demonstrate that GenerationPrograms can effectively function as a post-hoc attribution method, outperforming traditional techniques in recovering accurate attributions. In addition, the interpretable programs generated by GenerationPrograms enable localized refinement through modular-level improvements that further enhance overall attribution quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces GENERATIONPROGRAMS, a novel framework for source-conditioned text generation aimed at improving attribution quality. Unlike traditional methods that directly generate outputs and attributions or rely on post-hoc attribution, GENERATIONPROGRAMS decomposes the process into two distinct stages: (1) creating an executable program plan composed of modular text operations (paraphrasing, compression, fusion, extraction) tailored to the query, and (2) executing these operations according to the program's instructions to produce the final response. This modular approach allows for explicit tracking of source inputs at each step, thereby enhancing attribution. The authors demonstrate improved attribution quality across long-form question answering and multi-document summarization tasks, including its usability as a post-hoc attribution method and its utility in enabling localized refinement through modular-level improvements.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies primarily in its modular decomposition of the text generation process for improved attribution and interpretability. While the idea of using "code agent" architectures for complex tasks is gaining traction, its application to explicitly manage and enhance attribution in text generation is less common. The idea of the executable program being an explanation of the output is a particularly strong contribution. The use of explicit instructions within the programs enhances this interpretability and controllability. However, the individual modules themselves (paraphrasing, compression, etc.) are not entirely new, so the impact primarily comes from the overall architectural design.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from addressing a key challenge in the use of Large Language Models (LLMs): the difficulty in verifying their outputs and the lack of transparency in their reasoning process. By improving attribution, the framework promotes verifiability, faithfulness, and trustworthiness, which are essential for many real-world applications, especially in areas like deep research workflows. The performance gains in attribution quality are significant.  The framework's adaptability, as showcased in its use as a post-hoc attribution method, is also valuable.  Furthermore, the ability to perform localized refinements offers a pathway to iterative improvement and debugging of the generation process, which is a particularly novel and useful contribution.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Improved Attribution:** Clear and measurable improvements in attribution quality (F1 scores) compared to baseline methods.
    *   **Interpretability:** The generated programs provide an explicit explanation of the generation process, enabling users to understand how source documents are leveraged.
    *   **Modularity:** The framework's modular design facilitates localized refinement and targeted edits, enabling iterative improvement.
    *   **Flexibility:** The approach can function both for simultaneous generation/attribution and post-hoc attribution.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation across multiple tasks and metrics, including answer accuracy and human evaluation, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Trade-off in Answer Correctness:** The results indicate a potential trade-off between attribution quality and answer correctness, a factor that requires further investigation. While mitigated by summarization techniques, it warrants attention.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework relies heavily on LLMs for program generation and module execution. Its performance could be affected by the capabilities and limitations of the underlying LLMs.
    *   **Stylistic Divergence:** The paper identifies a "stylistic divergence" which it argues explains the degradation in correctness scores, which is a weakness of the study, not providing compelling results that GENERATIONPROGRAMS improves on baseline methods.
    *   **Limited Complexity of Tasks:** The type of tasks considered primarily requires synthesizing of information. It's unclear if the approach is suitable for tasks involving complex reasoning, multi-hop inference or logic.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in attributed generation, interpretable AI, and LLM applications. It provides a concrete and effective method for improving attribution, which can be adopted and extended by other researchers. The framework's modularity and interpretability can also inspire new approaches to designing and evaluating text generation systems.

*   **Score Justification:** The score reflects the paper's contribution in tackling a practically relevant challenge and introducing a novel method that shows significant improvements in attribution quality. However, the reduction in answer correctness, while addressed, limits its immediate applicability in all scenarios. The reliance on LLMs and the lack of support for tasks needing complex reasoning restrict its generality. Taking into account these factors, I assign the paper a score of:

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Busting the Paper Ballot: Voting Meets Adversarial Machine Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14582v1)
- **Authors**: Kaleel Mahmood, Caleb Manicke, Ethan Rathbun, Aayushi Verma, Sohaib Ahmad, Nicholas Stamatakis, Laurent Michel, Benjamin Fuller
- **Abstract**: We show the security risk associated with using machine learning classifiers in United States election tabulators. The central classification task in election tabulation is deciding whether a mark does or does not appear on a bubble associated to an alternative in a contest on the ballot. Barretto et al. (E-Vote-ID 2021) reported that convolutional neural networks are a viable option in this field, as they outperform simple feature-based classifiers. Our contributions to election security can be divided into four parts. To demonstrate and analyze the hypothetical vulnerability of machine learning models on election tabulators, we first introduce four new ballot datasets. Second, we train and test a variety of different models on our new datasets. These models include support vector machines, convolutional neural networks (a basic CNN, VGG and ResNet), and vision transformers (Twins and CaiT). Third, using our new datasets and trained models, we demonstrate that traditional white box attacks are ineffective in the voting domain due to gradient masking. Our analyses further reveal that gradient masking is a product of numerical instability. We use a modified difference of logits ratio loss to overcome this issue (Croce and Hein, ICML 2020). Fourth, in the physical world, we conduct attacks with the adversarial examples generated using our new methods. In traditional adversarial machine learning, a high (50% or greater) attack success rate is ideal. However, for certain elections, even a 5% attack success rate can flip the outcome of a race. We show such an impact is possible in the physical domain. We thoroughly discuss attack realism, and the challenges and practicality associated with printing and scanning ballot adversarial examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the vulnerability of machine learning classifiers used in United States election tabulators to adversarial attacks.  The authors focus on the core classification task of determining whether a bubble on a ballot is marked or blank. They introduce four new labeled ballot datasets and train various models (SVMs, CNNs, Transformers). The paper demonstrates that standard white-box attacks fail due to gradient masking, which they attribute to numerical instability. They overcome this issue using a modified difference of logits ratio (DLR) loss. Finally, they show that adversarial examples generated using their methods can impact election outcomes in the physical world, even with relatively low attack success rates, given the typically narrow margins in elections.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:
    *   Applying adversarial machine learning techniques specifically to the domain of ballot mark recognition is relatively new.  While others have explored ML in voting systems, this is a focused adversarial attack analysis.
    *   The introduction of new, publicly available ballot datasets fills a gap in the research community.  The lack of standard datasets has hindered reproducibility and progress in this area.
    *   The detailed analysis of gradient masking *not* due to a defensive mechanism, but rather due to numerical instability within standard PyTorch training, is a significant contribution. They pinpoint specific causes and suggest a solution with modified DLR.
    *   The exploration of physical attacks, accounting for the realities of printing and scanning, adds practical relevance.
    *   The analysis of how small adversarial attack success rates can have a large impact on elections with close margins is a crucial observation, differing from the typical ML focus on high overall accuracy.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the following:
    *   It raises awareness of a potential security vulnerability in a critical part of the election process, especially as tabulators increasingly rely on ML.
    *   It provides a concrete attack strategy that could be used to manipulate election outcomes, highlighting the importance of robust design and testing of tabulators.
    *   The detailed analysis of gradient masking provides valuable insights for researchers working on adversarial robustness in other domains.
    *   The consideration of physical world constraints is vital, as many adversarial ML studies focus solely on the digital domain.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains complex concepts.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, covering a variety of models, attacks, and datasets.
    *   The identification and explanation of gradient masking are thorough and convincing.
    *   The physical world experiments add practical relevance.
    *   The discussion of attack realism and limitations is important.
    *   The released datasets and code promote reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper identifies a potential vulnerability, it doesn't demonstrate successful attacks on *actual* tabulator systems. The findings are based on experiments using commonly available models. However, real systems are proprietary.
    *   The reliance on specific printing and scanning hardware might limit the generalizability of the physical attack results.
    *   The attacker model assumes a compromised vendor, while perhaps plausible, might not be the *most* likely threat vector.  Insider attacks within municipalities may be equally or more concerning.
    *   The paper states that there is not obvious data linked to specific elections/ballots but this does not remove the sensitivity of working with/reproducing ballot datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could significantly influence the design and testing of election tabulators. It might prompt vendors to adopt more robust ML techniques, implement defenses against adversarial attacks, or move away from using ML in critical classification tasks. It is likely to spur further research into adversarial robustness in voting systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to election security and adversarial machine learning. It identifies a potentially serious vulnerability and demonstrates a practical attack strategy. The paper also provides insights into gradient masking and the challenges of physical world adversarial attacks. The open-sourcing of datasets and code and general research contribution strengthens its overall value.

However, some limitations include the lack of experiments on real tabulators, dependence on specific hardware, and a focus on a specific attack vector.

Therefore, based on the above points, a reasonable score would be an 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### NetRoller: Interfacing General and Specialized Models for End-to-End Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14589v1)
- **Authors**: Ren Xin, Hongji Liu, Xiaodong Mei, Wenru Liu, Maosheng Ye, Zhili Chen, Jun Ma
- **Abstract**: Integrating General Models (GMs) such as Large Language Models (LLMs), with Specialized Models (SMs) in autonomous driving tasks presents a promising approach to mitigating challenges in data diversity and model capacity of existing specialized driving models. However, this integration leads to problems of asynchronous systems, which arise from the distinct characteristics inherent in GMs and SMs. To tackle this challenge, we propose NetRoller, an adapter that incorporates a set of novel mechanisms to facilitate the seamless integration of GMs and specialized driving models. Specifically, our mechanisms for interfacing the asynchronous GMs and SMs are organized into three key stages. NetRoller first harvests semantically rich and computationally efficient representations from the reasoning processes of LLMs using an early stopping mechanism, which preserves critical insights on driving context while maintaining low overhead. It then applies learnable query embeddings, nonsensical embeddings, and positional layer embeddings to facilitate robust and efficient cross-modality translation. At last, it employs computationally efficient Query Shift and Feature Shift mechanisms to enhance the performance of SMs through few-epoch fine-tuning. Based on the mechanisms formalized in these three stages, NetRoller enables specialized driving models to operate at their native frequencies while maintaining situational awareness of the GM. Experiments conducted on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate that integrating GM through NetRoller significantly improves human similarity and safety in planning tasks, and it also achieves noticeable precision improvements in detection and mapping tasks for end-to-end autonomous driving. The code and models are available at https://github.com/Rex-sys-hk/NetRoller .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "NetRoller: Interfacing General and Specialized Models for End-to-End Autonomous Driving."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of integrating large language models (LLMs) or general models (GMs) with specialized driving models (SMs) in end-to-end autonomous driving (E2E-AD) systems. It proposes NetRoller, an adapter with three key mechanisms to facilitate seamless, asynchronous integration. These mechanisms are: 1) **Information Collection:** Harvesting semantically rich and computationally efficient representations from LLMs using an early stopping mechanism. 2) **Information Translation:**  Employing learnable query embeddings, nonsensical embeddings, and positional layer embeddings to enable robust and efficient cross-modality translation between the GM's output and the SM's input. 3) **Information Distribution:** Utilizing Query Shift and Feature Shift mechanisms to enhance the performance of SMs through few-epoch fine-tuning. Experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate improvements in human similarity, safety in planning tasks, and precision in detection and mapping.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the proposed NetRoller architecture, especially its three-stage approach for asynchronous GM-SM integration. While the use of LLMs in autonomous driving is not entirely new, the specific mechanisms implemented by NetRoller, like the early stopping-based information collection and the Query/Feature Shift distribution, appear to be novel contributions. The use of "nonsense embedding" as a technique to avoid NaN issues is interesting and potentially broadly applicable. The early stop/first token approach to minimize LLM latency represents a worthwhile contribution.

*   **Significance:** The integration of LLMs with SMs represents a promising direction for addressing limitations in data diversity and model capacity of existing specialized driving models. By enabling SMs to leverage the reasoning capabilities of GMs, the proposed approach has the potential to enhance the safety, robustness, and human-like behavior of autonomous vehicles. Improvements in planning, detection, and mapping, as demonstrated in the experiments, highlight the practical significance of this work. The opensourcing of code and models is a positive step towards reproducibility and adoption.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture with three distinct stages.
    *   Novel mechanisms for information collection, translation, and distribution.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on a standard dataset (nuScenes).
    *   Demonstrated improvements in key performance metrics (safety, similarity, precision).
    *   Open-sourced code and models.
    *   The ablation study does a reasonable job of dissecting the contribution of various components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The presentation could be improved. Some of the mathematical notations and explanations of the different modules are not as clear as they could be.
    *   The paper acknowledges using LLaMA 2, which is outperformed by newer models; therefore, gains might be even greater with better models. Further exploration with more robust closed-source or larger LLMs may be needed.
    *   The experiments could be more extensive, especially regarding generalization to unseen scenarios. The paper only experiments on the nuScenes dataset, it would be beneficial to test on other datasets or even real-world scenarios. A deeper dive into the qualitative results (e.g., examples of scenarios where NetRoller excels) would be insightful.
    *   While the early-stopping mechanism is a valuable contribution, the paper could explore adaptive or dynamic early-stopping strategies, rather than relying on a fixed number of tokens.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper introduces a practical approach for integrating LLMs into E2E-AD systems. The proposed NetRoller adapter can serve as a building block for future research in this area. The open-sourced code and models should facilitate further investigation and development by other researchers.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and technically sound contribution to the field of autonomous driving by addressing the important challenge of GM-SM integration.  The core idea of NetRoller and its three-stage architecture are novel and promising.  The experimental results on nuScenes demonstrate practical improvements in key performance metrics. However, the paper is not groundbreaking in the sense of completely revolutionizing the field. There are limitations in experimental validation and the use of older LLMs that somewhat temper the overall impact. More exploration with modern, larger LLMs could potentially improve performance even further. Nonetheless, the paper provides a solid foundation for future research and development in the integration of general intelligence into autonomous driving systems, making it a significant, but not exceptional, contribution warranting a score of 7. The novelty and solid experimental results support the score, but room for improving the scope and experiments holds it back from a higher rating.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Align Your Flow: Scaling Continuous-Time Flow Map Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14603v1)
- **Authors**: Amirmojtaba Sabour, Sanja Fidler, Karsten Kreis
- **Abstract**: Diffusion- and flow-based models have emerged as state-of-the-art generative modeling approaches, but they require many sampling steps. Consistency models can distill these models into efficient one-step generators; however, unlike flow- and diffusion-based methods, their performance inevitably degrades when increasing the number of steps, which we show both analytically and empirically. Flow maps generalize these approaches by connecting any two noise levels in a single step and remain effective across all step counts. In this paper, we introduce two new continuous-time objectives for training flow maps, along with additional novel training techniques, generalizing existing consistency and flow matching objectives. We further demonstrate that autoguidance can improve performance, using a low-quality model for guidance during distillation, and an additional boost can be achieved by adversarial finetuning, with minimal loss in sample diversity. We extensively validate our flow map models, called Align Your Flow, on challenging image generation benchmarks and achieve state-of-the-art few-step generation performance on both ImageNet 64x64 and 512x512, using small and efficient neural networks. Finally, we show text-to-image flow map models that outperform all existing non-adversarially trained few-step samplers in text-conditioned synthesis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Align Your Flow: Scaling Continuous-Time Flow Map Distillation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Align Your Flow" (AYF), a novel distillation method for training flow maps. Flow maps generalize diffusion and consistency models, allowing for efficient generation with any number of steps. AYF contributes two new continuous-time objectives and stabilization techniques, enabling the distillation of autoguided teacher models for improved performance.  The authors demonstrate that standard consistency models inherently suffer from error accumulation during multi-step sampling, a limitation that AYF overcomes.  AYF achieves state-of-the-art few-step generation performance on ImageNet benchmarks and scales to high-resolution text-to-image generation, outperforming existing non-adversarial approaches. The method also employs adversarial fine-tuning for added sharpness with minimal impact on sample diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers significant novelty in several aspects:
    *   **Theoretical Contribution:** The analytical demonstration of the limitations of consistency models in multi-step sampling is a solid theoretical contribution that motivates the flow map approach. It provides a formal understanding of an observed empirical problem.
    *   **Methodological Advancements:** The introduction of two new continuous-time objectives (AYF-EMD and AYF-LMD) for flow map training, along with stabilization techniques, expands the toolkit for generative modeling. The generalization of existing consistency and flow matching objectives within the EMD framework is valuable.
    *   **Autoguidance Distillation:** The application of autoguidance to teacher model distillation is a novel way to improve performance, offering an alternative to traditional classifier-free guidance and adversarial training.
    *   **Focus on Flow maps:** While flow maps themselves are not entirely new, this paper showcases their practical superiority at scale vs distilled diffusion and consistency models in the efficient regime.

*   **Significance:**  The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of generative modeling.

    *   **Improved Efficiency:** AYF addresses a critical bottleneck in generative models: slow sampling. By achieving state-of-the-art performance in few-step generation, AYF makes generative models more practical for real-time applications and reduces computational costs.
    *   **Broader Applicability:** The ability to train effective flow maps without relying on adversarial training offers a more stable and controllable approach, potentially leading to broader adoption. The scalability of AYF to high-resolution text-to-image synthesis is also significant.
    *   **Overcoming Limitations of CMs:** The analytical and empirical demonstration of the shortcomings of multi-step consistency models is an important contribution, guiding future research directions towards more robust alternatives like flow maps.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains complex concepts.
    *   The theoretical analysis is rigorous and provides valuable insights.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering a range of benchmarks and ablation studies.
    *   The results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing methods, particularly in the few-step generation setting.
    *   Open-source implementation is expected to boost adoption and impact.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While autoguidance is effective, it introduces an additional component that might add complexity to the training process and may not be suitable for all scenarios (e.g., FLUX.1 requires distillation rather than having its own autoguiding system).
    *   The paper acknowledges that AYF, in some configurations, leads to slightly degraded single-step performance compared to methods explicitly designed for one-step generation. While adversarial finetuning mitigates this, it would be desirable to achieve optimal performance across all step counts without needing to add a GAN objective.
    *   The performance of LMD objective on image datasets can be improved.

*   **Justification of Score:**
I am assigning a score of **8.5** to this paper.

*   The **theoretical contribution** about *Consistency Model's limitations* is novel and valuable. The new **method** "Align Your Flow" achieves state-of-the-art or near state-of-the-art performance in few-step generative modeling. It **scales** well to large datasets. The code is or will be publicly available, **increasing impact**. The **limitations** section provides open points to guide follow-up works.

The paper represents a significant advancement in generative modeling, addressing a critical challenge (sampling efficiency) with a theoretically sound and empirically validated approach.
Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Guaranteed Guess: A Language Modeling Approach for CISC-to-RISC Transpilation with Testing Guarantees
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14606v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Heakl, Sarim Hashmi, Chaimaa Abi, Celine Lee, Abdulrahman Mahmoud
- **Abstract**: The hardware ecosystem is rapidly evolving, with increasing interest in translating low-level programs across different instruction set architectures (ISAs) in a quick, flexible, and correct way to enhance the portability and longevity of existing code. A particularly challenging class of this transpilation problem is translating between complex- (CISC) and reduced- (RISC) hardware architectures, due to fundamental differences in instruction complexity, memory models, and execution paradigms. In this work, we introduce GG (Guaranteed Guess), an ISA-centric transpilation pipeline that combines the translation power of pre-trained large language models (LLMs) with the rigor of established software testing constructs. Our method generates candidate translations using an LLM from one ISA to another, and embeds such translations within a software-testing framework to build quantifiable confidence in the translation. We evaluate our GG approach over two diverse datasets, enforce high code coverage (>98%) across unit tests, and achieve functional/semantic correctness of 99% on HumanEval programs and 49% on BringupBench programs, respectively. Further, we compare our approach to the state-of-the-art Rosetta 2 framework on Apple Silicon, showcasing 1.73x faster runtime performance, 1.47x better energy efficiency, and 2.41x better memory usage for our transpiled code, demonstrating the effectiveness of GG for real-world CISC-to-RISC translation tasks. We will open-source our codes, data, models, and benchmarks to establish a common foundation for ISA-level code translation research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Guaranteed Guess" (GG), a novel approach to CISC-to-RISC assembly transpilation using a custom-trained language model (LLM) integrated with a software testing framework.  GG aims to translate x86 assembly to ARM or RISC-V assembly while ensuring functional correctness through unit tests. The system leverages hardware-aware training, tokenizer extensions, and ROPE extrapolation to improve the LLM's ability to generate semantically valid RISC code.  The authors evaluate GG on HumanEval and BringUpBench, demonstrating high functional correctness and showing GG's generated ARM code outperforms Rosetta 2 in terms of runtime, energy efficiency, and memory usage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The combination of LLMs and rigorous software testing for assembly transpilation is a relatively novel concept, especially for bridging the gap between CISC and RISC architectures. The emphasis on "guaranteed" correctness through testing addresses a critical weakness of purely LLM-based code generation.
*   **Hardware-Aware Design:**  The paper emphasizes the importance of incorporating hardware-specific knowledge into the training process. The tokenizer extension is well-motivated and contributes to improved token alignment with instruction semantics.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The HumanEval results demonstrate a significant performance improvement compared to other LLMs, highlighting the effectiveness of GG's architecture-aware training. The comparison with Rosetta 2 provides practical evidence of the potential of LLM-based transpilation to surpass runtime virtualization.
*   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper includes a thorough error analysis, shedding light on the challenges of compiler optimizations and identifying areas for future improvement. The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of individual components.
*   **Open-Source Availability:** The authors' commitment to open-sourcing their code, data, models, and benchmarks is crucial for promoting further research in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** The primary baseline is a comparison against other LLMs that weren't specifically trained for assembly translation tasks. A more direct comparison against existing symbolic translation or binary rewriting techniques would strengthen the evaluation. The Rosetta comparison is compelling, but it addresses a different use case (runtime emulation vs. ahead-of-time transpilation). A better benchmark comparison of relevant translation baselines.
*   **Context Window Limitations:** The error analysis reveals that context truncation is a significant source of failures. While ROPE extrapolation is used, a more sophisticated mechanism for handling long-range dependencies might be required to address this issue more effectively.
*   **Optimization Fragility:** The model's accuracy drops significantly under aggressive compiler optimizations (-O2). This indicates a lack of robustness to code transformations that abstract data and control flow, which are common in real-world binaries. The method should be more robust to the variance in compiler optimization levels to be practically useful.
*   **BringUpBench Performance:** The performance on BringUpBench is significantly lower than on HumanEval. The performance isn't high enough to be practically useful.
*   **LLM scale: LLM is limited to smaller models (1.5b) which could limit the applicability to other architectures and ISAs.**

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a novel approach to assembly transpilation by integrating LLMs with software testing. While LLMs have been explored for code generation tasks, their application to low-level assembly translation with a focus on correctness guarantees is relatively unexplored. The detailed error analysis and architecture-aware design contribute valuable insights into the challenges and opportunities of LLM-based transpilation. The practical demonstration of outperforming Rosetta 2 in a real-world scenario is a significant result.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a technically sound and well-evaluated approach to a challenging problem. The combination of LLMs and testing is a valuable contribution, and the empirical results are promising. The architecture-aware design and detailed analysis demonstrate a clear understanding of the domain. However, the limitations in baseline comparison, context window limitations, optimization fragility, BringUpBench accuracy, and LLM scale prevent it from receiving a higher score.
