### Cost-Aware Routing for Efficient Text-To-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14753v1)
- **Authors**: Qinchan, Li, Kenneth Chen, Changyue, Su, Wittawat Jitkrittum, Qi Sun, Patsorn Sangkloy
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models are well known for their ability to generate a high-fidelity image for an input prompt through an iterative denoising process. Unfortunately, the high fidelity also comes at a high computational cost due the inherently sequential generative process. In this work, we seek to optimally balance quality and computational cost, and propose a framework to allow the amount of computation to vary for each prompt, depending on its complexity. Each prompt is automatically routed to the most appropriate text-to-image generation function, which may correspond to a distinct number of denoising steps of a diffusion model, or a disparate, independent text-to-image model. Unlike uniform cost reduction techniques (e.g., distillation, model quantization), our approach achieves the optimal trade-off by learning to reserve expensive choices (e.g., 100+ denoising steps) only for a few complex prompts, and employ more economical choices (e.g., small distilled model) for less sophisticated prompts. We empirically demonstrate on COCO and DiffusionDB that by learning to route to nine already-trained text-to-image models, our approach is able to deliver an average quality that is higher than that achievable by any of these models alone.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CATImage, a framework for cost-aware routing in text-to-image generation. The core idea is to adaptively allocate computational resources (e.g., the number of denoising steps in a diffusion model or selecting between different generative models) based on the complexity of the input prompt. The framework aims to optimize the trade-off between image quality and computational cost by routing each prompt to the most appropriate text-to-image generation function. The paper formulates the problem as a constrained optimization, derives a theoretically optimal routing rule, and proposes practical estimators that can be trained from data. Experiments on COCO and DiffusionDB datasets demonstrate that CATImage can achieve comparable or higher image quality than individual models while significantly reducing the overall computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in text-to-image generation: the high computational cost associated with generating high-fidelity images. The need for efficient and sustainable generative models is well-motivated, particularly for on-device applications and reducing environmental impact.
*   **Novelty:** The adaptive routing approach, which tailors computational effort to prompt complexity, is a valuable contribution. It moves beyond uniform optimization techniques and offers a more fine-grained control over the quality-cost trade-off. The combination of homogeneous (varying denoising steps) and heterogeneous (different models) routing options provides flexibility.
*   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a solid theoretical foundation by formulating the routing problem as a constrained optimization and deriving the optimal routing rule.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The experimental results on COCO and DiffusionDB datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework. The analysis of model selection rates and comparisons with existing baselines (including DeepCache, different distilled model variants) provide compelling evidence of the benefits of adaptive routing. Demonstrating the same or improved performance at a fraction of the computational cost is a strong argument.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** Thorough evaluation of the methods with different metrics such as CLIPScore, ImageReward, Aesthetic Score, and Sharpness to demonstrate that the methods aren't just good with a single metric.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on Quality Estimator:** The performance of CATImage heavily relies on the accuracy of the quality estimator. The quality estimator can be limited by the information it is trained upon (for example, if it is trained on CLIP embeddings, it might not be able to accurately capture all factors that humans find aesthetically pleasing).
*   **Implementation Complexity:** While the framework is conceptually clear, implementing and training the components (routing function and quality estimator) could add significant complexity to an existing text-to-image generation pipeline. This might limit its widespread adoption. While the approach is generic, its effectiveness may also vary depending on the specific choices of the base models and the quality metric used.
*   **Limited Exploration of Uncertainty:** The paper acknowledges that the method doesn't explicitly account for uncertainty arising from different noise variations during the generation process. Addressing this could lead to more robust and reliable routing decisions.
*   **Specifics of Implementation:** While the appendixes contain further descriptions of the implementation details, more can be provided in describing the trade offs with different hyperparameters or architectural choices for the components in the proposed system. For example, it would be useful to describe the trade-offs in using Transformer and KNN in the cost to performance ratio.
*   **Limited Model Breadth**: The range of models presented in the paper are largely focused on diffusion models. Further breadth would likely be required to adequately prove the capabilities of the proposed algorithm to be adapted to different forms of models.

**Significance and Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of text-to-image generation by promoting more efficient and sustainable models. The adaptive routing approach could be adopted by researchers and practitioners to optimize the quality-cost trade-off in their generative models. The framework's flexibility allows it to be adapted to different model architectures and evaluation metrics. The insights gained from this work could also inform the development of more energy-efficient and resource-aware AI systems in other domains. The framework can provide improvements in cost and efficiency for different tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the significance of the problem addressed, the novelty of the adaptive routing approach, the solid theoretical foundation, and the compelling experimental validation, the paper represents a strong contribution to the field. While the dependence on the quality estimator and the potential implementation complexity are valid concerns, the benefits of CATImage outweigh these limitations. The paper makes a valuable step towards more sustainable and resource-efficient text-to-image generation.

### Scaling-Up the Pretraining of the Earth Observation Foundation Model PhilEO to the MajorTOM Dataset
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14765v1)
- **Authors**: Nikolaos Dionelis, Jente Bosmans, Riccardo Musto, Giancarlo Paoletti, Simone Sarti, Giacomo Cascarano, Casper Fibaek, Luke Camilleri, Bertrand Le Saux, Nicolas Longépé
- **Abstract**: Today, Earth Observation (EO) satellites generate massive volumes of data, with the Copernicus Sentinel-2 constellation alone producing approximately 1.6TB per day. To fully exploit this information, it is essential to pretrain EO Foundation Models (FMs) on large unlabeled datasets, enabling efficient fine-tuning for several different downstream tasks with minimal labeled data. In this work, we present the scaling-up of our recently proposed EO Foundation Model, PhilEO Geo-Aware U-Net, on the unlabeled 23TB dataset MajorTOM, which covers the vast majority of the Earth's surface, as well as on the specialized subset FastTOM 2TB that does not include oceans and ice. We develop and study various PhilEO model variants with different numbers of parameters and architectures. Finally, we fine-tune the models on the PhilEO Bench for road density estimation, building density pixel-wise regression, and land cover semantic segmentation, and we evaluate the performance. Our results demonstrate that for all n-shots for road density regression, the PhilEO 44M MajorTOM 23TB model outperforms PhilEO Globe 0.5TB 44M. We also show that for most n-shots for road density estimation and building density regression, PhilEO 200M FastTOM outperforms all the other models. The effectiveness of both dataset and model scaling is validated using the PhilEO Bench. We also study the impact of architecture scaling, transitioning from U-Net Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) to Vision Transformers (ViT).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates scaling up the pretraining of the PhilEO Earth Observation Foundation Model (GFM). The authors expand the pretraining dataset from the PhilEO Globe (0.5TB) to the full MajorTOM dataset (23TB), including a smaller subset FastTOM (2TB), that contains only land regions without oceans and ice. They also explore scaling the model's architecture by transitioning from CNN-based U-Nets to Vision Transformer (ViT) architectures combined with UPerNet decoders.  The models are evaluated on the PhilEO Bench for road density estimation, building density regression, and land cover mapping, and the effectiveness of data and model scaling is validated. Results show that larger pretraining datasets generally improve performance, especially for road density regression. They also show that ViT-UPerNet architectures can match or exceed the performance of CNN-based U-Nets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the scale of the pretraining dataset used (23TB MajorTOM), which, to the authors' knowledge, is the first instance of such a large dataset being used to train a GFM.  The exploration of ViT architectures with UPerNet decoders in the PhilEO Bench setting is also valuable. However, the individual components – ViTs, UPerNet, masked reconstruction – are not novel in isolation, but the application and comprehensive evaluation on EO data are. Also the work comparing different models, datasets and architectures within the PhilEO Bench framework makes it unique.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is evident in the observed improvements in downstream task performance with larger pretraining datasets. This reinforces the importance of scaling up pretraining efforts for EO tasks, potentially enabling more accurate and efficient applications for environmental monitoring, urban planning, and disaster response. The study also provides valuable insights into the effectiveness of different model architectures, guiding future research in GFM design for EO. The inclusion of an analysis for a specialized smaller dataset is also significant to provide practical solutions for researchers with limited computing resources.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Large-scale experimentation:**  The use of a 23TB dataset and HPC resources provides a significant contribution, enabling robust evaluation of scaling effects.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The PhilEO Bench provides a standardized framework for comparing different GFMs across various downstream tasks.
    *   **Architectural exploration:** Investigating the transition from CNN-based U-Nets to ViT-based architectures is crucial for understanding the potential of transformers in EO.
    *   **Clear results:** The results are presented clearly with comparisons of different models, datasets, and architectures.
    *   **Practical results:** The benefits of land specialization of datasets are shown to be beneficial for specific tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental improvements:** The improvements, while present, are not always dramatic.  The impact of MajorTOM pretraining on land cover mapping wasn't as pronounced, for example.
    *   **Limited architectural diversity:** The explored architectures are primarily variations of U-Nets and ViTs. Exploring other architectures could be beneficial.
    *   **Land cover mapping analysis**: The land cover analysis is mostly centered around discussing the land specialisation of datasets.

*   **Impact:** This paper validates the benefits of scaling up pretraining for EO, both in terms of data volume and potentially model capacity (though further exploration of model size scaling is warranted). This contribution can influence future research directions towards developing larger and more robust GFMs for EO applications.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field. The results provide useful insights for the community, particularly as efforts to scale the pretraining of GFMs on EO data accelerate. However, the novelty is somewhat limited by the use of existing architectural building blocks (ViTs, UPerNet) and the incremental nature of the improvements in certain tasks.

### A Variational Framework for Improving Naturalness in Generative Spoken Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14767v1)
- **Authors**: Li-Wei Chen, Takuya Higuchi, Zakaria Aldeneh, Ahmed Hussen Abdelaziz, Alexander Rudnicky
- **Abstract**: The success of large language models in text processing has inspired their adaptation to speech modeling. However, since speech is continuous and complex, it is often discretized for autoregressive modeling. Speech tokens derived from self-supervised models (known as semantic tokens) typically focus on the linguistic aspects of speech but neglect prosodic information. As a result, models trained on these tokens can generate speech with reduced naturalness. Existing approaches try to fix this by adding pitch features to the semantic tokens. However, pitch alone cannot fully represent the range of paralinguistic attributes, and selecting the right features requires careful hand-engineering. To overcome this, we propose an end-to-end variational approach that automatically learns to encode these continuous speech attributes to enhance the semantic tokens. Our approach eliminates the need for manual extraction and selection of paralinguistic features. Moreover, it produces preferred speech continuations according to human raters. Code, samples and models are available at https://github.com/b04901014/vae-gslm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper proposes a novel approach to improving the naturalness of generative spoken language models (GSLMs).  The key idea is to augment semantic tokens (derived from self-supervised models like HuBERT) with continuous variational features learned directly from the input speech signal using a variational autoencoder (VAE). This avoids the need for manual extraction and selection of paralinguistic features like pitch, loudness, or spectral coefficients, which are often neglected in standard semantic token-based GSLMs.  The VAE is trained jointly with an autoregressive model predicting the semantic tokens and the learned variational features. The framework is evaluated on speech reconstruction and continuation tasks, demonstrating improved naturalness in generated speech compared to baselines that use semantic tokens alone or with hand-engineered pitch features. The models perform comparably to a baseline with acoustic tokens, without harming linguistic meaningfulness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of using a VAE to learn continuous paralinguistic features alongside discrete semantic tokens in a GSLM is fairly novel. While VAEs with autoregressive priors have been used in other domains (vision, TTS), applying this specifically to GSLMs with discrete tokens extracted from pre-trained speech models and jointly optimizing everything end-to-end has not been extensively explored. This allows to learn feature representations useful for both naturalness and predictive capabilities of the LM, therefore providing a meaningful contribution.
* **Significance:** The problem of generating natural-sounding speech is crucial for improving human-computer interaction. The paper addresses a key limitation of semantic token-based GSLMs: their neglect of paralinguistic information leading to unnatural speech. The proposed method offers a more automated and flexible way to incorporate such information compared to manual feature engineering. The finding that learned variational features can improve naturalness without significantly sacrificing linguistic meaningfulness is important. The results are good and shows promise, however scaling the results with other, more complex setups must be proven to improve overall impact.
* **Strengths:**
    * **End-to-end learning:** The approach eliminates the need for manual feature extraction and selection, making it more automated and potentially more adaptable to different datasets.
    * **Joint optimization:** The variational features are trained to both reconstruct speech and facilitate autoregressive modeling.
    * **Empirical results:** The subjective evaluation demonstrates improved naturalness compared to strong baselines. The objective metrics, while not always surpassing all baselines, support the claim that the approach does not severely degrade reconstruction quality or linguistic meaningfulness.
    * **Well-written and detailed:** The paper clearly explains the proposed method, experimental setup, and results.  It provides ablation studies and analyzes the impact of different hyperparameters.
    * **The code is made available:** provides a direct way to use the proposed method, which encourages other researchers to use the proposed ideas on different tasks.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental improvement:** While the naturalness improvement is significant, the objective metrics for reconstruction are not always better than all baselines, especially the method using acoustic tokens. This raises the question of whether the learned variational features fully capture all necessary information for accurate reconstruction.
    * **Dataset limitations:** The experiments are primarily conducted on audiobook data (LibriSpeech/Libri-light). The generalization to more diverse and spontaneous speech styles (e.g., conversational speech) needs to be investigated.
    * **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** The paper acknowledges that the performance is sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters (β and γ). More investigation is needed to develop robust strategies for hyperparameter tuning.
    * **Lack of comparison to more advanced approaches:** In the related work section, the paper mentions work about fine-tuning LLMs in speech. There is no evidence of these techniques used in this paper and, therefore the significance of the results can be debated.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future GSLMs by promoting the use of learned continuous representations for capturing paralinguistic information. It could also be integrated with text-based LLMs for improved speech synthesis or dialogue systems. However, more robust evaluation across diverse datasets and tasks is necessary to fully realize its impact. Also, the impact of the results could have better impact if the paper could prove that combining LLMs and the proposed technique improves overall results.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to improving the naturalness of GSLMs. The experimental results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method. However, there are several limitations, including incremental improvement in objective metrics, dataset limitations, and hyperparameter sensitivity, that need to be addressed. The impact and novelty do not rank as high as a potential score of 8 or 9. These limitations, although existing, do not detract from the importance of the novel contributions of this paper.

### CDP: Towards Robust Autoregressive Visuomotor Policy Learning via Causal Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14769v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahua Ma, Yiran Qin, Yixiong Li, Xuanqi Liao, Yulan Guo, Ruimao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Policy (DP) enables robots to learn complex behaviors by imitating expert demonstrations through action diffusion. However, in practical applications, hardware limitations often degrade data quality, while real-time constraints restrict model inference to instantaneous state and scene observations. These limitations seriously reduce the efficacy of learning from expert demonstrations, resulting in failures in object localization, grasp planning, and long-horizon task execution. To address these challenges, we propose Causal Diffusion Policy (CDP), a novel transformer-based diffusion model that enhances action prediction by conditioning on historical action sequences, thereby enabling more coherent and context-aware visuomotor policy learning. To further mitigate the computational cost associated with autoregressive inference, a caching mechanism is also introduced to store attention key-value pairs from previous timesteps, substantially reducing redundant computations during execution. Extensive experiments in both simulated and real-world environments, spanning diverse 2D and 3D manipulation tasks, demonstrate that CDP uniquely leverages historical action sequences to achieve significantly higher accuracy than existing methods. Moreover, even when faced with degraded input observation quality, CDP maintains remarkable precision by reasoning through temporal continuity, which highlights its practical robustness for robotic control under realistic, imperfect conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CDP: Towards Robust Autoregressive Visuomotor Policy Learning via Causal Diffusion":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel method, Causal Diffusion Policy (CDP), to improve the robustness and efficiency of visuomotor policy learning via diffusion models in robotic manipulation. CDP addresses the limitations of standard Diffusion Policy (DP) by incorporating historical action sequences into the action prediction process through a transformer-based architecture. This enables the model to reason about temporal context and improve coherence in action generation, especially under degraded observation conditions common in real-world robotic setups. The method also introduces a caching mechanism to store and reuse attention key-value pairs from previous timesteps, significantly reducing the computational cost associated with autoregressive inference. Experiments in simulated and real-world environments demonstrate that CDP outperforms existing methods in accuracy and robustness, particularly when dealing with sensor noise, occlusions, and hardware limitations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** The paper tackles a crucial challenge in robot learning: the lack of robustness of imitation learning techniques in the face of noisy or incomplete data, a frequent issue in real-world deployments.
*   **Sound Technical Approach:** Incorporating historical actions and a causal transformer is a logical and effective way to introduce temporal context and improve coherence in action generation. The caching mechanism is a valuable addition that makes the autoregressive inference process more efficient for real-time applications.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents a good range of experiments in both simulated and real-world environments, covering diverse manipulation tasks. The ablation studies provide further insights into the contributions of different components of the proposed method.
*   **Clearly Written and Well-Organized:** The paper is easy to understand, with a clear explanation of the proposed method and the experimental setup.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of causal reasoning and diffusion policies is reasonable, the individual components are relatively established. The novelty lies primarily in their integration and application to the specific problem of robust visuomotor control. The improvement is more about a targeted enhancement than a complete paradigm shift.
*   **Limited Exploration of Long Horizons:** While the introduction mentions the challenges in long-horizon tasks, the experiments presented do not fully explore this aspect. The tasks, while diverse, are still relatively short-term manipulation tasks.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Modes:** The paper mainly focuses on improved success rates. A more in-depth analysis of the types of errors that CDP avoids compared to baseline methods would provide stronger evidence for the benefits of causal reasoning. Are the failures more graceful, or do specific subtasks see the most improvement?
*   **Computational Cost of Caching:** While the caching mechanism improves inference speed, the paper lacks a detailed discussion on the memory overhead associated with storing the key-value pairs. This is a critical factor for real-time deployment, especially on resource-constrained robotic platforms. The number of previous steps that the caching mechanism can manage before becoming ineffective is unclear.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its practical contribution to making imitation learning for robotic manipulation more robust and deployable in real-world settings. The proposed CDP method provides a viable solution for dealing with noisy sensor data and hardware limitations, which are common challenges in robotic applications. This could accelerate the adoption of imitation learning in robotics by making it more reliable and easier to use.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a sound and well-executed approach to addressing an important problem, the novelty is primarily incremental. The core ideas of causal modeling and diffusion policies are not new, and the caching mechanism is a relatively straightforward optimization technique. The paper lacks thorough examination of the cost of caching and in-depth analyses of the error types, and long-horizon tasks are not fully explored. The improvement is still meaningful but more of an enhancement of existing methods, rather than a disruptive new approach.

### DETONATE: A Benchmark for Text-to-Image Alignment and Kernelized Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14903v1)
- **Authors**: Renjith Prasad, Abhilekh Borah, Hasnat Md Abdullah, Chathurangi Shyalika, Gurpreet Singh, Ritvik Garimella, Rajarshi Roy, Harshul Surana, Nasrin Imanpour, Suranjana Trivedy, Amit Sheth, Amitava Das
- **Abstract**: Alignment is crucial for text-to-image (T2I) models to ensure that generated images faithfully capture user intent while maintaining safety and fairness. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), prominent in large language models (LLMs), is extending its influence to T2I systems. This paper introduces DPO-Kernels for T2I models, a novel extension enhancing alignment across three dimensions: (i) Hybrid Loss, integrating embedding-based objectives with traditional probability-based loss for improved optimization; (ii) Kernelized Representations, employing Radial Basis Function (RBF), Polynomial, and Wavelet kernels for richer feature transformations and better separation between safe and unsafe inputs; and (iii) Divergence Selection, expanding beyond DPO's default Kullback-Leibler (KL) regularizer by incorporating Wasserstein and R'enyi divergences for enhanced stability and robustness. We introduce DETONATE, the first large-scale benchmark of its kind, comprising approximately 100K curated image pairs categorized as chosen and rejected. DETONATE encapsulates three axes of social bias and discrimination: Race, Gender, and Disability. Prompts are sourced from hate speech datasets, with images generated by leading T2I models including Stable Diffusion 3.5 Large, Stable Diffusion XL, and Midjourney. Additionally, we propose the Alignment Quality Index (AQI), a novel geometric measure quantifying latent-space separability of safe/unsafe image activations, revealing hidden vulnerabilities. Empirically, we demonstrate that DPO-Kernels maintain strong generalization bounds via Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization (HT-SR). DETONATE and complete code are publicly released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "DPO-Kernels," a novel extension of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) designed to improve text-to-image (T2I) alignment. The core idea is to embed alignment within a Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS), using kernels like Radial Basis Function (RBF), Polynomial, and Wavelet to enable more nuanced and semantically sensitive updates. The method also replaces the standard KL divergence with alternatives like Rényi and Wasserstein to enhance stability and robustness. The paper introduces "DETONATE," a new large-scale benchmark for T2I alignment focused on detecting social biases (Race, Gender, Disability).  Finally, it introduces the "Alignment Quality Index (AQI)," a metric that quantifies the geometric separability of safe and unsafe image activations within the model's latent space.  Experiments show that DPO-Kernels outperform existing alignment techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Significance:** Addressing the alignment crisis in T2I models is critically important given their potential for shaping public opinion and perpetuating biases.  The paper directly tackles a significant and growing problem.
    *   **Novelty of Approach:** Shifting from surface-level alignment fixes to structural regularization within the latent space is a conceptually strong and potentially more robust approach. The use of kernels within DPO is a novel and well-motivated idea to capture complex relationships in the representation space.
    *   **DETONATE Benchmark:** The creation of a large-scale, carefully curated benchmark focused on social biases is a major contribution. The dataset provides a valuable resource for the community. The construction process, including the keyword filtering and use of VLMs with human verification, increases its reliability.
    *   **AQI Metric:**  The introduction of the AQI provides a way to evaluate alignment fidelity *within* the model, rather than just relying on output-level classifications.  This is a significant step toward detecting "alignment faking" and ensuring genuine ethical behavior. The approach of using well-defined distance metrics for determining cluster separation and stability is sensible.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper presents strong empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of DPO-Kernels over existing methods on multiple metrics and using different backbone models (SD-XL, SD v1.5).
    *   **Theoretical Justification:**  The authors provide a good theoretical justification for their approach, drawing on concepts from RKHS, heavy-tailed self-regularization, and related literature.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The increased computational cost of DPO-Kernels (reported as a 3-4x increase in training time) is a significant drawback. While the paper discusses potential optimizations, this remains a practical limitation that could hinder widespread adoption.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The framework's sensitivity to hyperparameters like kernel bandwidth and polynomial degree is a concern, and the selection of optimal parameters needs to be a crucial part of the fine tuning process.
    *   **Kernel Choice Rationale:** While the paper explores different kernel choices, a stronger, more prescriptive guideline on *when* to choose each kernel would be helpful. Is there a theoretical basis for when each type is needed or would it involve empirical search only?
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the paper highlights successes, a more detailed examination of specific failure cases or types of prompts where DPO-Kernels still struggle would be valuable. Analyzing how AQI behaves in such cases could also provide further insight.
    *   **Overfitting Concerns:** Although the paper uses the Weighted Alpha metric, the reliance on highly expressive models increases the risk of overfitting.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of T2I alignment. The emphasis on structural alignment, the new benchmark, and the AQI metric offer valuable tools and insights for future research. If the computational cost can be addressed, DPO-Kernels could become a standard alignment technique.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a compelling and well-executed approach to a critical problem. The DETONATE benchmark and AQI metric are important contributions. While the computational cost and certain hyperparameters remain concerns, the conceptual novelty, strong empirical results, and theoretical backing justify a high score. The emphasis on a holistic improvement by moving beyond symptomatic fixes to actual structural changes in the latent space of models has potential to set new directions in safety-oriented AI development. The score is brought down due to the practical limitation in computational cost and lingering concerns about the difficulty of robust and generalisable fine-tuning.

### CrEst: Credibility Estimation for Contexts in LLMs via Weak Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14912v1)
- **Authors**: Dyah Adila, Shuai Zhang, Boran Han, Bonan Min, Yuyang Wang
- **Abstract**: The integration of contextual information has significantly enhanced the performance of large language models (LLMs) on knowledge-intensive tasks. However, existing methods often overlook a critical challenge: the credibility of context documents can vary widely, potentially leading to the propagation of unreliable information. In this paper, we introduce CrEst, a novel weakly supervised framework for assessing the credibility of context documents during LLM inference--without requiring manual annotations. Our approach is grounded in the insight that credible documents tend to exhibit higher semantic coherence with other credible documents, enabling automated credibility estimation through inter-document agreement. To incorporate credibility into LLM inference, we propose two integration strategies: a black-box approach for models without access to internal weights or activations, and a white-box method that directly modifies attention mechanisms. Extensive experiments across three model architectures and five datasets demonstrate that CrEst consistently outperforms strong baselines, achieving up to a 26.86% improvement in accuracy and a 3.49% increase in F1 score. Further analysis shows that CrEst maintains robust performance even under high-noise conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CrEst, a novel framework for estimating the credibility of context documents used by Large Language Models (LLMs) during inference.  CrEst employs a weak supervision approach, leveraging the insight that credible documents tend to exhibit greater semantic coherence with each other.  It doesn't require manual annotations for credibility estimation. The framework proposes two integration strategies for incorporating credibility scores: a black-box approach suitable for models with limited access to internals, and a white-box approach that directly modifies attention mechanisms.  The paper presents experimental results on multiple datasets and model architectures, demonstrating that CrEst outperforms strong baselines and maintains robust performance even under noisy conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using inter-document agreement as a signal for credibility, and operationalizing this through weak supervision, exhibits reasonable novelty. The distinction between black-box and white-box integration strategies is sensible and offers flexibility in applying CrEst to different model architectures. The adaptation of weak supervision for assessing document credibility within the context of LLM inference is a useful contribution. However, the core idea is not groundbreaking, and the use of semantic similarity for document coherence is a relatively standard approach in information retrieval.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is certainly relevant. The reliability of context is a known issue in retrieval-augmented generation. The fact that the method doesn't require manual annotations is also a definite plus in terms of scalability. The reported performance improvements across multiple datasets and models lend credence to the method's effectiveness. However, the improvements (while consistent) are sometimes modest, especially considering the already high performance levels of LLMs. A significant contribution would have to show drastically better performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Weak Supervision:** Avoids the need for expensive manual annotations.
    *   **Dual Integration Strategies:**  Offers flexibility for different LLM architectures.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  Extensive experiments across various datasets and models.
    *   **Noise Robustness:** Demonstrates the method's ability to maintain performance under noisy conditions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The performance gains, while consistent, aren't always dramatic.
    *   **Reliance on Document Coherence:** Assumes that a majority of documents are trustworthy; this assumption may be violated in some scenarios, especially in the face of sophisticated attacks (e.g., adversarial retrieval).
    *   **Limited Insight:** While the approach works empirically, the paper lacks deeper insights into *why* certain integration strategies are more effective for specific models or datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research in several ways:
    *   It highlights the importance of context credibility in retrieval-augmented generation.
    *   It provides a practical framework for estimating document credibility without manual annotations.
    *   It offers a baseline for future methods that aim to improve the robustness of LLMs to noisy or unreliable context.
    *   It inspires other research adapting weak supervision for other aspects of LLM inference.

**Score:** 6.5

**Justification:**

CrEst addresses a pertinent problem with a reasonably novel approach. The empirical results demonstrate the method's effectiveness, and the paper is well-written and organized. However, the performance gains are somewhat incremental, and the method relies on assumptions that may not always hold true. The paper also lacks deep theoretical insights. Thus, while it provides a useful practical solution and a solid baseline for future research, it falls short of being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

### Winter Soldier: Backdooring Language Models at Pre-Training with Indirect Data Poisoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14913v1)
- **Authors**: Wassim Bouaziz, Mathurin Videau, Nicolas Usunier, El-Mahdi El-Mhamdi
- **Abstract**: The pre-training of large language models (LLMs) relies on massive text datasets sourced from diverse and difficult-to-curate origins. Although membership inference attacks and hidden canaries have been explored to trace data usage, such methods rely on memorization of training data, which LM providers try to limit. In this work, we demonstrate that indirect data poisoning (where the targeted behavior is absent from training data) is not only feasible but also allow to effectively protect a dataset and trace its use. Using gradient-based optimization prompt-tuning, we make a model learn arbitrary secret sequences: secret responses to secret prompts that are absent from the training corpus. We validate our approach on language models pre-trained from scratch and show that less than 0.005% of poisoned tokens are sufficient to covertly make a LM learn a secret and detect it with extremely high confidence ($p < 10^{-55}$) with a theoretically certifiable scheme. Crucially, this occurs without performance degradation (on LM benchmarks) and despite secrets never appearing in the training set.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores a novel method for backdooring language models (LLMs) during pre-training using indirect data poisoning. Unlike traditional methods that rely on memorization of specific data points or canaries within the training data, this approach aims to subtly influence the model's behavior without directly exposing the target behavior in the training corpus. The core idea is to craft "poisonous" samples via gradient-based prompt tuning such that the model learns to associate specific "secret prompts" with "secret responses" that are never explicitly present in the training set. The authors demonstrate that a very small percentage of poisoned tokens can be sufficient to induce this behavior, making the backdoor difficult to detect while maintaining overall model performance.  They propose a dataset ownership verification (DOV) scheme that leverages the model's top-l predictions for detecting the presence of the backdoor, and extends theoretical guarantees from image poisoning to the text domain, allowing for certifiable false detection rate. They validate their approach on language models pre-trained from scratch.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of *indirect* data poisoning for LLMs is a significant contribution. Existing backdoor attacks often rely on explicit triggers or memorization, which are becoming easier to defend against. This indirect approach increases stealth by forcing the model to learn the association implicitly. Furthermore, the approach avoids relying on memorization of the target behavior in the training data, offering a potential advantage over membership inference attacks and canary-based detection methods. The adaptation of gradient-based prompt-tuning for crafting the poisonous samples is a clever technique to overcome the challenges posed by the discrete nature of text.

*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications for the security and trustworthiness of LLMs. If pre-trained models can be subtly influenced without explicit indicators in the data, it raises concerns about potential vulnerabilities exploited by malicious actors. The DOV scheme provides a valuable tool for dataset owners to verify whether their data has been used to train a model, even if the model providers attempt to obfuscate the training process. This has clear implications for copyright, data provenance, and model accountability. The approach might also pave the way for instilling knowledge implicitly, providing a mechanism for indirectly steering LLMs towards desired behaviors or imparting specific expertise.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The methodology is well-defined and technically sound.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, including ablations and comparisons to strong baselines.
    *   The extension of theoretical guarantees for false detection rates is rigorous and provides a strong foundation for the DOV scheme.
    *   The paper provides convincing empirical evidence of the effectiveness of indirect data poisoning and the utility of the DOV scheme.
    *   Addresses an increasingly important practical problem in the LLM field, as model training datasets grow in size, complexity and source diversity.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The threat model, while reasonable, requires that the attacker has knowledge of Bob's model architecture and tokenizer. This might not always be realistic in practice, especially for closed-source models (although they provide top-l prediction APIs). This assumption is addressed by considering scenarios where the attacker doesn't know model architecture, and poisons transfer well to models of different sizes.
    *   The compute intensiveness of the approach may limit its applicability for actors with limited resources. While the authors acknowledge this, it is still a significant barrier.
    *   The stealthiness of the poisons, while better than direct insertion, might still be detectable with advanced filtering techniques, such as those involving quality classifiers or perplexity.

**Rigorous Rationale**

This paper presents a genuinely innovative approach to data poisoning that has the potential to reshape the landscape of LLM security. By shifting the focus from explicit triggers to implicit learning, the authors have uncovered a significant vulnerability that warrants serious consideration. The DOV scheme provides a valuable tool for promoting data transparency and model accountability, which are becoming increasingly important as LLMs are deployed in a wider range of applications.

However, the threat model's assumptions and the computational cost constrain its immediate practicality. The detectability of the poisons, although an acknowledged issue, implies that a determined adversary could mitigate this attack. While the presented approach offers a substantial contribution to the field, it's not without limitations. The real-world applicability of this technique would likely be limited without modifications, like making it less computationally expensive and more difficult to detect.
*   .

### Frequency-Calibrated Membership Inference Attacks on Medical Image Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14919v1)
- **Authors**: Xinkai Zhao, Yuta Tokuoka, Junichiro Iwasawa, Keita Oda
- **Abstract**: The increasing use of diffusion models for image generation, especially in sensitive areas like medical imaging, has raised significant privacy concerns. Membership Inference Attack (MIA) has emerged as a potential approach to determine if a specific image was used to train a diffusion model, thus quantifying privacy risks. Existing MIA methods often rely on diffusion reconstruction errors, where member images are expected to have lower reconstruction errors than non-member images. However, applying these methods directly to medical images faces challenges. Reconstruction error is influenced by inherent image difficulty, and diffusion models struggle with high-frequency detail reconstruction. To address these issues, we propose a Frequency-Calibrated Reconstruction Error (FCRE) method for MIAs on medical image diffusion models. By focusing on reconstruction errors within a specific mid-frequency range and excluding both high-frequency (difficult to reconstruct) and low-frequency (less informative) regions, our frequency-selective approach mitigates the confounding factor of inherent image difficulty. Specifically, we analyze the reverse diffusion process, obtain the mid-frequency reconstruction error, and compute the structural similarity index score between the reconstructed and original images. Membership is determined by comparing this score to a threshold. Experiments on several medical image datasets demonstrate that our FCRE method outperforms existing MIA methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Frequency-Calibrated Membership Inference Attacks on Medical Image Diffusion Models" addresses privacy concerns surrounding the use of diffusion models for generating medical images. The authors propose a novel membership inference attack (MIA) method called Frequency-Calibrated Reconstruction Error (FCRE). FCRE aims to overcome the limitations of existing MIA techniques when applied to medical images, which often have distinct frequency characteristics compared to natural images. The method focuses on reconstruction errors within a specific mid-frequency range, excluding both high-frequency (difficult to reconstruct) and low-frequency (less informative) regions.  By using a frequency-selective approach and incorporating the Structural Similarity Index (SSIM), FCRE mitigates the confounding factor of inherent image difficulty and outperforms existing MIA methods on several medical image datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a well-defined approach to MIA by considering the specific frequency characteristics inherent in medical images. The idea of focusing on a mid-frequency band for reconstruction error analysis to calibrate for image difficulty is a worthwhile contribution. While the individual components (MIA, diffusion models, frequency analysis, SSIM) are not entirely new, their combination and application to the medical imaging domain with a focus on difficulty calibration represents significant novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it highlights the privacy risks associated with diffusion models in the context of sensitive medical data.  The development of an effective MIA method for medical images allows for a better assessment of these risks and can inform the development of more privacy-preserving generative models. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of the proposed FCRE method over existing MIAs, making it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners. Furthermore, by introducing difficulty calibration methods for privacy inference, this paper will potentially influence new defensive and adversarial techniques within the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies a specific problem (MIA on medical images with distinct frequency characteristics).
    *   The proposed FCRE method is well-motivated and explained.
    *   The experimental results are strong and demonstrate the effectiveness of FCRE.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of frequency range selection.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations of FCRE. For example, how does the performance vary across different medical imaging modalities or disease types?
    *   The selection of the optimal frequency range (Lmin and Lmax) requires some manual tuning, and a more adaptive approach would be desirable. While the authors acknowledge this in the ablation studies, it is still a major area for improvement.
    * The authors mention other methods could be improved by incorporating frequency ranges as well, but they are not included as baselines for comparison. The inclusion of these methods could highlight the value of their approach better.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several areas:

    *   Development of more robust MIA methods for different data types.
    *   Design of privacy-preserving diffusion models for medical imaging.
    *   Further exploration of difficulty calibration techniques in MIA.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of privacy and security in machine learning. It addresses a relevant problem in a sensitive domain, proposes a novel solution that overcomes the limitations of existing methods, and provides thorough experimental validation. While there are some areas for improvement, the paper's novelty, significance, and potential influence warrant a good evaluation.

- **Score**: 7/10

### FORTRESS: Frontier Risk Evaluation for National Security and Public Safety
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14922v1)
- **Authors**: Christina Q. Knight, Kaustubh Deshpande, Ved Sirdeshmukh, Meher Mankikar, Scale Red Team, SEAL Research Team, Julian Michael
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) introduces dual-use capabilities that could both threaten and bolster national security and public safety (NSPS). Models implement safeguards to protect against potential misuse relevant to NSPS and allow for benign users to receive helpful information. However, current benchmarks often fail to test safeguard robustness to potential NSPS risks in an objective, robust way. We introduce FORTRESS: 500 expert-crafted adversarial prompts with instance-based rubrics of 4-7 binary questions for automated evaluation across 3 domains (unclassified information only): Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear and Explosive (CBRNE), Political Violence & Terrorism, and Criminal & Financial Illicit Activities, with 10 total subcategories across these domains. Each prompt-rubric pair has a corresponding benign version to test for model over-refusals. This evaluation of frontier LLMs' safeguard robustness reveals varying trade-offs between potential risks and model usefulness: Claude-3.5-Sonnet demonstrates a low average risk score (ARS) (14.09 out of 100) but the highest over-refusal score (ORS) (21.8 out of 100), while Gemini 2.5 Pro shows low over-refusal (1.4) but a high average potential risk (66.29). Deepseek-R1 has the highest ARS at 78.05, but the lowest ORS at only 0.06. Models such as o1 display a more even trade-off between potential risks and over-refusals (with an ARS of 21.69 and ORS of 5.2). To provide policymakers and researchers with a clear understanding of models' potential risks, we publicly release FORTRESS at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ScaleAI/fortress_public. We also maintain a private set for evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the FORTRESS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FORTRESS, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the robustness of large language model (LLM) safeguards against potential misuse in areas related to national security and public safety (NSPS). The benchmark focuses on three domains: Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear, and Explosive (CBRNE); Political Violence & Terrorism; and Criminal & Financial Illicit Activities. FORTRESS consists of 500 expert-crafted adversarial prompts, each paired with a benign counterpart for assessing over-refusals (instances where the model inappropriately refuses to respond to a harmless request), and instance-specific rubrics with 4-7 binary questions for automated evaluation of responses. The authors evaluate several frontier LLMs using FORTRESS and analyze the trade-offs between potential risks and model usefulness. The public version of FORTRESS, with 500 prompts, is released for broader use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *specific focus* on NSPS-related risks.  While existing benchmarks address broader harm categories, they often lack the granularity and specialized knowledge necessary to effectively evaluate safeguards against threats like bioweapon development or terrorist attack planning. The inclusion of benign prompts to assess over-refusals is also a good design choice contributing to a more complete evaluation of model safety and usability. The instance-specific rubrics are a valuable addition for more granular and automatable assessment, however, this is not entirely new.
*   **Significance:** FORTRESS fills a critical gap in LLM safety research. The NSPS domain is of utmost importance, and current safeguard evaluations tend to be too general or lack the realistic adversarial scenarios necessary for rigorous testing. By providing a specialized benchmark, FORTRESS enables researchers and policymakers to better understand the potential risks posed by LLMs in this sensitive area and identify areas where safeguards need improvement. The benchmark could influence the design of more robust and nuanced safety mechanisms, leading to safer and more responsible deployment of LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Domain Expertise:** The involvement of expert red teamers in crafting the adversarial prompts ensures that the benchmark reflects realistic threat scenarios.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The combination of adversarial prompts, benign counterparts, and instance-specific rubrics provides a more comprehensive evaluation than simple pass/fail tests.
    *   **Automation:** The design of the instance-specific rubrics facilitates automated evaluation, allowing for more scalable testing of LLMs.
    *   **Public Release:** The public release of FORTRESS promotes transparency and enables broader participation in safety research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Static Prompts:** The benchmark focuses on static, single-turn prompts. Real-world misuse often involves more complex, multi-turn interactions that FORTRESS does not capture. This is a significant limitation.
    *   **Limited Scope (Public Set):** The relatively small size of the public dataset (500 adversarial prompts) may limit its statistical power and coverage of specific risk areas. Releasing a broader scope and more comprehensive taxonomy of threat models would serve as a more robust foundation.
    *   **Over-reliance on LLM Judges:** While the paper attempts to validate the automated evaluation process, reliance on LLM judges for rubric assessment introduces potential biases and inconsistencies, despite majority voting. Further validation with human experts would strengthen the validity of the evaluation results.
    *   **Potential for Misuse:** The very nature of the benchmark, detailing prompts that can elicit harmful responses, increases the risk of misuse. The authors acknowledge this and took steps to mitigate, but the risk remains.
*   **Potential Influence:** The benchmark has the potential to influence the direction of LLM safety research, particularly in the NSPS domain. It could also inform policy decisions related to the responsible development and deployment of LLMs.
*   **Overall:** This is a strong and significant contribution to the area of LLM security. While the reliance on static prompts, the challenges around automation, and the risk of misuse do detract slightly from the overall impact, it is nonetheless a valuable resource to researchers and policymakers alike.

### Vision Transformers for End-to-End Quark-Gluon Jet Classification from Calorimeter Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14934v1)
- **Authors**: Md Abrar Jahin, Shahriar Soudeep, Arian Rahman Aditta, M. F. Mridha, Nafiz Fahad, Md. Jakir Hossen
- **Abstract**: Distinguishing between quark- and gluon-initiated jets is a critical and challenging task in high-energy physics, pivotal for improving new physics searches and precision measurements at the Large Hadron Collider. While deep learning, particularly Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), has advanced jet tagging using image-based representations, the potential of Vision Transformer (ViT) architectures, renowned for modeling global contextual information, remains largely underexplored for direct calorimeter image analysis, especially under realistic detector and pileup conditions. This paper presents a systematic evaluation of ViTs and ViT-CNN hybrid models for quark-gluon jet classification using simulated 2012 CMS Open Data. We construct multi-channel jet-view images from detector-level energy deposits (ECAL, HCAL) and reconstructed tracks, enabling an end-to-end learning approach. Our comprehensive benchmarking demonstrates that ViT-based models, notably ViT+MaxViT and ViT+ConvNeXt hybrids, consistently outperform established CNN baselines in F1-score, ROC-AUC, and accuracy, highlighting the advantage of capturing long-range spatial correlations within jet substructure. This work establishes the first systematic framework and robust performance baselines for applying ViT architectures to calorimeter image-based jet classification using public collider data, alongside a structured dataset suitable for further deep learning research in this domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Vision Transformers (ViTs) and ViT-CNN hybrid architectures for quark-gluon jet classification using calorimeter images from the 2012 CMS Open Data. It aims to improve the distinction between quark- and gluon-initiated jets, a crucial task in high-energy physics. The authors construct multi-channel jet images from ECAL, HCAL, and tracking data, enabling an end-to-end learning approach. They benchmark ViT-based models against CNN baselines and investigate hyperparameter sensitivity. The results show that ViT-based models, particularly ViT+MaxViT and ViT+ConvNeXt, outperform CNNs in F1-score, ROC-AUC, and accuracy, highlighting the benefits of capturing long-range spatial correlations in jet substructure. The paper provides a framework and performance baselines for applying ViT architectures to calorimeter image-based jet classification using public collider data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel application of ViTs to calorimeter image-based jet classification within the specific context of CMS Open Data. While ViTs have been applied to particle physics before (mainly on particle cloud data), this is one of the first systematic studies to apply these architectures directly to images with energy deposits of particle tracks on a detector level and uses the publicly available and realistic CMS open data. The hybrid ViT-CNN architectures explored add a layer of sophistication.  Prior work leveraging CNNs on calorimeter images exists; however, this study extends that body of work by investigating ViTs and ViT-CNN hybrids.
*   **Significance:** The ability to distinguish quark- and gluon-initiated jets is essential for many precision measurements and beyond-the-standard-model searches at the LHC. The paper's finding that ViTs outperform CNNs on this task suggests a potential for improved jet tagging algorithms. The provision of performance baselines and a structured dataset is also valuable for future research. The usage of CMS Open Data is a crucial contribution because it offers the ability to benchmark on real data conditions, something that had been lacking in HEP DL research.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive benchmarking against CNN baselines.
    *   Systematic hyperparameter sensitivity analysis.
    *   Use of realistic CMS Open Data, allowing for the development of models more closely aligned with real-world experimental conditions.
    *   Well-structured dataset and code availability, enabling reproducibility and future research.
    *   Exploration of hybrid ViT-CNN architectures, potentially combining the strengths of both approaches.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study relies on simulated data, albeit using the CMS Open Data. While CMS Open Data is a crucial resource, evaluation on real experimental data with different detector and pileup conditions would further strengthen the findings.
    *   The paper acknowledges that the ViT models require significant computing resources, potentially limiting their practicality for real-time applications without further optimization.
    *   While the paper shows that ViTs perform well, it does not deeply investigate which specific jet features contribute to the performance gain. A deeper understanding of the model's learned representations would be highly valuable.
    *   It doesn't compare explicitly to all SoTA approaches. Several EFN, GNN or even sequential methods have shown state-of-the-art performance, especially when adding some level of explainability such as the Lund jet plane.

**Justification:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field of high-energy physics by demonstrating the potential of ViTs for quark-gluon jet classification using realistic detector-level images. While there are some limitations, particularly the reliance on simulated data, the comprehensive benchmarking, hyperparameter analysis, and availability of data and code make this a valuable resource for future research. The performance gains achieved by the ViT-based models, the exploration of hybrid architectures, and, importantly, the application to public CMS data represent significant progress. The lack of strong mechanistic insight into what the models learn and the missing comparison with all potentially viable SoTA approaches limit this work's potential.

### Structured Moral Reasoning in Language Models: A Value-Grounded Evaluation Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14948v1)
- **Authors**: Mohna Chakraborty, Lu Wang, David Jurgens
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in domains requiring moral understanding, yet their reasoning often remains shallow, and misaligned with human reasoning. Unlike humans, whose moral reasoning integrates contextual trade-offs, value systems, and ethical theories, LLMs often rely on surface patterns, leading to biased decisions in morally and ethically complex scenarios. To address this gap, we present a value-grounded framework for evaluating and distilling structured moral reasoning in LLMs. We benchmark 12 open-source models across four moral datasets using a taxonomy of prompts grounded in value systems, ethical theories, and cognitive reasoning strategies. Our evaluation is guided by four questions: (1) Does reasoning improve LLM decision-making over direct prompting? (2) Which types of value/ethical frameworks most effectively guide LLM reasoning? (3) Which cognitive reasoning strategies lead to better moral performance? (4) Can small-sized LLMs acquire moral competence through distillation? We find that prompting with explicit moral structure consistently improves accuracy and coherence, with first-principles reasoning and Schwartz's + care-ethics scaffolds yielding the strongest gains. Furthermore, our supervised distillation approach transfers moral competence from large to small models without additional inference cost. Together, our results offer a scalable path toward interpretable and value-grounded models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of shallow and inconsistent moral reasoning in large language models (LLMs). It introduces a value-grounded framework for evaluating and improving moral reasoning in LLMs using structured prompts based on value systems (e.g., Schwartz's Value Theory), ethical theories (e.g., Care Ethics), and cognitive reasoning strategies (e.g., first-principles reasoning). The authors benchmarked 12 open-source LLMs across four moral datasets, finding that structured moral prompting improves accuracy and coherence, especially with first-principles reasoning and Schwartz's + care-ethics scaffolds. They also demonstrate that supervised distillation can transfer moral competence from large to small models without additional inference cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic approach to scaffolding LLM reasoning with explicit normative structures drawn from established ethical and psychological theories. While prior work has explored moral reasoning in LLMs and used prompting techniques, this paper provides a comprehensive taxonomy and evaluation framework based on structured value systems, ethical theories, and cognitive reasoning strategies. It represents a step beyond simple chain-of-thought prompting and towards a more theoretically grounded approach to moral alignment. The application of distillation to transfer this structured reasoning to smaller models also contributes to the novelty.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue – the potential for biased and inconsistent moral judgments in LLMs, especially as they are deployed in sensitive domains. By demonstrating that structured moral prompting can improve the quality and consistency of LLM decisions, the paper offers a valuable contribution to the field of responsible AI. The finding that smaller models can acquire moral competence through distillation is also significant, as it suggests a more scalable path toward interpretable and value-grounded models.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper introduces a well-defined and theoretically grounded framework for evaluating and improving moral reasoning in LLMs.
    * **Systematic Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation of 12 LLMs across four datasets, providing robust empirical evidence to support their claims.
    * **Practical Implications:** The paper offers practical guidance for developing and deploying more ethically aligned LLMs.
    * **Distillation Approach:** The distillation approach enables the transfer of moral reasoning to smaller, more efficient models.
    * **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is clearly written and easy to follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to four moral datasets and 12 open-source LLMs.  Expanding the dataset and models considered would strengthen the findings.
    * **Static Evaluation:** The evaluation is performed in a static, single-turn setting.  Future work should explore interactive and multi-turn settings.
    * **Selection of Ethical Theories and value systems:** While the authors mention a wide array of such theories and systems, the selection used is not exhaustive and may reflect a bias. The impact of non-Western and underrepresented traditions should also be considered.
    * **Teacher model bias:** As the teacher model is LLM-based itself, this may reflect its biases and limitations which it imparts to the student models.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more responsible and ethically aligned LLMs. The value-grounded framework and the distillation approach offer concrete strategies for improving moral reasoning in LLMs and making them more scalable.

* **Score Justification:** While the paper has some limitations, its novel and systematic approach to addressing a critical issue in AI ethics warrants a high score. The paper offers practical guidance and empirical evidence to support its claims, making it a valuable contribution to the field. However, the limitations mentioned above keep it from achieving a score of 9 or 10.

### From Chat to Checkup: Can Large Language Models Assist in Diabetes Prediction?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14949v1)
- **Authors**: Shadman Sakib, Oishy Fatema Akhand, Ajwad Abrar
- **Abstract**: While Machine Learning (ML) and Deep Learning (DL) models have been widely used for diabetes prediction, the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for structured numerical data is still not well explored. In this study, we test the effectiveness of LLMs in predicting diabetes using zero-shot, one-shot, and three-shot prompting methods. We conduct an empirical analysis using the Pima Indian Diabetes Database (PIDD). We evaluate six LLMs, including four open-source models: Gemma-2-27B, Mistral-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and Llama-3.2-2B. We also test two proprietary models: GPT-4o and Gemini Flash 2.0. In addition, we compare their performance with three traditional machine learning models: Random Forest, Logistic Regression, and Support Vector Machine (SVM). We use accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score as evaluation metrics. Our results show that proprietary LLMs perform better than open-source ones, with GPT-4o and Gemma-2-27B achieving the highest accuracy in few-shot settings. Notably, Gemma-2-27B also outperforms the traditional ML models in terms of F1-score. However, there are still issues such as performance variation across prompting strategies and the need for domain-specific fine-tuning. This study shows that LLMs can be useful for medical prediction tasks and encourages future work on prompt engineering and hybrid approaches to improve healthcare predictions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to diabetes prediction using the Pima Indian Diabetes Database (PIDD). It compares the performance of six LLMs (Gemma-2-27B, Mistral-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Llama-3.2-2B, GPT-40, and Gemini Flash 2.0) against traditional machine learning models (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and SVM). The LLMs are evaluated using zero-shot, one-shot, and three-shot prompting techniques. The study analyzes accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score to assess the effectiveness of LLMs in this structured medical data classification task. The results show that proprietary LLMs generally outperform open-source ones, with Gemma-2-27B and GPT-40 achieving the highest accuracy in few-shot settings. Although LLMs demonstrate potential without task-specific training, their performance doesn't yet surpass that of traditional machine learning models on this dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper explores a relatively underexplored area: applying LLMs to structured numerical medical data for prediction tasks. While LLMs have seen use in various domains, their application to structured tabular data is less common. This study bridges this gap.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study includes a good variety of LLMs, including both open-source and proprietary models, allowing for a comparative analysis. The inclusion of traditional ML models as baselines provides a solid benchmark.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Exploration:** The paper investigates the impact of different prompting strategies (zero-shot, one-shot, three-shot), providing insights into how context affects LLM performance in this domain.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The experimental setup, evaluation metrics, and data preprocessing steps are well-defined and easy to understand, which enhances the reproducibility of the results.
    *   **Relevance:**  Diabetes prediction is a significant healthcare problem, making the study relevant to real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Methodology:** The application of prompt engineering to LLMs is not inherently novel. The novelty is primarily in the specific application domain (diabetes prediction).
    *   **Dataset Limitation:** The PIDD dataset is a relatively small and well-known dataset. While suitable as a starting point, demonstrating the generalizability of LLMs to larger, more complex, and potentially imbalanced medical datasets is crucial.
    *   **Suboptimal Performance:** The LLMs, even the best ones, don't outperform traditional ML models by a significant margin on this dataset. This raises the question of whether LLMs are truly beneficial for this type of task *without* fine-tuning. The paper suggests the potential, but it's not convincingly demonstrated. The lower F1 scores compared to Random Forest are telling.
    *   **Lack of Fine-Tuning:** The study explicitly avoids fine-tuning. While this allows for a baseline comparison of LLMs "out-of-the-box," it also misses the opportunity to explore the full potential of LLMs in this domain. Fine-tuning *might* significantly improve performance. The lack of this analysis is a missed opportunity.
    *   **Resource Intensity:** The study acknowledges that LLMs demand more resources than traditional ML models. For real-world deployment, this trade-off between performance and resource cost needs to be considered much more thoroughly. The paper doesn't offer a cost-benefit analysis.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** The paper lacks a detailed error analysis. Understanding the types of errors LLMs make (e.g., false positives vs. false negatives) and why they make them is crucial for improving their performance and trustworthiness in medical applications.
    *   **Overstatement of Potential:** The paper somewhat overstates the LLMs' potential for low-data scenarios. The observed performance differences are not substantial enough to declare LLMs significantly better than ML models in these environments.
    *   **Missing Ethical Considerations:** Medical applications require careful consideration of ethical implications, including fairness, bias, and privacy. This paper would be stronger if it at least touched upon these issues.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the understanding of how LLMs can be used for structured data classification in healthcare.
    *   It provides a baseline for future research exploring fine-tuning and hybrid approaches.
    *   It highlights the challenges and limitations of applying LLMs "off-the-shelf" to medical prediction tasks.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the paper presents a useful initial investigation into applying LLMs to a structured medical data task, several weaknesses limit its novelty and significance. The lack of substantial performance gains over traditional ML models, the use of a relatively simple dataset, the absence of fine-tuning, and the limited error analysis diminish its impact. The study's primary contribution is in providing a comparative analysis of different LLMs and prompting strategies, but the practical implications are not yet fully realized.  The ethical aspects have been missed as well.
