### Thinking in Directivity: Speech Large Language Model for Multi-Talker Directional Speech Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14973v1)
- **Authors**: Jiamin Xie, Ju Lin, Yiteng Huang, Tyler Vuong, Zhaojiang Lin, Zhaojun Yang, Peng Su, Prashant Rawat, Sangeeta Srivastava, Ming Sun, Florian Metze
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have demonstrated that prompting large language models (LLM) with audio encodings enables effective speech recognition capabilities. However, the ability of Speech LLMs to comprehend and process multi-channel audio with spatial cues remains a relatively uninvestigated area of research. In this work, we present directional-SpeechLlama, a novel approach that leverages the microphone array of smart glasses to achieve directional speech recognition, source localization, and bystander cross-talk suppression. To enhance the model's ability to understand directivity, we propose two key techniques: serialized directional output training (S-DOT) and contrastive direction data augmentation (CDDA). Experimental results show that our proposed directional-SpeechLlama effectively captures the relationship between textual cues and spatial audio, yielding strong performance in both speech recognition and source localization tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Thinking in Directivity: Speech Large Language Model for Multi-Talker Directional Speech Recognition":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Directional-SpeechLLaMA, a novel approach that uses the microphone array of smart glasses to enable directional speech recognition, source localization, and bystander cross-talk suppression.  The key contributions are two techniques designed to enhance the model's understanding of directivity: Serialized Directional Output Training (S-DOT) and Contrastive Direction Data Augmentation (CDDA). Experimental results indicate that Directional-SpeechLLaMA effectively learns the relationship between textual cues and spatial audio, resulting in strong performance on both speech recognition and source localization tasks. The model is fine-tuned in three stages, starting with multi-channel ASR, then direction-aware ASR using S-DOT, and finally target-direction ASR fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of adapting SpeechLLaMA to incorporate directional awareness using microphone arrays from smart glasses is somewhat novel, especially with the proposed S-DOT and CDDA techniques.  The application to smart glasses also has practical relevance.  However, the reliance on existing SpeechLLaMA architectures and beamforming techniques reduces the degree of innovation.  S-DOT builds upon prior work on serialized output training but extends it to directional information. CDDA adapts contrastive learning for this specific problem. The combination of these approaches is the most novel aspect. The use of beamforming as a front-end is standard practice.
* **Significance:** If the performance gains are significant and the model proves robust, this work could have an impact on speech recognition in real-world wearable device scenarios. The ability to filter out bystander speech and localize sources is particularly valuable. The multi-stage training process is a sound method for knowledge transfer. The experiments, while focused on simulated data, show a promising trend towards real-world applicability. A limitation is the lack of real-world data validation which might have highlighted the challenges of implementation on resource limited smart glasses (such as battery life, compute and memory constraints). The generalizability of the proposed methods to other architectures and datasets could also be explored to give a more wider appeal. The analysis on the effects of unseen directions and acoustic conditions is helpful to inform future directions.
* **Strengths:**
    * Addresses a relevant problem in speech recognition: handling multi-talker scenarios with spatial cues.
    * The S-DOT and CDDA techniques appear effective in improving directional understanding.
    * Clear experimental setup and evaluation metrics.
    * Demonstrates the potential of leveraging spatial information for improved ASR performance.
    * Explores unseen directions and acoustic conditions
* **Weaknesses:**
    * Relies heavily on existing architectures and established methods.
    * Evaluation is primarily based on simulated data which raises questions about real-world robustness.
    * Limited analysis on the model's computational complexity and suitability for resource-constrained devices like smart glasses.
    * Focuses on planar array arrangements, potentially limiting its generalizability.
    * Does not adequately address the performance for different speaker separations when both talkers are in frontal directions
* **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence research in the following areas:
    * Development of more sophisticated speech recognition systems for wearable devices.
    * Exploration of spatial audio processing techniques for source separation and localization.
    * Application of LLMs to multi-channel audio data.
    * Improved human computer interface and interaction with smart glasses in various scenarios
    * Further exploration of Serialized Output Training and Contrastive Learning for speech tasks.
* **Score Rationale:** The paper presents a practical application of existing techniques to a relevant problem, with some novel additions in the form of S-DOT and CDDA. The results are promising, but the reliance on simulated data and the lack of extensive analysis on computational constraints limit its overall significance.  Therefore, a score reflecting moderate novelty and potential impact is warranted.

### Hypothesis Testing for Quantifying LLM-Human Misalignment in Multiple Choice Settings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14997v1)
- **Authors**: Harbin Hong, Sebastian Caldas, Liu Leqi
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly appear in social science research (e.g., economics and marketing), it becomes crucial to assess how well these models replicate human behavior. In this work, using hypothesis testing, we present a quantitative framework to assess the misalignment between LLM-simulated and actual human behaviors in multiple-choice survey settings. This framework allows us to determine in a principled way whether a specific language model can effectively simulate human opinions, decision-making, and general behaviors represented through multiple-choice options. We applied this framework to a popular language model for simulating people's opinions in various public surveys and found that this model is ill-suited for simulating the tested sub-populations (e.g., across different races, ages, and incomes) for contentious questions. This raises questions about the alignment of this language model with the tested populations, highlighting the need for new practices in using LLMs for social science studies beyond naive simulations of human subjects.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of evaluating the alignment between Large Language Models (LLMs) and human behavior in multiple-choice settings.  It proposes a quantitative framework based on hypothesis testing to determine if LLM-simulated responses are statistically indistinguishable from actual human responses.  The null hypothesis is that LLM and human response distributions are equal, and permutation tests and the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test are used to potentially reject this. The paper applies this framework to the Disagreement500 dataset, finding that a popular LLM (GPT-3.5-Turbo) often fails to accurately simulate human opinions across various demographic subgroups, especially for contentious questions. The study highlights the limitations of using LLMs for naive human subject simulation in social science research, particularly when opinion diversity is high. The paper introduces the subgroup level and question level to analyze misalignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using hypothesis testing, specifically permutation tests, to assess LLM-human misalignment is a sound and novel approach. Prior work uses distance metrics which are subjective in determining their threshold. The use of rigorous statistical testing provides a more principled alternative.  The paper clearly defines the null and alternative hypotheses and chooses appropriate test statistics. Analyzing both subgroup-specific and question-specific misalignment metrics adds further depth.

*   **Significance:** Assessing the validity of LLMs as proxies for human subjects is increasingly crucial, especially as LLMs are being integrated into social science research, e.g., economics and marketing. The paper provides a valuable tool for evaluating this aspect, exposing limitations in the current generation of LLMs. The framework's ability to identify demographic biases is significant, highlighting the need for careful consideration when using LLMs to model human behavior. The observation that LLMs struggle with high-entropy, contentious questions is an important insight.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper presents a clearly defined and reproducible framework.
    *   The use of established statistical tests (permutation tests, Kolmogorov-Smirnov) adds rigor.
    *   The analysis of subgroup and question-level misalignment offers granular insights.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating the misalignment of a widely used LLM (GPT-3.5-Turbo).
    *   Clear writing style and logical flow.
    *   The method is easy to adapt to other LLMs and tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The study focuses on a single dataset (Disagreement500) which may limit the generalizability of the findings. More datasets are needed.
    *   While the framework is general, the experimental evaluation is limited to a specific type of survey question (multiple choice). More surveys, tasks and models should be tested.
    *   The paper does not delve deeply into *why* the misalignment occurs, but suggests potential factors, such as LLM steering challenges or safeguarding against providing controversial answers. This could be investigated further.
    *   The paper could benefit from exploring alternative test statistics for use cases where the specific trends are important.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research in using LLMs for social science simulations. It advocates for a shift from naive LLM usage to a more careful assessment of alignment with human populations. It encourages the development of more robust evaluation methods and could lead to the creation of LLMs that better represent diverse viewpoints.

**Overall Assessment:**
This paper makes a significant contribution by providing a solid framework to asses LLM-Human misalignment. While its experimental validation is somewhat limited, the methodology is rigorous, clearly explained, and has practical applications.

### Memory Tokens: Large Language Models Can Generate Reversible Sentence Embeddings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15001v1)
- **Authors**: Ignacio Sastre, Aiala Rosá
- **Abstract**: In this work, we observe an interesting phenomenon: it is possible to generate reversible sentence embeddings that allow an LLM to reconstruct the original text exactly, without modifying the model's weights. This is achieved by introducing a special memory token, whose embedding is optimized through training on a fixed sequence. When prompted with this embedding, the model reconstructs the fixed sequence exactly. We evaluate this phenomenon across English and Spanish datasets, sequences of up to approximately 240 tokens, and model scales ranging from 100M to 8B parameters. Notably, Llama 3.1 8B successfully reconstructs all tested sequences. Our findings highlight an interesting capability of LLMs and suggest potential applications in memory-based retrieval, compression, and controlled text generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores an intriguing phenomenon with Large Language Models (LLMs): the ability to create reversible sentence embeddings.  They achieve this by training a dedicated embedding for a special "memory token" on a fixed text sequence while keeping the rest of the LLM's weights frozen. After training, prompting the LLM with the memory token allows it to perfectly reconstruct the original text sequence.  The authors evaluate this phenomenon across different languages (English and Spanish), sequence lengths, model sizes (GPT-2 and the Llama 3 family), and datasets. They found that larger models, especially Llama 3.1 8B, are consistently successful in reconstructing sequences perfectly. The paper suggests potential applications for this technique in memory-based retrieval, compression, and controlled text generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of training a single token embedding to perfectly memorize and reconstruct a sequence is interesting, but not entirely groundbreaking.  The connection to P*-tuning techniques is clearly articulated, and the method closely resembles techniques where special embeddings are trained to guide model behavior. The novelty lies in the *degree of overfitting* and achieving *perfect reconstruction* without model modification, rather than introducing a fundamentally new training paradigm. The related work section does a good job positioning the contribution, but also highlights the relative lack of groundbreaking innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. It demonstrates a capability of LLMs that wasn't explicitly highlighted before, showcasing their representational capacity. While perfect reconstruction is a neat trick, the practical applications outlined are somewhat speculative at this stage. The paper's true value likely lies in inspiring further research into using learned embeddings for memory, compression, or even adversarial attacks. Demonstrating a way to induce specific behavior in LLMs without fine-tuning is always valuable, potentially contributing to interpretability research. The observation that larger models are better at this is not particularly surprising, but provides confirmation of the importance of scale in LLM capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined experimental setup.
    *   Clear presentation of results with quantitative evaluations.
    *   Evaluation across different datasets, model sizes, and languages.
    *   Well-placed within the landscape of related work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The applications discussed are preliminary and lack concrete demonstrations. The suggestions (memory-based retrieval, controlled generation) seem a bit forced.
    *   The method is computationally expensive due to the need for backpropagation through the entire LLM for embedding optimization.
    *   The study doesn't deeply explore why this phenomenon occurs. Mechanistic interpretability could greatly strengthen the paper.
    *   The limitations section is good, but should perhaps emphasize the lack of control over *what* the token "means". If you wanted the token to be a memory of a text *and* exhibit certain properties (e.g. relate to other texts in a certain way), you don't have direct control.
    *   The paper would benefit from more analysis on how the embedding vector itself is encoding the information.

**Overall:**

The paper presents an interesting observation and provides a solid experimental evaluation. However, the lack of concrete applications and deeper analysis, combined with the incremental nature of the contribution compared to existing P*-tuning methods, prevents it from being a truly high-impact paper.

### Scaling Intelligence: Designing Data Centers for Next-Gen Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15006v1)
- **Authors**: Jesmin Jahan Tithi, Hanjiang Wu, Avishaii Abuhatzera, Fabrizio Petrini
- **Abstract**: The explosive growth of Large Language Models (LLMs) - such as GPT-4 with 1.8 trillion parameters - demands a radical rethinking of data center architecture to ensure scalability, efficiency, and cost-effectiveness. Our work provides a comprehensive co-design framework that jointly explores FLOPS, HBM bandwidth and capacity, multiple network topologies (two-tier vs. FullFlat optical), the size of the scale-out domain, and popular parallelism/optimization strategies used in LLMs. We introduce and evaluate FullFlat network architectures, which provide uniform high-bandwidth, low-latency connectivity between all nodes, and demonstrate their transformative impact on performance and scalability. Through detailed sensitivity analyses, we quantify the benefits of overlapping compute and communication, leveraging hardware-accelerated collectives, wider scale-out domains, and larger memory capacity. Our study spans both sparse (mixture of experts) and dense transformer-based LLMs, revealing how system design choices affect Model FLOPS Utilization (MFU = Model flops per token x Observed tokens per sec / Peak flops of the hardware) and overall throughput. For the co-design study, we extended and validated a performance modeling tool capable of predicting LLM runtime within 10% of real-world measurements. Our findings offer actionable insights and a practical roadmap for designing AI data centers that can efficiently support trillion-parameter models, reduce optimization complexity, and sustain the rapid evolution of AI capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper, "Scaling Intelligence: Designing Data Centers for Next-Gen Language Models," investigates the co-design of data center architectures specifically tailored for training large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4. It addresses the growing demands for compute power, memory bandwidth, and network scalability required by these models. The authors introduce and evaluate the potential of FullFlat network architectures, which offer uniform high-bandwidth, low-latency connectivity between all nodes in the data center.  The study explores the impact of various parameters, including FLOPS, HBM capacity, network topologies (two-tier vs. FullFlat), scale-out domain size, and different parallelism strategies. Through extensive simulations and analysis using an extended version of the Calculon performance modeling tool, the paper provides actionable insights into designing AI data centers capable of efficiently supporting trillion-parameter models, reducing optimization complexity, and sustaining the rapid evolution of AI. The work covers both sparse (Mixture of Experts) and dense transformer-based LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The exploration of FullFlat optical networks for LLM training is a significant contribution. While the concept of FullFlat networks isn't entirely new in data center design, its systematic evaluation in the context of LLM training, with detailed performance comparisons against traditional two-tier architectures, adds a valuable perspective. Extending the open-source Calculon simulator to support MoE models and dynamic all-to-all routing is also novel, filling a gap in existing performance modeling tools. However, more details on the tool's internal mechanisms and specific implementation of MoE extensions are needed.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a critical challenge: how to build data centers that can efficiently train increasingly large and complex LLMs. The co-design framework that jointly considers compute, memory, networking, and storage is vital for optimizing resource utilization. The findings on the impact of HBD size, scale-out bandwidth, and communication overlap strategies are highly relevant to data center architects and LLM practitioners. The identification of diminishing returns from simply increasing HBD size and the demonstration of FullFlat's ability to enable tensor parallelism beyond node boundaries are crucial insights. The practical implications of lowering TCO and reducing reliance on extensive software optimizations are of significant value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive co-design framework.
    *   Systematic evaluation of FullFlat networks.
    *   Detailed sensitivity analysis of key parameters.
    *   Insights into optimizing communication strategies and hardware collectives.
    *   Comparison of sparse and dense models.
    *   Extension and validation of a performance modeling tool (Calculon).
    *   Clear articulation of required future data center capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on simulations, while necessary, limits the direct applicability of the findings. Real-world experiments on actual hardware would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   While the extension of Calculon is mentioned, the paper lacks specific technical details on the implementation of MoE, dynamic all-to-all routing, and new communication operation components within Calculon. This makes it harder to reproduce the findings or build upon the tool.
    *   The study is primarily focused on training.  The inference aspects of LLM deployment are not discussed, which represents a limitation given the increasing focus on efficient inference.
    *   The paper mentions TCO savings but does not provide a detailed breakdown of the cost model used for this calculation, which weakens the claim.
    *   The evaluation would benefit from a discussion of the limitations introduced by specific hardware choices and how these might impact the broader applicability of the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the design of future AI data centers, particularly for organizations training large LLMs. The insights on network topology, memory requirements, and optimization strategies can guide infrastructure investments and resource allocation decisions. The extended Calculon tool provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners in the field. The insights on how model architecture choices influence optimal system design, such as the trade-offs between sparse and dense models, contribute to the broader understanding of LLM system performance.

* **Rigorous Rationale**: The study is well-structured and uses a validated analytical tool. The strength of the study lies in its methodical, quantitative approach to analyzing numerous configurations. While simulations cannot replace actual hardware experiments, they provide valuable insights for early-stage co-design. The extensions on the simulation tool enable an in-depth consideration of the MoE paradigm. The weaknesses are mainly related to providing further details on the simulation methodology and the actual hardware costs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive analysis of data center design considerations for LLM training. The exploration of FullFlat networks and the extended Calculon tool are valuable assets. However, the reliance on simulations, limited details on the TCO calculations, and lack of discussion around inference constrain the overall impact. Future work involving real-world experiments, detailed cost modeling, and consideration of inference workloads would significantly strengthen the findings. The practical and actionable insights regarding how systems should be architected for training large language models is where the significant contribution of the study lies. The novelty of the work makes an important contribution that warrants a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hyper-Local Deformable Transformers for Text Spotting on Historical Maps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15010v1)
- **Authors**: Yijun Lin, Yao-Yi Chiang
- **Abstract**: Text on historical maps contains valuable information providing georeferenced historical, political, and cultural contexts. However, text extraction from historical maps is challenging due to the lack of (1) effective methods and (2) training data. Previous approaches use ad-hoc steps tailored to only specific map styles. Recent machine learning-based text spotters (e.g., for scene images) have the potential to solve these challenges because of their flexibility in supporting various types of text instances. However, these methods remain challenges in extracting precise image features for predicting every sub-component (boundary points and characters) in a text instance. This is critical because map text can be lengthy and highly rotated with complex backgrounds, posing difficulties in detecting relevant image features from a rough text region. This paper proposes PALETTE, an end-to-end text spotter for scanned historical maps of a wide variety. PALETTE introduces a novel hyper-local sampling module to explicitly learn localized image features around the target boundary points and characters of a text instance for detection and recognition. PALETTE also enables hyper-local positional embeddings to learn spatial interactions between boundary points and characters within and across text instances. In addition, this paper presents a novel approach to automatically generate synthetic map images, SynthMap+, for training text spotters for historical maps. The experiment shows that PALETTE with SynthMap+ outperforms SOTA text spotters on two new benchmark datasets of historical maps, particularly for long and angled text. We have deployed PALETTE with SynthMap+ to process over 60,000 maps in the David Rumsey Historical Map collection and generated over 100 million text labels to support map searching. The project is released at https://github.com/kartta-foundation/mapkurator-palette-doc.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces PALETTE, a novel end-to-end text spotter designed specifically for scanned historical maps. PALETTE uses a hyper-local sampling module to learn localized image features around the boundary points and characters of text instances. The method also incorporates hyper-local positional embeddings to capture spatial interactions. Additionally, the paper presents SYNTHMAP+, a new approach for automatically generating synthetic map images for training. Experiments on new benchmark datasets of historical maps demonstrate PALETTE's superior performance, particularly for long and angled text, compared to state-of-the-art text spotters. The method has been deployed to process a large collection of historical maps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key innovations of the paper are the hyper-local sampling and positional embeddings, which address limitations of existing deformable DETR-based methods when applied to historical maps. The hyper-local approach is more tailored for handling lengthy, curved, and rotated text common in these maps. The creation of the SYNTHMAP+ dataset is another significant contribution, as it tackles the lack of training data tailored for historical map styles. The iterative training procedure to leverage predicted character centers in the absence of ground truth character center annotations in real data is also a clever and valuable technique. While deformable DETR itself isn't new, its adaptation with these components and dataset for historical maps specifically makes the paper novel.

*   **Significance:** The ability to accurately extract text from historical maps has important implications for improving map searchability, metadata generation, and enabling research in various fields. The significant improvements reported over existing methods, especially on challenging text orientations and lengths, demonstrate the practical value of PALETTE. The release of the code, models, and datasets enhances reproducibility and promotes future research in this area.  The fact that this system has been deployed over 60,000 maps is significant, showing the capability of the method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The hyper-local sampling approach is well-motivated and effective in addressing the challenges of historical map text.
    *   The SYNTHMAP+ dataset fills a crucial gap in training resources for this domain.
    *   The iterative training approach alleviates the necessity of character center labels, thereby lessening data annotation costs.
    *   The experiments are thorough, comparing PALETTE to several state-of-the-art methods and including ablation studies to analyze the impact of individual components.
    *   The method has been deployed for processing large map collections.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper mentions that the performance degrades when the text instances are extremely large and characters are far apart, implying the limitations of the method. The paper does not offer solutions and improvements for these issues.
    *   The method still depends on synthetic data and transfer learning. Although SYNTHMAP+ helps, further research might explore unsupervised or self-supervised approaches for historical maps to minimize reliance on labeled data.
    *   While the experiments are comprehensive, a qualitative analysis showing specific examples of failure cases and the impact of the various components could strengthen the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** PALETTE has a high potential to influence the field. It provides a strong baseline for future text spotting research on historical maps. The SYNTHMAP+ dataset can serve as a valuable resource for training and evaluating new methods. The demonstrated improvements in accuracy and robustness, along with the successful deployment, suggest that PALETTE can become a widely adopted tool for processing and analyzing historical map collections.

**Score: 8.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a significant advancement in text spotting for historical maps, effectively addressing the limitations of existing methods and creating valuable resources. The hyper-local sampling and positional embeddings, coupled with the SYNTHMAP+ dataset, demonstrate a clear understanding of the specific challenges posed by historical map text. The iterative training procedure to alleviate reliance on labeled character center locations is a smart solution for reducing annotation burdens. While further improvements are possible, the current results and practical deployment are impressive and indicative of a high-impact contribution. The weaknesses are limitations that can be addressed in future work but do not overshadow the current value of the contributions. The score reflects the significant value, novelty, and potential influence of this research.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### SFT-GO: Supervised Fine-Tuning with Group Optimization for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15021v1)
- **Authors**: Gyuhak Kim, Sumiran Singh Thakur, Su Min Park, Wei Wei, Yujia Bao
- **Abstract**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) has become an essential step in tailoring large language models (LLMs) to align with human expectations and specific downstream tasks. However, existing SFT methods typically treat each training instance as a uniform sequence, giving equal importance to all tokens regardless of their relevance. This overlooks the fact that only a subset of tokens often contains critical, task-specific information. To address this limitation, we introduce Supervised Fine-Tuning with Group Optimization (SFT-GO), a novel approach that treats groups of tokens differently based on their importance.SFT-GO groups tokens in each sample based on their importance values and optimizes the LLM using a weighted combination of the worst-group loss and the standard cross-entropy loss. This mechanism adaptively emphasizes the most challenging token groups and guides the model to better handle different group distributions, thereby improving overall learning dynamics. We provide a theoretical analysis of SFT-GO's convergence rate, demonstrating its efficiency. Empirically, we apply SFT-GO with three different token grouping strategies and show that models trained with SFT-GO consistently outperform baseline approaches across popular LLM benchmarks. These improvements hold across various datasets and base models, demonstrating the robustness and the effectiveness of our method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SFT-GO (Supervised Fine-Tuning with Group Optimization), a novel approach to supervised fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs).  SFT-GO addresses the limitation of existing SFT methods that treat all tokens equally, regardless of their relevance. Instead, it groups tokens based on their importance and optimizes the LLM using a weighted combination of standard cross-entropy loss and a "worst-group loss." This forces the model to focus on token groups it finds most challenging, improving learning dynamics. The paper provides a theoretical analysis demonstrating SFT-GO's convergence efficiency. Empirically, it evaluates SFT-GO with three different token grouping strategies (TF-IDF, LLMLingua-2, and a reference model-based approach like Rho-1) on LIMA and Alpaca datasets, showing consistent outperformance compared to baseline SFT methods across popular LLM benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of assigning different importance to tokens within a sequence during fine-tuning isn't entirely new (e.g., the Rho-1 method the paper compares against). The true novelty lies in:

1.  **The Group Optimization Framework:** SFT-GO offers a *general* framework that allows for flexible grouping based on *various* importance criteria. It's not tied to a specific importance assignment method. It's an optimization scheme *on top* of existing/new importance strategies.
2.  **Worst-Group Loss:** Using the "worst-group loss" concept, inspired by Group DRO, in the *token-level* fine-tuning setting *is* a novel contribution. This incentivizes the model to improve on the subset of tokens where it's performing worst.
3.  **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis demonstrating the convergence properties of SFT-GO, lending more weight to the method's validity.

**Significance:** The potential significance is in:

1.  **Improved Fine-Tuning Efficiency:** By focusing on challenging tokens, SFT-GO could allow for more efficient use of data and compute during fine-tuning.
2.  **Better Model Alignment:**  The framework offers a potential way to better align LLMs with human expectations by emphasizing task-relevant information and reducing the influence of less important tokens. This could lead to more robust and reliable models.
3.  **Adaptability:** SFT-GO's flexibility in defining token groups based on different notions of importance (statistical, semantic, task-specific) makes it adaptable to various tasks and data types.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper effectively articulates the problem of uniform token treatment in standard SFT.
*   **Well-Defined Framework:**  SFT-GO is clearly defined and explained, with appropriate mathematical formalization.
*   **Theoretical Analysis:**  The convergence analysis adds rigor to the proposed approach.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes experiments with different datasets, base models, and token grouping strategies.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the impact of key hyperparameters.
*   **Qualitative Analysis:** The paper provides a qualitative analysis showing which tokens are deemed important by which strategy.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the empirical results show consistent outperformance, the *magnitude* of improvement is sometimes incremental. The increase in the average scores are generally small. The benchmark results could be interpreted as marginal.
*   **Limited Novelty of Token Grouping Strategies:** Some of the token grouping methods used (e.g., TF-IDF) are relatively simple and established. While the paper uses them as examples, the potential of SFT-GO might be greater with more sophisticated grouping methods.
*  **Practicality:** Implementation in production would depend heavily on choosing good clustering mechanisms in a dynamic setting. This might pose a computationally challenging problem.

**Overall:**

SFT-GO presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM fine-tuning. Its novelty lies primarily in the *general* framework and optimization approach, rather than groundbreaking individual components. The empirical evidence supports the effectiveness of SFT-GO, although the magnitude of improvement could be greater. It is relatively well argued and contributes a sensible methodology that could improve the overall fine-tuning of large language models.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** A score of 7 reflects a decent contribution. The *general* group optimization framework with a *worst-group loss* for token-level SFT *is* a valuable advancement. The experimental results show a real, albeit *incremental*, advantage, and the theoretical justification provides a strong argument for the approach. However, there are limitations related to the magnitude of the improvements. Future work with better clustering/grouping strategies and better results may improve the impact of this paper.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Optimal Embedding Learning Rate in LLMs: The Effect of Vocabulary Size
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15025v1)
- **Authors**: Soufiane Hayou, Liyuan Liu
- **Abstract**: Pretraining large language models is a costly process. To make this process more efficient, several methods have been proposed to optimize model architecture/parametrization and hardware use. On the parametrization side, $\mu P$ (Maximal Update Parametrization) parametrizes model weights and learning rate (LR) in a way that makes hyperparameters (HPs) transferable with width (embedding dimension): HPs can be tuned for a small model and used for larger models without additional tuning. While $\mu$P showed impressive results in practice, recent empirical studies have reported conflicting observations when applied to LLMs. One limitation of the theory behind $\mu$P is the fact that input dimension (vocabulary size in LLMs) is considered fixed when taking the width to infinity. This is unrealistic since vocabulary size is generally much larger than width in practice. In this work, we provide a theoretical analysis of the effect of vocabulary size on training dynamics, and subsequently show that as vocabulary size increases, the training dynamics \emph{interpolate between the $\mu$P regime and another regime that we call Large Vocab (LV) Regime}, where optimal scaling rules are different from those predicted by $\mu$P. Our analysis reveals that in the LV regime, the optimal embedding LR to hidden LR ratio should roughly scale as $\Theta(\sqrt{width})$, surprisingly close to the empirical findings previously reported in the literature, and different from the $\Theta(width)$ ratio predicted by $\mu$P. We conduct several experiments to validate our theory, and pretrain a 1B model from scratch to show the benefit of our suggested scaling rule for the embedding LR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of pretraining large language models (LLMs), focusing on how to optimally scale hyperparameters (HPs) – particularly learning rates – with increasing vocabulary size. It critiques the Maximal Update Parametrization (µP) theory, which assumes a fixed vocabulary size when scaling model width, an assumption unrealistic for LLMs. The authors provide a theoretical analysis demonstrating that as vocabulary size increases, the training dynamics shift from the µP regime to a "Large Vocabulary (LV)" regime.  In the LV regime, they theoretically derive and empirically validate a novel scaling rule: the optimal ratio of embedding layer learning rate to hidden layer learning rate should scale roughly as the square root of the model width (√width), in contrast to µP's prediction of a constant ratio or standard practice's approach to same LR. They conduct experiments, including pretraining a 1B model, demonstrating the effectiveness of their proposed scaling rule.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in:
    *   Recognizing the limitations of µP theory when applied to LLMs due to its assumption of fixed vocabulary size. This is a crucial observation, as vocabulary size is often significantly larger than model width in practice and grows with model size.
    *   Providing a theoretical framework that incorporates vocabulary size into the analysis of training dynamics.
    *   Deriving the LV regime scaling rule (LRemb/LRhidden ≈ √width), which contradicts both µP and standard practice and aligns with empirical observations.
    *   Experimentally validating their theory, including a meaningful 1B model pretraining experiment.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from:

    *   **Practical Impact:** The √width scaling rule offers a practical guideline for setting embedding layer learning rates in LLMs, potentially improving training efficiency and performance.
    *   **Theoretical Contribution:** The theoretical framework provides a more nuanced understanding of training dynamics in LLMs, considering the interplay between model width and vocabulary size. This can guide future research on hyperparameter optimization and model scaling.
    *   **Addressing a Gap:**  The work directly addresses a discrepancy between µP theory and empirical observations in LLM pretraining, helping to reconcile conflicting results in the literature.
    *   **Scalability:** The finding regarding the embedding LR to hidden LR ratio, if widely adopted and refined, has the potential to significantly impact the cost and performance of training future, larger LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a limitation of existing theory (µP) and its practical implications for LLMs.
    *   **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The theoretical analysis is well-motivated and provides a plausible explanation for the observed phenomena.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results, including the 1B model pretraining, support the theoretical findings and demonstrate the practical benefits of the proposed scaling rule.
    *   **Well-written and Organized:** The paper is clearly written and organized, making it accessible to researchers in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplified Model:** The theoretical analysis relies on a simplified linear model and SignSGD optimizer. While providing valuable insights, it might not fully capture the complexities of real-world LLM training.
    *   **Assumptions:**  The Zipf-Mandelbrot law assumption, while empirically supported, is still an approximation.  The reliance on specific optimizer normalization techniques is a limiting factor in generality. Further investigation with other more modern optimizers would strengthen the argument.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alternative Parametrizations:** While the paper effectively challenges µP and SP, it could benefit from a more thorough comparison with other emerging parametrization techniques beyond just analyzing those.
    *   **Variance in Optimal Embedding LR:** Figure 3 shows the variance observed in optimal embedding LR, signaling some potential underlying factors the parametrization does not account for. The paper acknowledges it but only calls for a more detailed Section 5 to address those details. Further research to identify such factors could strengthen the framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on hyperparameter optimization and model scaling in LLMs. The LV regime and the √width scaling rule could become important considerations in the development of more efficient and effective training strategies.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a critical gap in the application of µP theory to LLMs. The theoretical analysis and empirical validation provide a strong case for considering vocabulary size when scaling hyperparameters, particularly learning rates. The √width scaling rule offers a practical guideline that can potentially improve LLM training.

However, the limitations of the simplified model, assumptions, and limited exploration of alternate parametrizations prevent it from achieving a higher score. While significant, the paper builds upon existing theory (µP) and validates a specific scaling rule, rather than introducing a fundamentally new paradigm. Therefore, it's not a groundbreaking contribution that completely reshapes the field, it expands and adapts existing knowledge.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Identifying economic narratives in large text corpora -- An integrated approach using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15041v1)
- **Authors**: Tobias Schmidt, Kai-Robin Lange, Matthias Reccius, Henrik Müller, Michael Roos, Carsten Jentsch
- **Abstract**: As interest in economic narratives has grown in recent years, so has the number of pipelines dedicated to extracting such narratives from texts. Pipelines often employ a mix of state-of-the-art natural language processing techniques, such as BERT, to tackle this task. While effective on foundational linguistic operations essential for narrative extraction, such models lack the deeper semantic understanding required to distinguish extracting economic narratives from merely conducting classic tasks like Semantic Role Labeling. Instead of relying on complex model pipelines, we evaluate the benefits of Large Language Models (LLMs) by analyzing a corpus of Wall Street Journal and New York Times newspaper articles about inflation. We apply a rigorous narrative definition and compare GPT-4o outputs to gold-standard narratives produced by expert annotators. Our results suggests that GPT-4o is capable of extracting valid economic narratives in a structured format, but still falls short of expert-level performance when handling complex documents and narratives. Given the novelty of LLMs in economic research, we also provide guidance for future work in economics and the social sciences that employs LLMs to pursue similar objectives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Identifying economic narratives in large text corpora – An integrated approach using Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-40, to automatically extract economic narratives from news articles.  It addresses the growing interest in narrative economics and the need for scalable methods to identify narratives in large text corpora.  The authors argue that traditional NLP methods (e.g., BERT) struggle with the deeper semantic understanding required to identify complex economic narratives.  They propose using LLMs to overcome this limitation. The authors develop a detailed codebook for human annotation of narratives based on a causal definition involving two temporally and semantically distinct events.  They create a gold-standard dataset of narratives from Wall Street Journal and New York Times articles related to inflation,  compare GPT-40's performance against this gold standard, and explore different prompting strategies to optimize LLM performance. Post-processing steps, including event decomposition, valence and topic assignment, and topic normalization, are implemented to enable large-scale abstraction and aggregation of recurring economic patterns. Finally, the paper discusses the potential and limitations of LLM-based narrative analysis and proposes hybrid workflows to combine the scalability of language models with the interpretive capacity of human experts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in directly applying LLMs, specifically GPT-40, to the task of economic narrative extraction, validating the results against a human-annotated gold standard, and evaluating different prompt engineering strategies. While other studies have explored using LLMs for content analysis in social sciences, this work provides a well-structured approach, particularly concerning the development of a clearly defined narrative and comparison against the gold standard. The idea of combining it with scalable methods of abstraction is also promising.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in narrative economics: the need for scalable methods of identifying narratives in large datasets. The findings suggest that LLMs show promise in automating this process. Furthermore, the research highlights the importance of careful prompt engineering and human oversight in ensuring the accuracy and interpretability of LLM-generated results. The work provides guidance for future research in economics and the social sciences employing LLMs for similar objectives.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Definition and Operationalization of Narratives:** The authors develop a rigorous and well-defined operationalization of economic narratives based on causality and semantic distinction, that also mirrors theoretical concepts like DAGs.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation Methodology:** The comparison against a gold-standard dataset, consideration of inter-annotator agreement, and analysis of different types of errors (major vs. minor) provides a robust assessment of LLM performance.
*   **Detailed Prompt Engineering Exploration:** The discussion of various prompting strategies (zero-shot, few-shot, Chain-of-Thought) provides practical insights for researchers working with LLMs.
*   **Post-processing Pipeline:** The described post-processing steps for narrative abstraction and event clustering are a valuable contribution, offering potential avenues for aggregating and analyzing extracted narratives.
*   **Balanced Discussion of Limitations:** The paper honestly acknowledges the limitations of LLMs, including biases, hallucinations, and difficulties with complex narrative structures, and emphasizes the importance of human oversight.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The evaluation is restricted to inflation-related narratives from a single corpus (WSJ and NYT). The performance may vary across different topics, text types, or data sets.
*   **Dependence on a Closed-Source Model:** The reliance on GPT-40 makes the research less reproducible due to the model's proprietary nature and potential future unavailability. While acknowledged, this is a significant limitation.
*   **Modest Overall Performance:** Despite the advanced techniques, the overall accuracy of 44% suggests that significant improvements are needed for LLMs to be reliably used for narrative extraction in economics, which undermines the study's findings.
*   **Lack of deeper economic modeling:** Despite the use of an LLM, the extracted narratives are not related to any economic phenomena, other than some initial indications that government spending and rising inflation are related.

**Justification of Score:**

Despite the promising potential of LLMs and the well-defined methodology of this paper, there are limitations that must be considered. The results of the analysis against the gold standard dataset undermine confidence in the ability of LLMs to extract narratives consistently. Therefore, while the paper represents a valuable and timely contribution, particularly in its exploration of prompting strategies, its practical impact on the field is currently constrained. More research is warranted, however.

### Mapping Caregiver Needs to AI Chatbot Design: Strengths and Gaps in Mental Health Support for Alzheimer's and Dementia Caregivers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15047v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayue Melissa Shi, Dong Whi Yoo, Keran Wang, Violeta J. Rodriguez, Ravi Karkar, Koustuv Saha
- **Abstract**: Family caregivers of individuals with Alzheimer's Disease and Related Dementia (AD/ADRD) face significant emotional and logistical challenges that place them at heightened risk for stress, anxiety, and depression. Although recent advances in generative AI -- particularly large language models (LLMs) -- offer new opportunities to support mental health, little is known about how caregivers perceive and engage with such technologies. To address this gap, we developed Carey, a GPT-4o-based chatbot designed to provide informational and emotional support to AD/ADRD caregivers. Using Carey as a technology probe, we conducted semi-structured interviews with 16 family caregivers following scenario-driven interactions grounded in common caregiving stressors. Through inductive coding and reflexive thematic analysis, we surface a systemic understanding of caregiver needs and expectations across six themes -- on-demand information access, emotional support, safe space for disclosure, crisis management, personalization, and data privacy. For each of these themes, we also identified the nuanced tensions in the caregivers' desires and concerns. We present a mapping of caregiver needs, AI chatbot's strengths, gaps, and design recommendations. Our findings offer theoretical and practical insights to inform the design of proactive, trustworthy, and caregiver-centered AI systems that better support the evolving mental health needs of AD/ADRD caregivers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the needs and expectations of family caregivers of individuals with Alzheimer's Disease and Related Dementias (AD/ADRD) when interacting with an AI chatbot designed to provide mental health support. The authors developed "Carey," a GPT-4.0-based chatbot, and conducted semi-structured interviews with 16 caregivers who used the chatbot in scenario-based interactions related to common caregiving stressors. The analysis identified six key themes: on-demand information access, emotional support, a safe space for disclosure, crisis management, personalization, and data privacy.  For each theme, the paper maps the chatbot's strengths, gaps, and provides design recommendations for future AI systems. The findings highlight the importance of proactive, trustworthy, and caregiver-centered AI tools that are sensitive to the emotional and informational complexities of caregiving.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant and Timely Topic:** The increasing prevalence of AD/ADRD and the significant burden placed on family caregivers make this a vital area of research.  Exploring the potential of AI, especially LLMs, to provide scalable support is timely and important.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a well-defined mixed-methods approach, combining scenario-based interactions with semi-structured interviews and reflexive thematic analysis.  This allows for both a structured exploration of caregiver needs and a nuanced understanding of their experiences.
    *   **Clear Identification of Needs and Gaps:** The paper effectively identifies the key needs of caregivers and critically assesses the strengths and weaknesses of the AI chatbot in meeting those needs.  The thematic analysis is thorough and well-supported by quotes from the interviews.
    *   **Practical Design Recommendations:** The paper moves beyond simply identifying problems and offers concrete design recommendations for improving future AI systems for caregiver support.  These recommendations are grounded in the empirical findings and offer valuable guidance for researchers and developers.
    *   **Focus on Ethical Considerations:** The discussion of privacy concerns, potential biases, and the risk of over-reliance on AI highlights the ethical complexities of using these technologies to support vulnerable populations. This is crucial for responsible innovation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Small Sample Size:** While the methodological approach is rigorous, the sample size of 16 participants is relatively small. This limits the generalizability of the findings to the broader population of AD/ADRD caregivers.
    *   **Limited Generalizability of Carey:** While the findings provide useful insights, the specific strengths and weaknesses identified are tied to the Carey chatbot and its implementation. There is room for further research to examine different AI technologies and LLM variations. The selection and design of Carey as a technology probe is clearly described but may have introduced biases or artifacts.
    *   **Lack of Quantitative Outcome Measures:** The study relies primarily on qualitative data. The inclusion of quantitative measures of caregiver well-being, stress levels, or satisfaction with the chatbot could have strengthened the findings.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Individual Differences:** While the paper acknowledges the heterogeneity of caregivers, it could have further explored how factors such as the caregiver's age, gender, ethnicity, or caregiving experience influenced their needs and expectations for AI support.
    *   **The scenarios as design probe**: While the work has provided scenarios to guide participants in interacting with Carey as a design probe, it does not explicitly detail the process of refining or iterating on the scenarios based on preliminary participant feedback. Such an iterative process could have led to even more nuanced and relevant scenarios, further enriching the data and strengthening the findings.
    *   **Descriptive vs Predictive**: The study presents a descriptive analysis of caregivers' interactions with Carey, and it offers insightful observations about their needs and expectations. However, it would be valuable to expand on this by developing predictive models or frameworks. For instance, the current scenarios have been created based on literature - could they be used to predict which types of caregivers are more likely to benefit from different AI support scenarios? What are the specific user characteristics and scenarios where the use of AI tools is most effective in reducing caregiver burden or improving mental well-being?

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper contributes significantly to the growing body of research on AI for mental health by focusing specifically on the unique needs and challenges of AD/ADRD caregivers. It offers a practical and ethically informed framework for designing AI tools that can better support this vulnerable population. The emphasis on understanding the nuances of caregiver experiences and the potential for both benefits and harms is particularly valuable.
*   **Potential Influence:**  This work can inform future research on AI-based caregiver support, guide the development of more effective and ethical AI tools, and influence clinical practice by raising awareness of the potential benefits and limitations of using these technologies to support family caregivers. The paper also highlights the need for a more integrated approach to caregiver support, combining AI tools with existing healthcare resources and human support networks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents valuable insights into a relevant and important area of research. The rigorous methodology, clear identification of needs and gaps, and practical design recommendations are significant strengths. However, the small sample size and reliance on a single chatbot limit the generalizability of the findings. While it does not offer breakthrough theoretical advances, it makes a valuable contribution to the field.

### Truncated Proximal Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15050v1)
- **Authors**: Tiantian Fan, Lingjun Liu, Yu Yue, Jiaze Chen, Chengyi Wang, Qiying Yu, Chi Zhang, Zhiqi Lin, Ruofei Zhu, Yufeng Yuan, Xiaochen Zuo, Bole Ma, Mofan Zhang, Gaohong Liu, Ru Zhang, Haotian Zhou, Cong Xie, Ruidong Zhu, Zhi Zhang, Xin Liu, Mingxuan Wang, Lin Yan, Yonghui Wu
- **Abstract**: Recently, test-time scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated exceptional reasoning capabilities across scientific and professional tasks by generating long chains-of-thought (CoT). As a crucial component for developing these reasoning models, reinforcement learning (RL), exemplified by Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and its variants, allows models to learn through trial and error. However, PPO can be time-consuming due to its inherent on-policy nature, which is further exacerbated by increasing response lengths. In this work, we propose Truncated Proximal Policy Optimization (T-PPO), a novel extension to PPO that improves training efficiency by streamlining policy update and length-restricted response generation. T-PPO mitigates the issue of low hardware utilization, an inherent drawback of fully synchronized long-generation procedures, where resources often sit idle during the waiting periods for complete rollouts. Our contributions are two-folds. First, we propose Extended Generalized Advantage Estimation (EGAE) for advantage estimation derived from incomplete responses while maintaining the integrity of policy learning. Second, we devise a computationally optimized mechanism that allows for the independent optimization of the policy and value models. By selectively filtering prompt and truncated tokens, this mechanism reduces redundant computations and accelerates the training process without sacrificing convergence performance. We demonstrate the effectiveness and efficacy of T-PPO on AIME 2024 with a 32B base model. The experimental results show that T-PPO improves the training efficiency of reasoning LLMs by up to 2.5x and outperforms its existing competitors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Truncated Proximal Policy Optimization (T-PPO), a novel extension to the standard Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) algorithm designed to improve training efficiency when refining Large Language Models (LLMs) for reasoning tasks. T-PPO addresses the inefficiencies of PPO caused by long chains-of-thought (CoT) trajectories. The key innovations of T-PPO include: (1) Extended Generalized Advantage Estimation (EGAE) to allow for advantage computation from partially generated responses and progressive policy updates; (2) a token-level optimization strategy that filters training tokens for policy and value model updates, promoting computational efficiency and independent updates; and (3) A batching approach to accelerate the use of GPUs. Experimental results on the AIME 2024 benchmark demonstrate that T-PPO improves training efficiency by up to 2.5x while maintaining or improving performance compared to existing methods. The paper also presents a Roofline analysis to illustrate improved hardware utilization and analyzes training dynamics by examining response length evolution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a relevant problem:** Training LLMs for complex reasoning is computationally expensive, and improving efficiency is crucial for practical deployment. T-PPO directly tackles this problem.
*   **Clear and well-structured approach:** The paper clearly explains the limitations of PPO in the context of long CoT trajectories and presents a well-defined solution with EGAE and token-level optimization.
*   **Strong experimental results:** The empirical evaluation on the AIME 2024 benchmark shows significant efficiency gains (up to 2.5x) without sacrificing performance. The comparison against several baselines (e.g., VAPO, PPO-EWMA) strengthens the claims. The Roofline analysis provides valuable insights into the hardware utilization improvements.
*   **Sound theoretical basis:**  The EGAE maintains the integrity of policy learning using partial responses.
*   **Thorough analysis:** The training dynamics analysis (response length evolution) provides a deeper understanding of how T-PPO refines reasoning strategies.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scope of evaluation:** While the AIME 2024 results are promising, the evaluation is primarily limited to one benchmark. Testing on a broader range of reasoning tasks and LLM architectures would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Marginal novelty in individual components:**  While the combination is novel, the individual components like value function optimization have been addressed previously. The real innovation lies in how these elements are integrated and optimized for the specific problem of reasoning LLM training.
*   **Dependency on reward function:** The reward function design relies heavily on the assumption that rewards are non-zero only at the end of the response. Though the paper states the method can be easily applied to simpler scenarios, more details on how this would practically affect T-PPO's design would strengthen the paper.
*   **Lack of ablation studies:** The paper would benefit from ablations to isolate the individual contributions of the EGAE and token-level optimization components to better understand their impact on performance and efficiency. What happens if either one is removed?

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in providing a practical and efficient method for training reasoning LLMs. The 2.5x speedup can significantly reduce training costs and enable wider adoption of RL-based refinement techniques. Although the individual components may not be groundbreaking, the combination and the specific optimizations tailored for long CoT trajectories make T-PPO a valuable contribution.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates a clear improvement in training efficiency for reasoning LLMs, backed by solid experimental results and a good theoretical basis.  While the reliance on a single benchmark, marginal novelty in the individual techniques used, and lack of ablation studies limit its impact, the T-PPO algorithm offers a practical and efficient approach to tackling an increasingly important problem.  The efficiency gains are not incremental, they are substantial. This method is very likely to impact the way reasoning-based LLMs are refined.

### HEAL: An Empirical Study on Hallucinations in Embodied Agents Driven by Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15065v1)
- **Authors**: Trishna Chakraborty, Udita Ghosh, Xiaopan Zhang, Fahim Faisal Niloy, Yue Dong, Jiachen Li, Amit K. Roy-Chowdhury, Chengyu Song
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being adopted as the cognitive core of embodied agents. However, inherited hallucinations, which stem from failures to ground user instructions in the observed physical environment, can lead to navigation errors, such as searching for a refrigerator that does not exist. In this paper, we present the first systematic study of hallucinations in LLM-based embodied agents performing long-horizon tasks under scene-task inconsistencies. Our goal is to understand to what extent hallucinations occur, what types of inconsistencies trigger them, and how current models respond. To achieve these goals, we construct a hallucination probing set by building on an existing benchmark, capable of inducing hallucination rates up to 40x higher than base prompts. Evaluating 12 models across two simulation environments, we find that while models exhibit reasoning, they fail to resolve scene-task inconsistencies-highlighting fundamental limitations in handling infeasible tasks. We also provide actionable insights on ideal model behavior for each scenario, offering guidance for developing more robust and reliable planning strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HEAL: An Empirical Study on Hallucinations in Embodied Agents Driven by Large Language Models" investigates the prevalence and causes of hallucinations in LLM-based embodied agents performing long-horizon tasks. The authors create a hallucination probing set by systematically modifying a benchmark dataset to introduce scene-task inconsistencies. They evaluate 12 LLMs across two simulation environments, finding that models struggle to resolve these inconsistencies, often hallucinating objects or states to complete tasks, even when it leads to unsafe or impossible actions. They identify scene task contradiction as being particularly challenging. The paper also explores mitigation strategies and the impact of cross-modal information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic approach to studying hallucinations in the *embodied agent* context. While hallucinations in LLMs are a well-researched topic, this work specifically examines their manifestation and consequences in agents acting within a simulated environment. The construction of a probing set designed to induce hallucinations related to scene-task inconsistencies is another valuable contribution. The systematic controlled modifications of the task description and the environment is relatively new. Prior work focused on more general or incidental hallucination cases.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is relatively strong. LLMs are increasingly used in robotics and embodied AI, making it crucial to understand and address their limitations, especially hallucinations, which can lead to real-world problems (safety hazards, task failures, etc.). By identifying the specific types of inconsistencies that trigger hallucinations, the paper provides actionable insights for developing more robust and reliable planning strategies for these agents. The study's insights around failure patterns is vital towards robust planning strategies. The finding that the models are unable to reject infeasible tasks but still try to carry them out by repurposing available objects indicates that the models require fundamental algorithmic change towards rejecting infeasible tasks instead of trying to repurpose the available objects.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The use of controlled modifications (distractor injection, object removal, etc.) allows for a clear identification of triggers.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Evaluation of several models provides a broader understanding of the landscape.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The findings offer guidance on ideal model behavior and the types of reasoning models are failing at.
    *   **Focus on a Critical Application Area:** Embodied AI applications carry inherent risks if not properly addressed.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulation Limitations:** The study is limited to simulation environments. While valuable, it's important to note that the complexity and unpredictability of the real world may introduce new challenges.
    *   **Mitigation Strategies:** The mitigation strategies that were implemented were not too strong. Hallucination rates remain high which undermines the mitigation strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the following:
    *   **Dataset Creation:** The hallucination probing set can serve as a benchmark for future research.
    *   **Model Development:** The identified failure modes can inform the design of new architectures or training strategies to improve grounding and consistency.
    *   **System Design:** The findings can guide the development of more robust planning strategies that incorporate error detection and recovery mechanisms.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper’s novelty lies in its focused analysis of hallucinations in *embodied agents*, particularly under scene-task inconsistencies. The systematic approach, comprehensive evaluation, and actionable insights contribute significantly to the field. However, the limited scope of simulation and the simplicity of the proposed mitigation approaches limit the paper's overall impact. The use of controlled modification allows for clear identification of triggers which also serves as a solid point for future research. The actionable insights of failure modes can also inform the design of new architectures of training strategies to improve consistency and grounding.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a strong contribution to a growing field, providing valuable insights into a critical problem. The systematic design and comprehensive evaluation are commendable. However, the limited scope and simulation environment prevent it from achieving a higher score. More advanced mitigation techniques will be better for more generalizability to real world tasks.
