### HeurAgenix: Leveraging LLMs for Solving Complex Combinatorial Optimization Challenges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15196v1)
- **Authors**: Xianliang Yang, Ling Zhang, Haolong Qian, Lei Song, Jiang Bian
- **Abstract**: Heuristic algorithms play a vital role in solving combinatorial optimization (CO) problems, yet traditional designs depend heavily on manual expertise and struggle to generalize across diverse instances. We introduce \textbf{HeurAgenix}, a two-stage hyper-heuristic framework powered by large language models (LLMs) that first evolves heuristics and then selects among them automatically. In the heuristic evolution phase, HeurAgenix leverages an LLM to compare seed heuristic solutions with higher-quality solutions and extract reusable evolution strategies. During problem solving, it dynamically picks the most promising heuristic for each problem state, guided by the LLM's perception ability. For flexibility, this selector can be either a state-of-the-art LLM or a fine-tuned lightweight model with lower inference cost. To mitigate the scarcity of reliable supervision caused by CO complexity, we fine-tune the lightweight heuristic selector with a dual-reward mechanism that jointly exploits singals from selection preferences and state perception, enabling robust selection under noisy annotations. Extensive experiments on canonical benchmarks show that HeurAgenix not only outperforms existing LLM-based hyper-heuristics but also matches or exceeds specialized solvers. Code is available at https://github.com/microsoft/HeurAgenix.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HeurAgenix, a novel two-stage hyper-heuristic framework leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to solve complex combinatorial optimization (CO) problems.  The first stage, heuristic evolution, uses an LLM to analyze and refine existing heuristics by comparing solutions and extracting evolution strategies. The second stage, problem-solving, dynamically selects the most promising heuristic for each problem state, guided by the LLM's understanding. To improve robustness, the paper fine-tunes a lightweight LLM-based heuristic selector with a dual-reward mechanism that uses both context perception and outcome preferences. The authors demonstrate through experiments on standard benchmarks that HeurAgenix outperforms other LLM-based approaches and often matches or exceeds the performance of specialized solvers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper introduces several novel aspects:
    *   A fully autonomous and data-driven approach to heuristic evolution for CO problems, avoiding reliance on external solvers or manual rule design.
    *   The integration of an LLM-based adaptive heuristic selector for instance-level adaptation, a key differentiator from existing methods.
    *   The dual-reward mechanism for robust fine-tuning of the heuristic selector, addressing challenges related to noisy data in CO.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the two-stage approach is novel, the individual components (LLM-based heuristic generation and selection) have been explored in isolation in previous works. The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination and refinement of these ideas.
    *   The reliance on proprietary LLMs (GPT-4) for core components introduces potential limitations regarding reproducibility and accessibility for the broader research community.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper offers a significant contribution to the field by:
    *   Pushing the boundaries of automatic heuristic design, demonstrating the potential of LLMs to create effective solvers without requiring extensive domain expertise.
    *   Addressing the generalization problem in CO by developing a framework adaptable to different problem instances and evolving problem states.
    *   Providing a flexible and efficient approach that can leverage both powerful frontier LLMs and fine-tuned lightweight models depending on the specific application requirements.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper primarily focuses on classical CO problems.  The broader applicability and scalability of HeurAgenix to more complex real-world scenarios (e.g., those involving constraints or dynamic environments) remains to be fully explored.
    *   While the experiments demonstrate strong performance, the computational cost associated with LLM-based heuristic evolution and selection may still be a concern for certain applications.
    *   The analysis of the evolved heuristics themselves is somewhat limited. A deeper understanding of the strategies discovered by the LLM and their underlying rationale would further enhance the paper's contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

HeurAgenix presents a novel combination of LLM capabilities for automated heuristic design and adaptive selection, addressing key limitations in existing hyper-heuristic approaches. While the reliance on proprietary models and the limited exploration of real-world applications represent minor weaknesses, the paper makes a significant contribution by demonstrating a fully autonomous, data-driven approach that can achieve state-of-the-art performance on several CO benchmarks. The dual-reward mechanism is also a notable contribution for enhancing robustness in the face of noisy evaluations, common to complex optimization settings.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Comparative Study of Task Adaptation Techniques of Large Language Models for Identifying Sustainable Development Goals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15208v1)
- **Authors**: Andrea Cadeddu, Alessandro Chessa, Vincenzo De Leo, Gianni Fenu, Enrico Motta, Francesco Osborne, Diego Reforgiato Recupero, Angelo Salatino, Luca Secchi
- **Abstract**: In 2012, the United Nations introduced 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) aimed at creating a more sustainable and improved future by 2030. However, tracking progress toward these goals is difficult because of the extensive scale and complexity of the data involved. Text classification models have become vital tools in this area, automating the analysis of vast amounts of text from a variety of sources. Additionally, large language models (LLMs) have recently proven indispensable for many natural language processing tasks, including text classification, thanks to their ability to recognize complex linguistic patterns and semantics. This study analyzes various proprietary and open-source LLMs for a single-label, multi-class text classification task focused on the SDGs. Then, it also evaluates the effectiveness of task adaptation techniques (i.e., in-context learning approaches), namely Zero-Shot and Few-Shot Learning, as well as Fine-Tuning within this domain. The results reveal that smaller models, when optimized through prompt engineering, can perform on par with larger models like OpenAI's GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative study of various Large Language Models (LLMs) – both proprietary and open-source – for the task of classifying text data related to the UN's Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The authors create a benchmark dataset from the OSDG community dataset and evaluate LLMs using zero-shot learning, few-shot learning, and fine-tuning techniques. The study examines the impact of different sample selection strategies in few-shot learning (random, semantic similarity, and semantic similarity with class diversity) and assesses the effectiveness of model quantization.  The main findings are that smaller models, particularly when fine-tuned with high-quality data or optimized with prompt engineering, can achieve performance comparable to larger models like GPT-3.5, emphasizing the potential of resource-efficient approaches for SDG-related text classification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Benchmark Creation:** The creation of a specific benchmark dataset for SDG text classification is valuable and facilitates future research in the area. It provides a standardized evaluation platform. The usage of pre-existing OSDG data to create the benchmark is a sound methodology.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper offers a reasonably comprehensive comparison of a range of LLMs, including recent models like LLaMa 3 and Phi-3.
    * **Task Adaptation Techniques:** The inclusion of Zero-Shot Learning (ZSL), Few-Shot Learning (FSL) with various selection strategies, and Fine-Tuning (FT) allows for a detailed understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of each approach in the context of SDG classification.
    * **Quantization Analysis:** The assessment of quantized models adds practical value, highlighting the potential for deploying these models in resource-constrained environments.
    * **Demonstrates Resource Efficiency:** The key finding that smaller models can compete with larger ones when properly optimized is a significant contribution, promoting more accessible and sustainable AI solutions for SDG monitoring.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty in Methods:** The techniques used (zero-shot, few-shot, fine-tuning, quantization) are well-established in the LLM literature. The novelty lies primarily in their application to the specific problem of SDG text classification and the comparative analysis.
    * **Dataset limitations:** The dataset, while useful, is based on existing data that may reflect existing biases and assumptions in SDG labeling. The manual verification steps help, but inherent biases could still influence the results. The description of the dataset balancing is also sparse and could use further articulation.
    * **Prompt Engineering details:**  While the prompts are available online, a more thorough discussion of the prompt engineering process (e.g., specific variations tested and rationale for the final choices) would strengthen the paper.  The quality of prompts significantly impacts the outcome.
    * **Metric Interpretation:** The paper uses macro-averaged metrics, which are generally suitable for multi-class classification. However, a discussion on the performance variations between different SDGs would provide deeper insights. Some SDGs could be easier to classify than others.
    * **Limited Analysis of Errors:** The paper lacks in-depth error analysis. Understanding the types of errors that each model makes could lead to better model selection and task adaptation strategies in the future. Qualitative examples would be useful.
    * **Focus on technical performance, limited discussion of practical implications:** While the study demonstrates feasibility and relative performance, there is limited discussion of how these systems could be practically integrated into real-world SDG monitoring workflows or what policy implications might arise from adopting different LLM-based solutions. The practical utility of a 92% vs. 85% accurate SDG classifier should be further explored.

* **Significance:**
    * The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: automating the monitoring of SDGs, which is critical for achieving sustainability goals.
    * The findings provide practical guidance for researchers and practitioners working in this area, helping them to select appropriate LLMs and task adaptation techniques for SDG text classification based on resource constraints and desired performance levels.
    * The benchmark dataset contributes to the field, fostering future research and development in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a useful contribution by providing a practical evaluation of different LLMs for SDG text classification. While the methodology is not particularly novel, the comprehensive comparison and the finding about the effectiveness of smaller models when optimized, coupled with the publicly available benchmark, provide valuable insights. The limitations discussed (dataset biases, lack of prompt engineering detail, limited error analysis, limited discussion of practical integration) hold the paper back from achieving a higher score. The lack of error analysis specifically limits the insights on why each model performs as it does and what the potential risks might be.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ProtoReasoning: Prototypes as the Foundation for Generalizable Reasoning in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15211v1)
- **Authors**: Feng He, Zijun Chen, Xinnian Liang, Tingting Ma, Yunqi Qiu, Shuangzhi Wu, Junchi Yan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) trained with Long Chain-of-Thought (Long CoT) reasoning have demonstrated remarkable cross-domain generalization capabilities. However, the underlying mechanisms supporting such transfer remain poorly understood. We hypothesize that cross-domain generalization arises from shared abstract reasoning prototypes -- fundamental reasoning patterns that capture the essence of problems across domains. These prototypes minimize the nuances of the representation, revealing that seemingly diverse tasks are grounded in shared reasoning structures.Based on this hypothesis, we propose ProtoReasoning, a framework that enhances the reasoning ability of LLMs by leveraging scalable and verifiable prototypical representations (Prolog for logical reasoning, PDDL for planning).ProtoReasoning features: (1) an automated prototype construction pipeline that transforms problems into corresponding prototype representations; (2) a comprehensive verification system providing reliable feedback through Prolog/PDDL interpreters; (3) the scalability to synthesize problems arbitrarily within prototype space while ensuring correctness. Extensive experiments show that ProtoReasoning achieves 4.7% improvement over baseline models on logical reasoning (Enigmata-Eval), 6.3% improvement on planning tasks, 4.0% improvement on general reasoning (MMLU) and 1.0% on mathematics (AIME24). Significantly, our ablation studies confirm that learning in prototype space also demonstrates enhanced generalization to structurally similar problems compared to training solely on natural language representations, validating our hypothesis that reasoning prototypes serve as the foundation for generalizable reasoning in large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ProtoReasoning: Prototypes as the Foundation for Generalizable Reasoning in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "ProtoReasoning," a framework designed to improve the general reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is that cross-domain generalization arises from shared, abstract reasoning prototypes that capture the essence of problems across domains. The framework uses prototype representations – Prolog for logical reasoning and PDDL for planning – combined with automated prototype construction, a verification system, and scalability features.  The authors demonstrate empirically that training LLMs with ProtoReasoning leads to improvements in logical reasoning, planning, general reasoning, and mathematical problem-solving benchmarks. Ablation studies support the hypothesis that training in prototype space enhances generalization to structurally similar problems compared to training solely on natural language representations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Hypothesis:** The hypothesis that abstract reasoning prototypes underlie cross-domain generalization in LLMs is interesting and potentially valuable.  It provides a possible explanation for the surprising transfer learning abilities observed in some LLMs.

*   **Well-Defined Framework:**  ProtoReasoning offers a concrete framework with clear components (prototype construction, verification, training).  The use of Prolog and PDDL is motivated by their declarative nature, expressiveness, and verifiability.

*   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper presents compelling experimental results showing improvements on several standard benchmarks (Enigmata-Eval, MMLU, AIME24) and planning tasks. The improvements, while not massive, are consistent across multiple domains. Ablation studies add further weight to the claims.

*   **Scalability:** The approach aims at scalability by leveraging prototype systems with built-in verification, reducing the need for human annotation and allowing for automatic problem generation. This is a significant advantage.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is primarily focused on Prolog and PDDL.  While these are well-established representations, the framework's effectiveness with other types of reasoning prototypes is unclear.  The generalizability of ProtoReasoning to domains lacking well-defined formal languages is not thoroughly addressed.

*   **Incremental Improvements:** While statistically significant, the performance improvements are somewhat incremental. The results don't represent a breakthrough in LLM reasoning. This suggests that while the concept is promising, its implementation might need further refinement.

*   **Prototype Definition:** The definition of "reasoning prototypes" remains somewhat vague. The paper would benefit from a more formal and precise characterization of what constitutes a "reasoning prototype," making it easier to extend the framework to other domains and understand its theoretical underpinnings. What makes a good prototype representation?

*   **MoE Architecture:** The paper utilizes a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture, which is known to perform better than standard architectures. This brings into question whether ProtoReasoning provides improvements on its own or if the MoE architecture is responsible for the observed improvements.

*   **Dataset Generation:** Dataset generation relies on LLMs and can be prone to bias or artifacts. How was the quality of generated datasets assessed beyond just the basic verification steps? The impact of dataset quality on ProtoReasoning performance needs further investigation.

*   **Clarity on "Structurally Similar Problems":** While the ablation study aims to show enhanced generalization to structurally similar problems, the definition of "structurally similar" is not rigorously defined. More quantitative metrics or examples would strengthen this claim.

* **Novelty in Applying Formal Languages:** The idea of using formal languages isn't necessarily *new*. The novelty is in applying it at this scale, and in combining it with RLVR.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its attempt to provide a principled explanation for cross-domain generalization in LLMs and in offering a concrete framework to leverage this phenomenon. It also points to the importance of using more structured or symbolic representations to enhance reasoning abilities. However, the limited scope and incremental improvements necessitate further research to fully realize the potential of the proposed approach.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

Here's my rationale:

*   **+2 for Novelty:**  The core hypothesis about abstract reasoning prototypes and the ProtoReasoning framework is a novel contribution to understanding and improving LLM reasoning.

*   **+2 for Technical Soundness:** The framework is well-defined, and the experiments are reasonably well-designed. The use of Prolog and PDDL and the verification systems are appropriate.

*   **+1 for Empirical Results:** The improvements on standard benchmarks provide solid evidence to support the claims. The ablation studies are also important.

*   **-1 for Limited Scope:** The focus on Prolog and PDDL limits the generalizability of the findings. The absence of more diverse reasoning prototypes is a significant weakness.

*   **-1 for Incremental Improvements:**  The performance gains are not game-changing. While consistent, the improvements are not substantial enough to warrant a higher score.

*   **+1 for Scalability:** The focus on automated data generation through formal methods is a significant strength, hinting towards improved scalability compared to purely human-annotated training.

*   **-1 for Prototype Definition:** Lack of rigorous definition for "reasoning prototype" and "structurally similar problem"

The paper provides a valuable contribution by exploring the idea of abstract reasoning prototypes. However, the limitations and ambiguities in prototype definition, scope, and potential improvements prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. It offers a promising starting point for further research in this direction.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM vs. SAST: A Technical Analysis on Detecting Coding Bugs of GPT4-Advanced Data Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15212v1)
- **Authors**: Madjid G. Tehrani, Eldar Sultanow, William J. Buchanan, Mahkame Houmani, Christel H. Djaha Fodja
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancements in Natural Language Processing (NLP), large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 have gained significant traction in diverse applications, including security vulnerability scanning. This paper investigates the efficacy of GPT-4 in identifying software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools. Drawing from an array of security mistakes, our analysis underscores the potent capabilities of GPT-4 in LLM-enhanced vulnerability scanning. We unveiled that GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis) outperforms SAST by an accuracy of 94% in detecting 32 types of exploitable vulnerabilities. This study also addresses the potential security concerns surrounding LLMs, emphasising the imperative of security by design/default and other security best practices for AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of the GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis) model in detecting software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools.  The authors tested GPT-4 and two SAST tools (SonarQube and Cloud Defence) against a set of 32 known security vulnerabilities. The results suggest that GPT-4 outperforms SAST tools in identifying these vulnerabilities with a higher accuracy (around 94%). The paper also discusses potential security concerns related to the use of LLMs and emphasizes the importance of secure-by-design principles and security best practices. Finally, it proposes future research directions, including the integration of LLMs into development environments and the creation of security-focused LLMs trained with Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computers (FTQC).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Topical Relevance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and timely issue: the application of large language models to cybersecurity, specifically vulnerability detection. As LLMs like GPT-4 become more capable, understanding their potential and limitations in security is crucial.
    * **Clear Methodology:** The paper outlines a clear methodology for comparing GPT-4 with SAST tools, including the selection of vulnerabilities, code samples, and evaluation metrics. The use of the Chi-Squared Test for Independence and McNemar's test adds rigor to the analysis.
    * **Comprehensive Discussion:** The paper comprehensively discusses potential benefits of LLMs in vulnerability detection, such as the evolution of detection tools, cost implications, and time efficiency. It also identifies security risks associated with LLMs, covering a wide range of attacks, and providing a detailed listing.
    * **Well-articulated Future Research Directions:** The paper proposes relevant and actionable future research areas, including broader SAST tool comparisons, real-world usability testing, integration with development environments, and the development of security-trained LLMs. The mention of LLMs-trained using FTQCs is forward looking.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Scope of SAST Tools:**  The comparison is limited to only two SAST tools, which may not be representative of the entire SAST landscape.  Different SAST tools have varying strengths and weaknesses, and a broader selection would provide a more robust evaluation.
    * **Dataset Size and Representativeness:** While 32 vulnerabilities are tested, the paper doesn't explicitly describe the size or complexity of the code snippets used for each vulnerability. If the code snippets are relatively simple or synthetic, the results may not generalize well to real-world applications.  Additionally, how well do these 32 vulnerabilities truly represent all vulnerabilities, in terms of occurrence and severity in real world applications?
    * **Lack of Details on GPT-4 Configuration and Prompting:** The paper mentions using GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis), but provides limited detail on the specific configuration used.  Prompt engineering plays a critical role in LLM performance, and the authors don't describe the prompts used to elicit vulnerability detection from GPT-4. Without this transparency, the results are difficult to reproduce or compare with other studies.  It is noted that there is no API pricing model known, and the analysis occurred during a beta timeframe.
    * **Potential for Bias:** The paper authors seem predisposed towards the conclusion that GPT-4 out-performs traditional SAST. Some statements and conclusions aren't explicitly backed by data or empirical reasoning but suggest bias.
    * **Limited Novelty:** The core finding that LLMs can potentially identify vulnerabilities is not particularly novel. Many other recent papers have explored similar themes. The novelty in this paper lies more in the direct comparison with SAST tools and the exploration of LLM security concerns.
    * **Generalization Concerns:**  It's unclear how well the findings generalize to other LLMs or to different types of software projects.
    * **No API Pricing/Token Size Details**: The lack of information regarding the LLM's specifics is a major limitation.
    * **"SAST-Correct" Definition:** This metric could result in issues, because it relies on an OR function. What if a tool identifies an issue, but it's a false-positive, will it be labelled as "SAST-Correct"? How were false positives ruled out by the authors?
* **Significance:**

    * The paper contributes to the growing body of research on the application of LLMs in cybersecurity. It provides empirical evidence suggesting that GPT-4 can be a valuable tool for vulnerability detection, potentially complementing or even surpassing traditional SAST tools.
    * The paper's discussion of LLM security concerns and the need for secure-by-design principles is highly relevant and can inform the development of more secure AI-powered security tools.
    * The proposed future research directions can guide further investigations into the use of LLMs in software security.

**Score:** 6/10

**Justification:**

The paper has topical relevance and contributes to an ongoing discussion within the field of cybersecurity. The methodology is reasonably sound, and the discussion is comprehensive. However, the limited scope of SAST tool comparison, the lack of detail on GPT-4 configuration, dataset specifics, SAST-Correct metric, and potential bias in the research limit the significance of the findings. The work lacks key details on costs, token size and the overall methodology that limits the reproducibility and overall scientific value of the experiments. While the paper offers a useful comparison, it doesn't represent a groundbreaking or highly novel contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15215v1)
- **Authors**: Yongqi Fan, Yating Wang, Guandong Wang, Jie Zhai, Jingping Liu, Qi Ye, Tong Ruan
- **Abstract**: Open-ended question answering (QA) is a key task for evaluating the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Compared to closed-ended QA, it demands longer answer statements, more nuanced reasoning processes, and diverse expressions, making refined and interpretable automatic evaluation both crucial and challenging. Traditional metrics like ROUGE and BERTScore struggle to capture semantic similarities due to different patterns between model responses and reference answers. Current LLM-based evaluation approaches, such as pairwise or listwise comparisons of candidate answers, lack intuitive interpretability. While pointwise scoring of each response provides some descriptions, it fails to adapt across different question contents. Most notably, existing methods overlook the distinction between factoid and non-factoid questions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{MinosEval}, a novel evaluation method that first distinguishes open-ended questions and then ranks candidate answers using different evaluation strategies. For factoid questions, it applies an adaptive key-point scoring strategy, while for non-factoid questions, it uses an instance-aware listwise ranking strategy. Experiments on multiple open-ended QA datasets, including self-built ones with more candidate responses to complement community resources, show that MinosEval better aligns with human annotations and offers more interpretable results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper "MinosEval" addresses the challenges of automatically evaluating open-ended question answering (QA) systems powered by Large Language Models (LLMs).  It argues that traditional metrics and existing LLM-based evaluation methods fail to adequately capture the nuances and complexity of open-ended QA, particularly because they do not account for the fundamental difference between factoid (fact-based) and non-factoid (creative/opinion-based) questions.

MinosEval proposes a two-stage approach:

1.  **Question Classification:** First, it uses an LLM to classify a given question as either factoid or non-factoid.
2.  **Tailored Evaluation:**
    *   For **factoid** questions, it uses an adaptive key-point scoring strategy, extracting key information from a reference answer and using Natural Language Inference (NLI) to determine if candidate answers entail these key points.
    *   For **non-factoid** questions, it employs an instance-aware listwise ranking approach, generating multiple silver-standard answers to enhance LLM ranking performance.

The authors create new datasets and demonstrate, through experiments, that MinosEval outperforms existing LLM-based evaluation approaches, aligning better with human annotations and providing more interpretable results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty (6/10):** The novelty of this paper is moderate.  While the idea of tailoring evaluation strategies based on question type (factoid vs. non-factoid) isn't entirely new in NLP generally, applying it specifically and systematically to open-ended QA evaluation with LLMs is a contribution.  The adaptive key-point scoring and instance-aware listwise ranking are also valuable techniques. However, both key-point extraction and NLI-based scoring are established techniques. The innovation lies in the way these methods are combined and adapted for this particular evaluation task.
*   **Significance (7/10):**  The paper addresses a significant problem: the need for more robust and interpretable evaluation metrics for open-ended QA. As LLMs become more sophisticated and are applied to tasks requiring nuanced reasoning and creative expression, the limitations of traditional metrics become more apparent. MinosEval's approach of distinguishing between factoid and non-factoid questions has the potential to significantly improve the reliability and accuracy of automated LLM evaluations. Better evaluation metrics drive better model development and deployment.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current open-ended QA evaluation techniques.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** MinosEval's two-stage approach is well-defined and explained. The details of each module (question classification, key-point extraction, NLI scoring, instance generation, listwise ranking) are provided.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate that MinosEval outperforms existing baselines on several open-ended QA datasets, and especially when more complex questions are taken into account.  The creation of new datasets is also a valuable contribution to the research community.
    *   **Interpretability:** One of the biggest strengths is that it provides better interpretability, making it easier to understand why a given answer was ranked the way it was. This enhances trust in the evaluation system.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The entire pipeline relies heavily on the performance of LLMs (for question classification, key-point extraction, and NLI scoring). The accuracy of MinosEval is therefore directly tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. The cascading error risk is only briefly mentioned and is real concern.
    *   **Complexity:** The system has lots of moving parts. It is a relatively complex system to implement and tune compared to simple metrics like ROUGE or BERTScore.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** The performance might be sensitive to the specific datasets and LLMs used. More extensive testing across a wider range of datasets and LLMs would be beneficial. The paper also mentions the language constraints of some of its metrics, so cross-lingual evaluation capabilities should be taken into account.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis:** Although the paper mentions the conducted error analysis, I would find it more compelling if the discussion were more detailed to show insights about failure cases for better future improvements.
*   **Potential Influence:** The work has the potential to influence the development of more sophisticated and nuanced LLM evaluation techniques. The distinction between factoid and non-factoid questions is a valuable conceptual contribution that could be adopted by other researchers. Furthermore, the new datasets created will serve as a valuable resource for the research community.

**Justification for Score:**

A score of 7/10 reflects the paper's significant contribution to the field while acknowledging its limitations. The work is novel in its specific application to open-ended QA evaluation with LLMs and its integrated approach. However, the reliance on LLMs and the inherent complexity of the system keep it from reaching a higher score.  If the authors had shown detailed error analysis and addressed the LLM-specific limitations more robustly, the novelty and significance scores could have been higher.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DM-FNet: Unified multimodal medical image fusion via diffusion process-trained encoder-decoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15218v1)
- **Authors**: Dan He, Weisheng Li, Guofen Wang, Yuping Huang, Shiqiang Liu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF) extracts the most meaningful information from multiple source images, enabling a more comprehensive and accurate diagnosis. Achieving high-quality fusion results requires a careful balance of brightness, color, contrast, and detail; this ensures that the fused images effectively display relevant anatomical structures and reflect the functional status of the tissues. However, existing MMIF methods have limited capacity to capture detailed features during conventional training and suffer from insufficient cross-modal feature interaction, leading to suboptimal fused image quality. To address these issues, this study proposes a two-stage diffusion model-based fusion network (DM-FNet) to achieve unified MMIF. In Stage I, a diffusion process trains UNet for image reconstruction. UNet captures detailed information through progressive denoising and represents multilevel data, providing a rich set of feature representations for the subsequent fusion network. In Stage II, noisy images at various steps are input into the fusion network to enhance the model's feature recognition capability. Three key fusion modules are also integrated to process medical images from different modalities adaptively. Ultimately, the robust network structure and a hybrid loss function are integrated to harmonize the fused image's brightness, color, contrast, and detail, enhancing its quality and information density. The experimental results across various medical image types demonstrate that the proposed method performs exceptionally well regarding objective evaluation metrics. The fused image preserves appropriate brightness, a comprehensive distribution of radioactive tracers, rich textures, and clear edges. The code is available at https://github.com/HeDan-11/DM-FNet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DM-FNet, a novel two-stage diffusion model-based network for unified multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF). The approach aims to address limitations of existing MMIF methods related to insufficient detail capture and cross-modal feature interaction.  In Stage I, a UNet is trained using a diffusion process for image reconstruction, enabling it to capture detailed information through progressive denoising and multilevel feature representation. In Stage II, a fusion network leverages features extracted at various noise levels during the diffusion process to improve feature recognition. The fusion network incorporates three key modules for adaptive processing of medical images from different modalities.  The overall framework balances brightness, color, contrast, and detail through a hybrid loss function, resulting in improved image quality and information density. Experimental results across different medical image types (MRI-CT, MRI-PET, MRI-SPECT) and other tasks demonstrate the method's superior performance compared to existing techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach by integrating diffusion models into a MMIF framework. Using a diffusion model to train a UNet for feature extraction is a sound strategy, as is the use of extracted features at different noise levels. However, the use of diffusion models in image fusion is not entirely new, though its application to *unified* multimodal medical image fusion is less explored.
* **Significance:** The paper is potentially significant due to the practical importance of MMIF in medical diagnosis. The proposed method addresses the challenges of fusing images from different modalities effectively. By enhancing feature extraction and cross-modal interaction, DM-FNet has the potential to improve the quality and accuracy of fused images, leading to better clinical insights. The experimental results seem promising, demonstrating improved performance in terms of objective evaluation metrics and visual quality. The extension to PC-GFP and IVIF shows potential for generalization.
* **Strengths:**
    *   The use of a diffusion model for feature extraction in MMIF is innovative.
    *   The two-stage approach allows for independent training and optimization of the feature extractor (UNet) and fusion network.
    *   The introduction of three key fusion modules (AMFF, MSFF) contributes to the adaptive processing of multimodal medical images.
    *   The hybrid loss function helps balance various image attributes, leading to better fusion results.
    *   Comprehensive experimental results across different medical image types and comparison with multiple state-of-the-art methods.
    *   The demonstration of strong generalizability through extensions to PC-GFP and IVIF with minimal fine-tuning.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational cost: The paper admits that it requires high GPU utilization, though the diffusion model isn't used in the inference stage. This is a relevant issue in resource-constrained situations.
    *   While the unified framework is a key focus, the experimental setup still involves some level of tuning across datasets, reducing the strength of claim for a totally unified model.  The discussion on "fine-tuning" the fusion network for IVIF raises questions about the generality of the approach.  Are the changes minimal, or are they significant enough to constitute a task-specific customization? This needs clarification.
    *   The objective evaluation metrics are standard, but subjective evaluations are limited. A more extensive perceptual study involving medical professionals could further validate the clinical relevance of the improved fusion results.
    *   While the paper emphasizes "unified" MMIF, the discussion could benefit from a more detailed analysis of why the specific network design and loss function enable this unification. What properties of the architecture and loss function are key for handling the variations between different MMIF tasks?
* **Potential Impact:** The paper can potentially have a moderate impact by offering a new strategy to incorporate diffusion models in MMIF. By effectively capturing important features while avoiding problems suffered by conventional methods, it could lead to higher accuracy and more comprehensive diagnosis. However, it's important to conduct a more detailed analysis of potential application scenarios.

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel approach with strong empirical results. However, it falls short of being a groundbreaking contribution. It integrates a relatively mature diffusion model into the MMIF field, addressing limitations that have been identified in earlier works. Furthermore, more information is needed to truly establish the 'unified' aspect of the approach and its generalizability, and the results are limited to standard datasets.
