- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### video-SALMONN 2: Captioning-Enhanced Audio-Visual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15220v1)
- **Authors**: Changli Tang, Yixuan Li, Yudong Yang, Jimin Zhuang, Guangzhi Sun, Wei Li, Zejun Ma, Chao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Videos contain a wealth of information, and generating detailed and accurate descriptions in natural language is a key aspect of video understanding. In this paper, we present video-SALMONN 2, an advanced audio-visual large language model (LLM) with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) designed for enhanced video (with paired audio) captioning through directed preference optimisation (DPO). We propose new metrics to evaluate the completeness and accuracy of video descriptions, which are optimised using DPO. To further improve training, we propose a novel multi-round DPO (MrDPO) approach, which involves periodically updating the DPO reference model, merging and re-initialising the LoRA module as a proxy for parameter updates after each training round (1,000 steps), and incorporating guidance from ground-truth video captions to stabilise the process. Experimental results show that MrDPO significantly enhances video-SALMONN 2's captioning accuracy, reducing the captioning error rates by 28\%. The final video-SALMONN 2 model, with just 7 billion parameters, surpasses leading models such as GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5-Pro in video captioning tasks, while maintaining highly competitive performance to the state-of-the-art on widely used video question-answering benchmarks among models of similar size. Codes are available at \href{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the video-SALMONN 2 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents video-SALMONN 2, an audio-visual large language model (LLM) designed to improve video (with audio) captioning.  The core innovations involve using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) and a novel multi-round directed preference optimisation (MrDPO) training method.  MrDPO addresses limitations of standard DPO by periodically updating the reference model, merging and re-initializing the LoRA module, and incorporating ground-truth caption guidance to stabilize training.  The authors also introduce new metrics for evaluating caption completeness and accuracy. Experimental results show that video-SALMONN 2 outperforms leading models like GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5-Pro in video captioning tasks while maintaining competitive performance on video question-answering benchmarks, despite being a relatively small 7B parameter model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the MrDPO training approach.  While LoRA and DPO are established techniques, the specific adaptation strategy using periodic reference model updates, LoRA proxy merging/re-initialization, and guided stabilization is a unique combination. The proposed metrics for evaluating video caption quality are also a valuable contribution, as this area has been lacking robust evaluation methodologies. The specific combination of these elements for audio-visual captioning is novel.

*   **Significance:** Improving video captioning is important for enhancing video understanding tasks. The paper shows a tangible improvement in caption quality by using MrDPO, achieving performance exceeding much larger commercial models. This is a significant result, demonstrating the potential of more efficient training strategies for multimodal LLMs. The fact that video-SALMONN 2 is a 7B parameter model achieving superior performance further enhances its practical significance. The new evaluation metrics are a positive step towards more reliable evaluation of video captioning.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The MrDPO training method provides a well-defined and justified approach to improve model stability and performance.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement in captioning accuracy and completeness, exceeding other leading models.
    *   The proposed evaluation metrics offer a more granular and reliable way to assess video caption quality, addressing a critical gap in the field.
    *   Code availability promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   The comprehensive evaluation and ablation analysis provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a closed-source base visual LLM and audio encoder somewhat limits the accessibility and reproducibility of the model for broader research and the precise comparisons that can be drawn. (The authors mentioned the visual base LLM in the first line of Model Specifications).
    *   While captioning performance is improved, video QA performance does not improve, suggesting potential trade-offs or limitations in how the captioning model is being trained or applied.
    *   The "atomic events" approach and use of GPT-3.5 for evaluation adds complexity.  While GPT models are powerful, their responses can be variable and subjective. (However, the use of these LLMs are a result of the sub-tasks being designed with that technology in mind)

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research on multimodal LLM training, particularly for tasks like video captioning. The MrDPO technique could be adapted for other tasks or modalities. The proposed metrics provide a valuable tool for researchers working on video captioning evaluation.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper offers a novel and effective training strategy (MrDPO) that achieves significant improvements in video captioning quality, exceeding the performance of much larger commercial models. The introduction of more reliable evaluation metrics for video captions is a valuable contribution. While the reliance on a pre-existing base LLM and evaluation GPT model present some limitations, the paper demonstrates a clear and significant advance in training multimodal models for video understanding. The code availability and comprehensive evaluation further strengthen the paper's contribution and its potential impact on the field. There are always areas for further improvement, such as the video QA results and the dependency on external LLMs, but the core innovations are substantial and well-executed.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Unit Testing: A Systematic Literature Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15227v1)
- **Authors**: Quanjun Zhang, Chunrong Fang, Siqi Gu, Ye Shang, Zhenyu Chen, Liang Xiao
- **Abstract**: Unit testing is a fundamental practice in modern software engineering, with the aim of ensuring the correctness, maintainability, and reliability of individual software components. Very recently, with the advances in Large Language Models (LLMs), a rapidly growing body of research has leveraged LLMs to automate various unit testing tasks, demonstrating remarkable performance and significantly reducing manual effort. However, due to ongoing explorations in the LLM-based unit testing field, it is challenging for researchers to understand existing achievements, open challenges, and future opportunities. This paper presents the first systematic literature review on the application of LLMs in unit testing until March 2025. We analyze \numpaper{} relevant papers from the perspectives of both unit testing and LLMs. We first categorize existing unit testing tasks that benefit from LLMs, e.g., test generation and oracle generation. We then discuss several critical aspects of integrating LLMs into unit testing research, including model usage, adaptation strategies, and hybrid approaches. We further summarize key challenges that remain unresolved and outline promising directions to guide future research in this area. Overall, our paper provides a systematic overview of the research landscape to the unit testing community, helping researchers gain a comprehensive understanding of achievements and promote future research. Our artifacts are publicly available at the GitHub repository: https://github.com/iSEngLab/AwesomeLLM4UT.
- **Summary**: This paper presents a systematic literature review of the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in unit testing, covering publications up to March 2025. It analyzes 105 relevant papers from the perspectives of both unit testing tasks and LLM usage, categorizing unit testing tasks that benefit from LLMs (e.g., test generation, oracle generation), discussing integration aspects (model usage, adaptation, hybrid approaches), summarizing challenges, and outlining future directions. The authors make their artifacts publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and rapidly growing area: the application of LLMs to software engineering, specifically unit testing. While LLMs have demonstrated impressive capabilities in various SE tasks, including code generation and program repair, their application in unit testing needs a structured overview. The paper aims to provide this overview by systematically reviewing the existing literature.

**Strengths:**

*   **Timeliness:** The review covers publications until March 2025, indicating its currency in a fast-evolving domain.
*   **Systematic Approach:** The authors use a well-defined methodology for literature selection (Quasi-Gold Standard strategy), minimizing bias and enhancing reproducibility.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The review is thorough, categorizing papers from both the unit testing and LLM perspectives, covering aspects like model usage, adaptation strategies, and hybrid approaches.
*   **Practical Contributions:** Identifying unresolved challenges and outlining promising future directions provides valuable guidance for researchers in the field.
*   **Publicly Available Artifacts:** Making the artifacts publicly available promotes transparency and allows for further investigation and validation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth in Evaluation Metrics:** The analysis of bug detection capabilities highlights dataset limitations. While mentioned, the impact of synthetic vs. real bugs and false positives could be explored in greater depth, along with potential mitigation strategies. The metrics analysis also focuses mainly on "test generation" and less on other areas like "Test Repair" and "Test Refactoring" which are rapidly growing fields.
*   **Bias Towards Commercial LLMs:** While the review acknowledges open-source alternatives, there seems to be a skew in favour towards "commercial" models for analysis. The implications of using closed-source vs. open-source LLMs on reproducibility, cost, and model transparency merit further critical assessment.
*   **Surface-Level Discussion of Specific Techniques:** Although the paper identifies the popular strategies for prompt engineering such as zero-shot and few-shot learning, it can expand on their practical implementations and their limitations in specific scenarios of Unit Testing.
*   **Lack of Comparison With Other SE Tasks:** While focusing on Unit Testing specifically, the paper misses out on making connections to LLMs in SE tasks, where LLMs have shown great efficiency. This comparison may have given readers some perspective.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper claims to be the first systematic literature review specifically on LLMs in unit testing. This is a significant claim, and if true, provides a clear contribution. It can guide research by identifying gaps in existing research and outlining promising avenues.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a valuable synthesis of existing research in a rapidly evolving area. However, the limitations concerning depth in evaluation, bias towards commercial LLMs and lack of connection with similar areas in SE limit the overall impact.
Thus, the score would be:
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Lost in Variation? Evaluating NLI Performance in Basque and Spanish Geographical Variants
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15239v1)
- **Authors**: Jaione Bengoetxea, Itziar Gonzalez-Dios, Rodrigo Agerri
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we evaluate the capacity of current language technologies to understand Basque and Spanish language varieties. We use Natural Language Inference (NLI) as a pivot task and introduce a novel, manually-curated parallel dataset in Basque and Spanish, along with their respective variants. Our empirical analysis of crosslingual and in-context learning experiments using encoder-only and decoder-based Large Language Models (LLMs) shows a performance drop when handling linguistic variation, especially in Basque. Error analysis suggests that this decline is not due to lexical overlap, but rather to the linguistic variation itself. Further ablation experiments indicate that encoder-only models particularly struggle with Western Basque, which aligns with linguistic theory that identifies peripheral dialects (e.g., Western) as more distant from the standard. All data and code are publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of current language technologies, specifically Large Language Models (LLMs), in understanding geographical variations of Basque and Spanish. The authors introduce XNLIvar, a novel, manually-curated parallel dataset in both languages that incorporates dialectal variations. Through cross-lingual and in-context learning experiments using both encoder-only and decoder-based LLMs, they demonstrate a performance drop when handling linguistic variations, particularly in Basque. Error analysis indicates this decline isn't due to lexical overlap but to the linguistic variation itself, with Western Basque proving particularly challenging.  The study also finds that LLM performance on Spanish variants might benefit from the larger presence of Spanish language content in pre-training data, and that orthographic variations pose a challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The creation and release of the XNLIvar dataset is a significant contribution. While prior work acknowledges the importance of language variation, manually-curated datasets that specifically target dialectal variations, particularly for a low-resource language like Basque, are rare. The use of NLI as a pivot task is also a reasonable choice for evaluating language understanding across variations. However, the methodology used for adaptation, though described, is somewhat vague and lacks concrete details on ensuring consistency and controlling for potential biases during dialectal adaptation.
* **Significance:** The findings highlight a crucial limitation of current LLMs: a difficulty in generalizing across dialectal variations. This is important for promoting fairness and inclusivity in NLP, as models trained primarily on standard language risk underperforming on dialectal data, potentially disadvantaging speakers of those dialects. The insights into the challenges posed by Western Basque align with linguistic theory, adding weight to the findings. The investigation of the impact of orthographic variations in Spanish is a valuable, albeit smaller, contribution. The error analysis is essential and provides actionable insights.
* **Strengths:**
    * Introduction of a valuable new dataset.
    * Clear demonstration of a performance gap with dialectal data.
    * Error analysis providing insights into the causes of the performance gap.
    * Consideration of a low-resource language (Basque) alongside a high-resource one (Spanish).
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The adaptation methodology could benefit from more detail and justification, particularly regarding inter-annotator agreement and bias mitigation. The reliance on manual adaptation, while necessary, introduces potential subjectivity. A more standardized and quantifiable adaptation approach would strenghten the results.
    * The proportion of Navarrese in the dataset is under-represented, limiting the generalizability of the results for that dialect.
    * While the paper examines the impact of lexical and phonological variations, there's limited discussion of the syntactic differences between dialects and their impact on NLI performance.
    * Claims regarding the specific effect of "orthographic changes" are based on potentially subjective interpretations from the error analysis and warrant further validation through controlled experiments.
    * The conclusion that better LLM performance with Spanish dialect is related to pre-training corpora could benefit from further analysis.
* **Impact:** This paper provides a valuable starting point for future research on language variation in NLP. The dataset can be used to benchmark models specifically designed to handle dialectal variations. The findings can inform the development of more robust and inclusive NLP systems.
* **Rigorous Rationale:** Despite the presence of some weaknesses, the paper introduces a useful dataset and provides evidence for an important issue in NLP - its sensitivity to geographical language variations. The rigorous experiments and error analysis justify the claim that language models have issues dealing with language variety. While the conclusions regarding the impact of orthography and the influence of pre-training data presence needs more analysis, the work opens a new avenue for researching language variation in NLP.

**Score: 7**

The paper makes a solid contribution by highlighting the challenges faced by language models when handling geographical variations, especially for under-resourced languages. The new dataset is a valuable resource.  While the adaptation methodology could be stronger and some of the conclusions need more robust support, the work is well-executed and impactful enough to receive a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Research on Graph-Retrieval Augmented Generation Based on Historical Text Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15241v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Fan, Zhang Qi, Xing Wenqian, Liu Chang, Liu Liu
- **Abstract**: This article addresses domain knowledge gaps in general large language models for historical text analysis in the context of computational humanities and AIGC technology. We propose the Graph RAG framework, combining chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to create a The First Four Histories character relationship dataset with minimal manual annotation. This dataset supports automated historical knowledge extraction, reducing labor costs. In the graph-augmented generation phase, we introduce a collaborative mechanism between knowledge graphs and retrieval-augmented generation, improving the alignment of general models with historical knowledge. Experiments show that the domain-specific model Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B, with Simplified Chinese input and chain-of-thought prompting, achieves optimal performance in relation extraction (F1 = 0.68). The DeepSeek model integrated with GraphRAG improves F1 by 11% (0.08-0.19) on the open-domain C-CLUE relation extraction dataset, surpassing the F1 value of Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B (0.12), effectively alleviating hallucinations phenomenon, and improving interpretability. This framework offers a low-resource solution for classical text knowledge extraction, advancing historical knowledge services and humanities research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Research on Graph-Retrieval Augmented Generation Based on Historical Text Knowledge Graphs" addresses the challenge of historical knowledge gaps in general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs).  It proposes a GraphRAG framework that combines chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to construct a character relationship dataset based on "The First Four Histories." The system introduces a collaborative mechanism between knowledge graphs and retrieval-augmented generation to enhance the alignment of general models with historical knowledge. Experimental results indicate that a domain-specific model (Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B) achieves good performance in relation extraction, and the integration of GraphRAG improves performance on open-domain datasets, mitigating hallucinations and improving interpretability. The framework offers a resource-efficient solution for classical text knowledge extraction, advancing historical knowledge services and humanities research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its specific application of GraphRAG to historical text analysis and the combination of several techniques. While GraphRAG itself is not entirely novel, the authors' approach of integrating it with chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction for dataset creation, and process supervision specifically tailored for historical text processing represents a reasonable incremental advance. The focus on minimal manual annotation in creating the character relationship dataset also adds to the practical novelty. The domain-specific fine-tuning using Xunzi series model is also a plus.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important and relevant problem: the lack of historical knowledge in general-purpose LLMs and the resulting inaccuracies in historical text analysis. The proposed GraphRAG framework offers a practical solution for enhancing LLMs with historical knowledge, and the experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness in improving relation extraction performance, mitigating hallucinations, and improving interpretability. The work holds practical significance because it offers a cost-effective method for digital preservation of ancient texts and humanistic research. This work facilitates downstream applications using knowledge enhanced historical information.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of historical knowledge gaps in LLMs and their impact on historical text analysis.
    *   **Well-defined framework:** The proposed GraphRAG framework is well-defined and integrates several relevant techniques in a coherent manner.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments to evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed framework, including comparisons with baseline models and ablation studies.
    *   **Practical focus:** The paper emphasizes the practical aspects of the framework, such as its ability to construct character relationship datasets with minimal manual annotation and its resource efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental contribution:** While the paper's application of GraphRAG to historical text analysis is novel, the individual components of the framework are not entirely novel. There could have been stronger emphasis on how GraphRAG improves on existing techniques to improve performance.
    *   **Limited evaluation dataset:** The evaluation dataset is confined to the Four Histories, which limits the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Lack of comparison with alternative methods:** The paper does not compare the proposed framework with other methods for enhancing LLMs with historical knowledge, such as fine-tuning with large historical datasets or using external knowledge bases.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of digital humanities and historical text analysis by providing a practical and effective framework for enhancing LLMs with historical knowledge. The framework can be used by researchers and practitioners to develop new applications for historical text analysis, such as knowledge extraction, question answering, and historical event reconstruction.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a solid contribution to the field by addressing a relevant problem and proposing a practical and effective framework for enhancing LLMs with historical knowledge. While the individual components of the framework are not entirely novel, the integration of these techniques and their application to historical text analysis represent a worthwhile contribution. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework, and the paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a resource-efficient solution for classical text knowledge extraction, advancing historical knowledge services and humanities research. Given its strengths and weaknesses, the paper deserves a score of:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unlocking Post-hoc Dataset Inference with Synthetic Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15271v1)
- **Authors**: Bihe Zhao, Pratyush Maini, Franziska Boenisch, Adam Dziedzic
- **Abstract**: The remarkable capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) can be mainly attributed to their massive training datasets, which are often scraped from the internet without respecting data owners' intellectual property rights. Dataset Inference (DI) offers a potential remedy by identifying whether a suspect dataset was used in training, thereby enabling data owners to verify unauthorized use. However, existing DI methods require a private set-known to be absent from training-that closely matches the compromised dataset's distribution. Such in-distribution, held-out data is rarely available in practice, severely limiting the applicability of DI. In this work, we address this challenge by synthetically generating the required held-out set. Our approach tackles two key obstacles: (1) creating high-quality, diverse synthetic data that accurately reflects the original distribution, which we achieve via a data generator trained on a carefully designed suffix-based completion task, and (2) bridging likelihood gaps between real and synthetic data, which is realized through post-hoc calibration. Extensive experiments on diverse text datasets show that using our generated data as a held-out set enables DI to detect the original training sets with high confidence, while maintaining a low false positive rate. This result empowers copyright owners to make legitimate claims on data usage and demonstrates our method's reliability for real-world litigations. Our code is available at https://github.com/sprintml/PostHocDatasetInference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper tackles the problem of Dataset Inference (DI) for Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically addressing the practical limitation that DI requires a held-out dataset with the same distribution as the suspected training data, which is often unavailable in real-world scenarios. The authors propose a novel approach to synthetically generate this held-out data using a data generator trained on the suspect dataset itself. To overcome potential distribution shifts introduced by the synthetic data, they introduce a post-hoc calibration technique using a dual-classifier approach to disentangle membership signals from distributional artifacts. Extensive experiments on various text datasets demonstrate that their method enables reliable DI with high confidence and low false positives.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using synthetically generated data for dataset inference is innovative and addresses a significant bottleneck in applying DI in practice. The dual-classifier calibration approach is also a valuable contribution, as it directly tackles the problem of distribution shifts that can undermine the reliability of DI. The combination of these techniques provides a practical and effective solution to a pressing problem.

**Significance:**  The ability to perform dataset inference is becoming increasingly important in the age of large language models, where unauthorized use of copyrighted material is a major concern. This work directly empowers data owners to verify whether their data has been used to train LLMs without their consent, fostering greater transparency and accountability. The practicality of the method makes it a significant contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a critical limitation:** The paper directly addresses the need for in-distribution held-out data for DI, which is the major bottleneck limiting its applicability in real-world situations.
*   **Novel approach:**  Using synthetic data generation combined with a clever calibration method is a novel way to address the problem.
*   **Solid methodology:**  The data generator based on suffix completion, and the dual-classifier calibration strategy, are well-reasoned and empirically sound.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The extensive experiments on diverse text datasets (single-author blogs and subsets of the Pile) demonstrate the robustness and generalizability of the proposed method.
*   **Clear and well-written:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on Language Model Quality:** The quality of the synthetic data is reliant on the quality and fine-tuning of the language model used for generation. While the paper focuses on suffix-completion, the performance of the proposed method can be tied to the quality of the generative model.
*   **Computational Cost:** Training both the generator and the dual-classifiers can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting its accessibility for some users. The ablation studies help to identify less computationally intensive model architectures.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While empirically strong, the paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of the properties and limitations of the proposed method. This would strengthen the contributions. For instance, under what conditions can we guarantee the synthetic data is "sufficiently" representative?

**Justification for Score:**

This paper offers a practical solution to a significant problem in the field of data ownership and LLM transparency. It overcomes a crucial bottleneck for DI, providing a valuable tool for data owners. The methodology is sound, and the experimental results are convincing. While the method relies on a LM model and lacks deep theoretical analysis, it offers a crucial stepping stone. This warrants a score of **8**.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Human Motion Capture from Loose and Sparse Inertial Sensors with Garment-aware Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15290v1)
- **Authors**: Andela Ilic, Jiaxi Jiang, Paul Streli, Xintong Liu, Christian Holz
- **Abstract**: Motion capture using sparse inertial sensors has shown great promise due to its portability and lack of occlusion issues compared to camera-based tracking. Existing approaches typically assume that IMU sensors are tightly attached to the human body. However, this assumption often does not hold in real-world scenarios. In this paper, we present a new task of full-body human pose estimation using sparse, loosely attached IMU sensors. To solve this task, we simulate IMU recordings from an existing garment-aware human motion dataset. We developed transformer-based diffusion models to synthesize loose IMU data and estimate human poses based on this challenging loose IMU data. In addition, we show that incorporating garment-related parameters while training the model on simulated loose data effectively maintains expressiveness and enhances the ability to capture variations introduced by looser or tighter garments. Experiments show that our proposed diffusion methods trained on simulated and synthetic data outperformed the state-of-the-art methods quantitatively and qualitatively, opening up a promising direction for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of full-body human pose estimation using sparse, loosely-attached inertial measurement units (IMUs). It challenges the prevalent assumption in existing IMU-based motion capture research that sensors are tightly strapped to the body. The authors propose a method that leverages transformer-based diffusion models, trained on a combination of simulated, synthetic, and real-world IMU data, to estimate poses from loosely worn IMUs.  A key aspect of their approach is the incorporation of garment-related parameters into the training process, which allows the model to better capture variations due to different garment fits. The paper presents experimental results on three datasets, demonstrating that their approach outperforms state-of-the-art methods both quantitatively and qualitatively.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a new task (full-body pose estimation from loosely attached IMUs) and a novel training strategy. Previous work on loose IMUs was limited to upper-body pose estimation.  The innovative aspect lies in the clever use of diffusion models, a secondary diffusion model for synthesizing loose-wear data, and, importantly, the incorporation of garment parameters to enhance realism and robustness. The method effectively integrates simulation and real-world data. The method shows an innovative approach by adding a hyperparameter, `alpha`, to weight the synthetic and real data to control for looseness in garment configurations.
*   **Significance:** Addressing motion capture with loosely attached IMUs is highly significant for real-world applications where user comfort and convenience are crucial. The reported results suggest that the proposed method can achieve robust and accurate pose estimation even with variations in garment looseness, moving towards more practical motion capture systems. The experimental results demonstrate clear improvements over existing approaches.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defines a new and relevant problem.
    *   Proposes a well-designed and effective solution based on modern diffusion models.
    *   The training strategy combining simulated, synthetic, and real data is a notable strength.
    *   Incorporating garment parameters is a valuable contribution to improving robustness.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of different components.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the garment parameter approach is innovative, the precise garment parameters used (TallThin/ShortFat Physique and the y-parameter) are limited to simulation. It could be argued that these are not direct garment parameters but physique/shape parameters that indirectly influence garment fit. The generalization of this aspect to real-world scenarios with varying garment types might be challenging.
    *   The "real-time inference" approach relies on a sliding window, limiting real-time performance. This approach needs to denoise and predict a new pose for every frame.
    *   Limited discussion on failure cases or limitations of the model.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of IMU-based motion capture by pushing the boundaries of what's possible with loosely attached sensors. It opens avenues for future research in:

    *   Exploring more sophisticated garment modeling techniques.
    *   Developing more efficient inference strategies for diffusion models to enable truly real-time performance.
    *   Investigating adaptive methods for automatically estimating garment parameters from sensor data.

**Score:** 8.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant advancement in IMU-based motion capture by addressing the challenging but practically relevant scenario of loosely attached sensors. The integration of diffusion models and garment parameters demonstrates considerable ingenuity. The experimental results convincingly show that the method outperforms the state-of-the-art. While the generalization of the garment parameter approach and the "real-time" performance could be improved, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. The potential influence on the field is high, making it a valuable contribution.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 8/10

### MEGC2025: Micro-Expression Grand Challenge on Spot Then Recognize and Visual Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15298v1)
- **Authors**: Xinqi Fan, Jingting Li, John See, Moi Hoon Yap, Wen-Huang Cheng, Xiaobai Li, Xiaopeng Hong, Su-Jing Wang, Adrian K. Davision
- **Abstract**: Facial micro-expressions (MEs) are involuntary movements of the face that occur spontaneously when a person experiences an emotion but attempts to suppress or repress the facial expression, typically found in a high-stakes environment. In recent years, substantial advancements have been made in the areas of ME recognition, spotting, and generation. However, conventional approaches that treat spotting and recognition as separate tasks are suboptimal, particularly for analyzing long-duration videos in realistic settings. Concurrently, the emergence of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and large vision-language models (LVLMs) offers promising new avenues for enhancing ME analysis through their powerful multimodal reasoning capabilities. The ME grand challenge (MEGC) 2025 introduces two tasks that reflect these evolving research directions: (1) ME spot-then-recognize (ME-STR), which integrates ME spotting and subsequent recognition in a unified sequential pipeline; and (2) ME visual question answering (ME-VQA), which explores ME understanding through visual question answering, leveraging MLLMs or LVLMs to address diverse question types related to MEs. All participating algorithms are required to run on this test set and submit their results on a leaderboard. More details are available at https://megc2025.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the MEGC 2025 paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces the Micro-Expression Grand Challenge (MEGC) 2025, focusing on two tasks: (1) ME spot-then-recognize (ME-STR) and (2) ME visual question answering (ME-VQA). ME-STR integrates spotting and recognition in a unified sequential pipeline, requiring algorithms to first detect and then classify micro-expressions within long videos. ME-VQA explores the use of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) or large vision-language models (LVLMs) for ME understanding through visual question answering, enabling a more interpretable and flexible approach to ME analysis. The paper outlines the datasets used, evaluation metrics, baseline methods, and preliminary experimental results for both tasks.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty*: The ME-VQA task is genuinely novel.  The application of MLLMs/LVLMs to micro-expression understanding is a relatively unexplored area. Reformulating ME analysis as a visual question-answering problem offers a fresh perspective.  The ME-STR task, while a continuation from a previous challenge, further emphasizes the importance of a unified approach to spotting and recognition, reflecting real-world scenarios better than treating them as separate tasks.  The specific datasets used (unreleased CAS(ME)3 clips) and the focus on long videos also add some novelty.

*Significance*: The significance of this work lies in its potential to advance the field of micro-expression analysis in several ways:

*   **Addressing a Practical Limitation:** By focusing on the ME-STR task, the challenge encourages research that tackles the difficulty of real-world ME analysis where spotting is a necessary first step. This addresses the limitations of current ME recognition approaches that assume pre-segmented data.
*   **Exploring Multimodal Approaches:** The ME-VQA task explores how powerful MLLMs/LVLMs can contribute to a richer understanding of MEs. This goes beyond simple classification and allows for more complex reasoning about emotions and related attributes.  The challenge potentially bridges the gap between computer vision and natural language processing for ME analysis.
*   **Benchmarking Progress:** The MEGC provides standardized datasets and evaluation metrics, enabling fair comparisons of different algorithms and driving progress in the field.
*   **Promoting Real-World Applicability:** The use of long videos and spontaneous MEs makes the challenge more relevant to real-world applications such as security, deception detection, and clinical psychology.

*Strengths*:

*   Clear problem formulation for both tasks.
*   Well-defined evaluation metrics.
*   Use of challenging and ecologically valid datasets.
*   Addresses relevant and timely research directions in ME analysis.
*   The ME-VQA is a very novel concept.

*Weaknesses*:

*   The preliminary results presented are rather weak. This is understandable as these are baseline results. However, they highlight the significant challenges associated with both tasks, particularly ME spotting in long videos.  It also means that there is still a lot of research required.
*   The paper describes one baseline method (MEAN) for ME-STR and Qwen2.5VL-3B for ME-VQA. This might limit the scope of the initial analysis presented. More baseline methods could make the challenge more comprehensive.

*Potential Influence*: The MEGC series has consistently driven progress in the ME analysis field. This challenge is likely to stimulate further research into ME spotting, recognition, and, more importantly, the integration of multimodal reasoning through MLLMs/LVLMs. The ME-VQA task may inspire new approaches to emotion understanding that go beyond traditional classification methods.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The challenge has a strong focus on real-world ME analysis and promotes the integration of advanced AI models. The novelty of the ME-VQA task and the focus on addressing the practical limitations of existing ME research (spotting in long videos) are significant contributions. The main weaknesses are the preliminary results. Although the focus is on the challenge, more comprehensive baseline would also boost the score. The challenge will likely influence future directions in ME analysis by promoting the use of MLLMs/LVLMs and encouraging research that focuses on more practical, real-world scenarios. The score reflects the significance of tackling complex and practical problems in the ME analysis field and the potential of MLLMs/LVLMs for this domain.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SecFwT: Efficient Privacy-Preserving Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models Using Forward-Only Passes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15307v1)
- **Authors**: Jinglong Luo, Zhuo Zhang, Yehong Zhang, Shiyu Liu, Ye Dong, Xun Zhou, Hui Wang, Yue Yu, Zenglin Xu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have transformed numerous fields, yet their adaptation to specialized tasks in privacy-sensitive domains, such as healthcare and finance, is constrained by the scarcity of accessible training data due to stringent privacy requirements. Secure multi-party computation (MPC)-based privacy-preserving machine learning offers a powerful approach to protect both model parameters and user data, but its application to LLMs has been largely limited to inference, as fine-tuning introduces significant computational challenges, particularly in privacy-preserving backward propagation and optimizer operations. This paper identifies two primary obstacles to MPC-based privacy-preserving fine-tuning of LLMs: (1) the substantial computational overhead of backward and optimizer processes, and (2) the inefficiency of softmax-based attention mechanisms in MPC settings. To address these challenges, we propose SecFwT, the first MPC-based framework designed for efficient, privacy-preserving LLM fine-tuning. SecFwT introduces a forward-only tuning paradigm to eliminate backward and optimizer computations and employs MPC-friendly Random Feature Attention to approximate softmax attention, significantly reducing costly non-linear operations and computational complexity. Experimental results demonstrate that SecFwT delivers substantial improvements in efficiency and privacy preservation, enabling scalable and secure fine-tuning of LLMs for privacy-critical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SECFWT, a novel privacy-preserving framework for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) using Secure Multi-Party Computation (MPC). SECFWT tackles the computational challenges of MPC-based fine-tuning by employing a forward-only tuning (FoT) paradigm, which eliminates the need for backward propagation and optimizer computations. It also introduces MPC-friendly Random Feature Attention (RFA) to approximate softmax attention, thereby reducing expensive non-linear operations. The authors demonstrate through experiments that SECFWT significantly improves efficiency and privacy preservation, enabling scalable and secure fine-tuning of LLMs for privacy-critical applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty of this paper lies in the combination of existing techniques (GFO, RFA, MPC) into a coherent framework for privacy-preserving LLM fine-tuning.  Specifically, it's the *first* MPC-based framework, specifically addressing and overcoming the challenges that prior works (focused primarily on inference) sidestepped.

*   **Forward-Only Tuning in MPC:** While forward-only tuning (GFO) exists, its application within an MPC context for fine-tuning large language models is novel.  The key challenge here is adapting the GFO to work efficiently with secret-shared data and the communication overhead inherent in MPC.
*   **MPC-Friendly RFA:** Adapting RFA to MPC involved tailored optimizations that exploit trigonometric periodicity and sum-to-product formulas, which addresses the inherent non-linearity (cosine functions)  that make it less suited for secure computation without these adaptations.  The specifically designed  privacy-preserving cosine function protocol is a practical contribution.

**Significance:**

The significance of this paper hinges on addressing a very real bottleneck in LLM development: the difficulty of fine-tuning models on private or sensitive data. By providing an efficient MPC-based solution, the authors open the door for more collaborative and privacy-compliant LLM development in domains like healthcare and finance.

*   **Practicality:** The reported experimental results demonstrate a substantial speedup (10.2x) and communication cost reduction (14.8x) compared to naive implementations of privacy-preserving fine-tuning.  These are meaningful improvements that could make MPC-based fine-tuning a viable option in practice.  Table 2, in particular, highlights the potential of "Fine-tuning as a Service" made possible by the efficiency gains.
*   **Impact on Privacy:** The paper directly addresses concerns about data privacy and ownership, which are critical roadblocks for LLM development using specialized data.  The framework contributes to trustworthiness and promotes data sharing, addressing legal compliance requirements.
*   **Limitations and Future Work:** The authors are upfront about limitations, like focusing on a specific model (RoBERTa) and not addressing all possible optimization techniques. This honesty lends credibility, but highlights areas for improvement. Scalability to larger LLMs and different architectures will be a crucial test. Also, the semi-honest security model, while common, isn't the strongest guarantee; future work could explore stronger security assumptions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper successfully tackles a significant problem in the field of LLMs (privacy-preserving fine-tuning) with a practical and well-evaluated framework. The approach is technically sound, and the experimental results are convincing. However, the framework relies on a combination of existing techniques, which lowers the novelty score slightly. The limitations mentioned by the authors also prevent a higher score. Nevertheless, the work significantly advances the state-of-the-art by providing a practical and efficient solution for a critical challenge, and lays the groundwork for further research in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### One-shot Face Sketch Synthesis in the Wild via Generative Diffusion Prior and Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15312v1)
- **Authors**: Han Wu, Junyao Li, Kangbo Zhao, Sen Zhang, Yukai Shi, Liang Lin
- **Abstract**: Face sketch synthesis is a technique aimed at converting face photos into sketches. Existing face sketch synthesis research mainly relies on training with numerous photo-sketch sample pairs from existing datasets. However, these large-scale discriminative learning methods will have to face problems such as data scarcity and high human labor costs. Once the training data becomes scarce, their generative performance significantly degrades. In this paper, we propose a one-shot face sketch synthesis method based on diffusion models. We optimize text instructions on a diffusion model using face photo-sketch image pairs. Then, the instructions derived through gradient-based optimization are used for inference. To simulate real-world scenarios more accurately and evaluate method effectiveness more comprehensively, we introduce a new benchmark named One-shot Face Sketch Dataset (OS-Sketch). The benchmark consists of 400 pairs of face photo-sketch images, including sketches with different styles and photos with different backgrounds, ages, sexes, expressions, illumination, etc. For a solid out-of-distribution evaluation, we select only one pair of images for training at each time, with the rest used for inference. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed method can convert various photos into realistic and highly consistent sketches in a one-shot context. Compared to other methods, our approach offers greater convenience and broader applicability. The dataset will be available at: https://github.com/HanWu3125/OS-Sketch
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a one-shot face sketch synthesis method using a generative diffusion prior and instruction tuning. It addresses the limitations of existing methods that rely on large datasets of photo-sketch pairs, which are often scarce and costly to acquire. The core idea is to optimize text instructions for a diffusion model using a single photo-sketch pair, and then use these instructions for inference on other photos. The paper introduces a new benchmark dataset, OS-Sketch, to evaluate the method's performance in real-world scenarios with diverse sketch styles and photo variations.  The proposed method involves two stages: optimizing text instructions by leveraging image reconstruction strategies from diffusion models and refining it via determined image editing direction. Additionally, it performs hybrid instruction tuning which is used to create an additional instruction for inference. Experiments demonstrate that the approach can generate realistic and consistent sketches from various photos in a one-shot setting, offering greater convenience and broader applicability than existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in adapting diffusion models for the one-shot face sketch synthesis task, and in using optimized text instructions derived from a single photo-sketch pair. Image editing using diffusion models with text prompts is not entirely new (e.g., InstructPix2Pix, GLIDE), but the application to face sketch synthesis with a strong emphasis on the one-shot setting is a distinct contribution. Creating the new OS-Sketch dataset is also a valuable contribution as it provides a more diverse benchmark for evaluating this kind of task. However, many papers in this field generate datasets of photos and corresponding sketches (CUHK Face Sketch, CUFSF, WildSketch, etc). It does not appear that OS-Sketch has done anything particularly groundbreaking, except combining samples of several existing datasets with several pictures sourced from the internet.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the data scarcity and cost issues associated with traditional face sketch synthesis methods.  A successful one-shot approach could have practical applications where it is difficult or impossible to obtain large datasets of matching face photos and sketches, in these cases a framework that reduces the required dataset to only one sketch-photo pair allows for easier application in different domains, such as forensics or art creation. OS-Sketch is the most challenging dataset, however, it might not be high-quality, for example, sketches from the internet would be of inconsistent quality. Also, the claim that the dataset's wide range of subjects makes it more realistic is questionable, since a forensic-context the dataset would be highly-homogenous.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proposed method offers a practical solution for face sketch synthesis with limited data.
    *   The introduction of the OS-Sketch dataset fills a gap in the availability of diverse and challenging benchmarks.
    *   The paper presents thorough experiments and comparisons to validate the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   It is convenient for real-world applications and offers broader applicability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty in instruction tuning with visual prompting is incremental rather than revolutionary, borrowing existing techniques and adapting them to a specific task. This is less groundbreaking research, instead, it adapts current technologies towards a specific application.
    *   The reliance on a pre-trained diffusion model may limit the method's adaptability to different sketch styles or domains. The results are decent, but could be improved with more careful dataset selection and instruction tuning.
    *   The quality of the OS-Sketch dataset can be questioned due to how the data was collected. The range of the OS-Sketch dataset may not match the actual data in a specific application (i.e. a forensic setting), so it might not be as realistic as it claims.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in face sketch synthesis by encouraging the development of more data-efficient methods. The OS-Sketch dataset could become a standard benchmark for evaluating one-shot or few-shot face sketch synthesis approaches.  However, the long-term impact will depend on the practical utility and robustness of the method in real-world applications.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution by addressing the data scarcity problem in face sketch synthesis. While the core ideas are built upon existing diffusion model techniques, the application to the one-shot setting and the creation of the OS-Sketch dataset represent a valuable advance. The approach's effectiveness is demonstrated through comprehensive experiments and comparisons. The main weaknesses are the incremental novelty in instruction tuning, the potential limitations of relying on a pre-trained model, and the potential concerns with the data collection for OS-Sketch dataset. These weaknesses, in addition to the lack of high-end breakthroughs, suggest that the research is important, but not extremely innovative.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 6/10

### When and How Unlabeled Data Provably Improve In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15329v1)
- **Authors**: Yingcong Li, Xiangyu Chang, Muti Kara, Xiaofeng Liu, Amit Roy-Chowdhury, Samet Oymak
- **Abstract**: Recent research shows that in-context learning (ICL) can be effective even when demonstrations have missing or incorrect labels. To shed light on this capability, we examine a canonical setting where the demonstrations are drawn according to a binary Gaussian mixture model (GMM) and a certain fraction of the demonstrations have missing labels. We provide a comprehensive theoretical study to show that: (1) The loss landscape of one-layer linear attention models recover the optimal fully-supervised estimator but completely fail to exploit unlabeled data; (2) In contrast, multilayer or looped transformers can effectively leverage unlabeled data by implicitly constructing estimators of the form $\sum_{i\ge 0} a_i (X^\top X)^iX^\top y$ with $X$ and $y$ denoting features and partially-observed labels (with missing entries set to zero). We characterize the class of polynomials that can be expressed as a function of depth and draw connections to Expectation Maximization, an iterative pseudo-labeling algorithm commonly used in semi-supervised learning. Importantly, the leading polynomial power is exponential in depth, so mild amount of depth/looping suffices. As an application of theory, we propose looping off-the-shelf tabular foundation models to enhance their semi-supervision capabilities. Extensive evaluations on real-world datasets show that our method significantly improves the semisupervised tabular learning performance over the standard single pass inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how unlabeled data can improve in-context learning (ICL), particularly within a semi-supervised setting using Gaussian Mixture Models (GMMs). It theoretically analyzes attention models with varying depths, demonstrating that: (1) single-layer linear attention models, while optimal in fully-supervised scenarios, fail to leverage unlabeled data; (2) multi-layer or looped transformers can effectively use unlabeled data by implicitly constructing estimators related to Expectation Maximization (EM); and (3) shallow models can suffice due to the exponential increase in polynomial power with depth. The paper proposes a looping strategy for tabular foundation models to enhance semi-supervision and shows empirical improvements on real-world datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in providing a theoretical understanding of how transformer depth enables the use of unlabeled data in ICL.  Previous works have focused primarily on fully supervised ICL, or have lacked a formal theoretical treatment of the role of depth in semi-supervised settings. The connection to EM and pseudo-labeling is interesting. However, the idea of using EM-like updates in the context of transformers is not entirely new, so the level of surprise is moderate. Furthermore, the theoretical analysis is specifically focused on linear attention and GMMs, limiting its broad applicability. The LoopTabFM algorithm seems more incremental.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a practically relevant issue: how to leverage the large amounts of unlabeled data available when high-quality labeled data is scarce. The analysis provides insights into architectural choices (depth, looping) for ICL, guiding future model design. The practical demonstration on tabular data, while specific, shows the potential of the theoretical findings in real-world applications. However, the contribution is incremental.
*   **Clarity and Rigor:** The paper is reasonably well-written and mathematically sound.  The theoretical proofs appear solid. The experimental setup is clearly described, and the results seem to support the theory, although there are some inconsistencies as they stated that model performances drops on the OpenML datasets which could be attibuted to factors such as noise levels in the raw data.
*   **Limitations:** The analysis is heavily dependent on the linear attention mechanism and the GMM assumption. This makes the results less generalizable to other attention mechanisms (e.g., softmax attention in deeper, non-linear models) or other data distributions.  The empirical evaluation is limited to tabular data and TabPFN. More extensive experiments on diverse datasets and modalities would strengthen the conclusions. The presented algorithm seems like an incremental improvement. Additionally, the paper doesn't explicitly delve into computational complexity of proposed algorithms.
*   **Impact:** The paper's impact will depend on how well its insights translate to more complex ICL settings. It provides a foundation for further research in semi-supervised ICL and inspires the design of more effective architectures. It's unlikely to be a paradigm shift but could contribute a solid piece to the understanding of transformer architectures.

**Justification:**

The paper provides a novel, if somewhat specialized, theoretical analysis of a practically important problem. The link to EM is interesting. However, the limitations related to the specific model and data assumptions prevent a higher score. The incremental nature of the empirical contribution and the somewhat narrow scope reduce the overall impact.
