### Creating User-steerable Projections with Interactive Semantic Mapping
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15479v1)
- **Authors**: Artur André Oliveira, Mateus Espadoto, Roberto Hirata Jr., Roberto M. Cesar Jr., Alex C. Telea
- **Abstract**: Dimensionality reduction (DR) techniques map high-dimensional data into lower-dimensional spaces. Yet, current DR techniques are not designed to explore semantic structure that is not directly available in the form of variables or class labels. We introduce a novel user-guided projection framework for image and text data that enables customizable, interpretable, data visualizations via zero-shot classification with Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). We enable users to steer projections dynamically via natural-language guiding prompts, to specify high-level semantic relationships of interest to the users which are not explicitly present in the data dimensions. We evaluate our method across several datasets and show that it not only enhances cluster separation, but also transforms DR into an interactive, user-driven process. Our approach bridges the gap between fully automated DR techniques and human-centered data exploration, offering a flexible and adaptive way to tailor projections to specific analytical needs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel user-guided approach to dimensionality reduction (DR) for image and text data. The key idea is to enable users to steer the projection dynamically by leveraging Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for zero-shot classification. This involves using natural language prompts to specify high-level semantic relationships of interest, which are then incorporated into the data representation before applying a standard DR technique like t-SNE or UMAP. This process fuses embeddings from the original data with embeddings of the text labels generated by the MLLM, allowing users to control the grouping of data points in the lower-dimensional space. The authors demonstrate the efficacy of their method across several datasets, showing improved cluster separation and interpretable visualizations tailored to specific analytical needs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of using MLLMs and zero-shot classification to steer DR via natural language prompts is the paper's primary novel contribution. While semantic alignment and embedding steering have been explored in the context of classification and clustering, applying them in a truly user-driven, unsupervised manner to DR is innovative. The experiments demonstrate how prompts can shape the projection to emphasize attributes not directly encoded in the data, which moves beyond traditional DR methods focusing on distance or neighborhood preservation. The technique goes beyond existing supervised DR methods by not expecting explicit class labels.

*   **Significance:** The paper bridges the gap between automated DR techniques and human-centered data exploration. By allowing users to express high-level analytical interests through natural language, the method makes DR more accessible and adaptable to specific needs. The ability to refine projections by varying the prompts and the fusion weight opens the door for more interactive and insightful data analysis. This potentially influences how researchers and practitioners visually explore and understand complex, high-dimensional datasets, particularly in domains like image and text analysis where semantic understanding is crucial. Also the tool provides novel capabilities over existing DR tools.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing DR techniques and the need for more user control and semantic integration.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The proposed method is well-defined and clearly explained, with a logical pipeline involving embedding, labeling, fusion, and projection.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments across diverse datasets (AG News, MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, CIFAR-10) demonstrate the broad applicability of the approach. The qualitative results, showcasing how prompts shape the projections, are compelling. The quantitative metrics also support the claim that the method preserves data structure while enhancing interpretability.
    *   **Thorough analysis of parameters and the results** The experiments show how different projection algorithms behave under varying parameter settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on MLLM Quality:** The performance of the method heavily relies on the accuracy and reliability of zero-shot classification from MLLMs like Qwen and CLIP. As the authors acknowledge, the zero-shot classification process is not perfect, and inaccurate labels can influence the projection negatively. This limitation highlights the need for robust MLLMs and careful prompt engineering.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper mentions that the proposed method is linear in the sample count, the text embedding and classification step is expensive especially considering very large datasets.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Metric Interpretability:** While the paper computes a wide range of quality metrics, they do not fully demonstrate the improvement of "Interpretability" for the users.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of future DR techniques and interactive data visualization tools. By demonstrating the power of MLLMs in steering projections, it opens the door for more sophisticated user-guided methods that incorporate semantic understanding. It could inspire research on prompt optimization for DR and the integration of other AI models for data exploration.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to user-steered DR, leveraging MLLMs for semantic integration. The experimental results demonstrate the efficacy of the method and its potential to enhance data exploration. While the reliance on MLLM quality and the computational cost are limitations, the paper offers a significant contribution by bridging the gap between automated DR techniques and human-centered data analysis. The potential for future research and the influence on the development of more intelligent and interactive visualization tools justify a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Context-Informed Grounding Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15480v1)
- **Authors**: Hyunji Lee, Seunghyun Yoon, Yunjae Won, Hanseok Oh, Geewook Kim, Trung Bui, Franck Dernoncourt, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Mohit Bansal, Minjoon Seo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are often supplemented with external knowledge to provide information not encoded in their parameters or to reduce hallucination. In such cases, we expect the model to generate responses by grounding its response in the provided external context. However, prior work has shown that simply appending context at inference time does not ensure grounded generation. To address this, we propose Context-INformed Grounding Supervision (CINGS), a post-training supervision in which the model is trained with relevant context prepended to the response, while computing the loss only over the response tokens and masking out the context. Our experiments demonstrate that models trained with CINGS exhibit stronger grounding in both textual and visual domains compared to standard instruction-tuned models. In the text domain, CINGS outperforms other training methods across 11 information-seeking datasets and is complementary to inference-time grounding techniques. In the vision-language domain, replacing a vision-language model's LLM backbone with a CINGS-trained model reduces hallucinations across four benchmarks and maintains factual consistency throughout the generated response. This improved grounding comes without degradation in general downstream performance. Finally, we analyze the mechanism underlying the enhanced grounding in CINGS and find that it induces a shift in the model's prior knowledge and behavior, implicitly encouraging greater reliance on the external context.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Context-Informed Grounding Supervision (CINGS), a post-training technique designed to improve the grounding ability of large language models (LLMs).  CINGS involves training the model with relevant external context prepended to the desired response, while the loss is calculated only over the response tokens.  The authors demonstrate that CINGS leads to enhanced grounding in both textual and visual domains. In text, CINGS outperforms standard instruction tuning and other grounding techniques, and is complementary to inference-time methods. In vision-language tasks, CINGS reduces hallucinations and maintains factual consistency. The paper also analyzes the mechanisms behind CINGS, suggesting it reduces reliance on prior knowledge and encourages greater attention to the external context.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of CINGS – using context as a prefix to the response during training while masking the context during loss calculation – is a relatively simple yet effective technique. While prior work has focused on either inference-time context incorporation or training objectives, the specific strategy of masked context prefixing for enhanced grounding seems to be novel, at least in its specific implementation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs. The prevalence of hallucinations is a major obstacle to the broader adoption of LLMs, and CINGS offers a potentially valuable tool for mitigating this issue. The fact that CINGS is complementary to inference-time techniques further enhances its practical relevance. The results, showing improvements across various benchmarks and modalities, suggest the technique is robust.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the CINGS method and its benefits. The figures and tables are informative.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets, modalities (text and vision-language), and model sizes. The comparison against various baselines and ablation studies provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of CINGS.
    *   **Mechanistic Analysis:** The analysis of how CINGS affects model behavior (reducing prior knowledge reliance, altering attention patterns) is valuable and contributes to a deeper understanding of the technique.
    *   **Practicality:** The approach can be readily applied as a post-training step to existing LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplicity:** While the simplicity of CINGS is a strength, it may also limit its potential impact. It might be considered a relatively incremental improvement rather than a paradigm shift.
    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The effectiveness of CINGS likely depends on the quality and relevance of the external context used during training. The paper could benefit from further investigation into the impact of different context sources or selection methods.
    *   **Limited Scale:** While the paper does use Llama 3.1, larger models such as GPT-4 are not tested. The benefits of CINGS might diminish at very large scales. Also, a limited training dataset may impact the observed performance improvement.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research on grounding techniques for LLMs. Its simplicity and effectiveness make CINGS a strong candidate for adoption and further exploration. Other researchers may build upon CINGS by exploring different context integration strategies or combining it with other training objectives. It also demonstrates an alternate simple approach to improve VLM training.

**Justification:**

CINGS tackles an important problem (hallucination in LLMs) with a practical and effective technique. The comprehensive experimental evaluation and insightful analysis provide strong evidence for its value. While the method itself is relatively straightforward, its impact on grounding performance and its complementarity to existing techniques warrant recognition. The weaknesses are relatively minor, and the paper is likely to stimulate further research in this area.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### GenHOI: Generalizing Text-driven 4D Human-Object Interaction Synthesis for Unseen Objects
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15483v1)
- **Authors**: Shujia Li, Haiyu Zhang, Xinyuan Chen, Yaohui Wang, Yutong Ban
- **Abstract**: While diffusion models and large-scale motion datasets have advanced text-driven human motion synthesis, extending these advances to 4D human-object interaction (HOI) remains challenging, mainly due to the limited availability of large-scale 4D HOI datasets. In our study, we introduce GenHOI, a novel two-stage framework aimed at achieving two key objectives: 1) generalization to unseen objects and 2) the synthesis of high-fidelity 4D HOI sequences. In the initial stage of our framework, we employ an Object-AnchorNet to reconstruct sparse 3D HOI keyframes for unseen objects, learning solely from 3D HOI datasets, thereby mitigating the dependence on large-scale 4D HOI datasets. Subsequently, we introduce a Contact-Aware Diffusion Model (ContactDM) in the second stage to seamlessly interpolate sparse 3D HOI keyframes into densely temporally coherent 4D HOI sequences. To enhance the quality of generated 4D HOI sequences, we propose a novel Contact-Aware Encoder within ContactDM to extract human-object contact patterns and a novel Contact-Aware HOI Attention to effectively integrate the contact signals into diffusion models. Experimental results show that we achieve state-of-the-art results on the publicly available OMOMO and 3D-FUTURE datasets, demonstrating strong generalization abilities to unseen objects, while enabling high-fidelity 4D HOI generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GenHOI paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GenHOI, a two-stage framework designed to synthesize 4D human-object interaction (HOI) sequences conditioned on text, even for objects not seen during training. The first stage uses an Object-AnchorNet trained on 3D HOI datasets to reconstruct sparse 3D HOI keyframes from human point clouds and object geometries. This stage avoids reliance on large 4D HOI datasets. The second stage employs a Contact-Aware Diffusion Model (ContactDM) to interpolate these sparse keyframes into dense, temporally coherent 4D HOI sequences.  ContactDM uses a Contact-Aware Encoder to extract contact patterns from the keyframes and a Contact-Aware HOI Attention mechanism to integrate these patterns into the diffusion process.  Experiments on OMOMO and 3D-FUTURE datasets demonstrate strong generalization to unseen objects and high-fidelity 4D HOI generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a novel two-stage approach that addresses a critical limitation in the field: the lack of large-scale 4D HOI datasets. By decoupling spatial and temporal modeling, it successfully generalizes to unseen objects. The Object-AnchorNet is a well-designed component, and the Contact-Aware Diffusion Model effectively leverages keyframe contact information. The Contact-Aware Encoder and HOI Attention mechanism are novel contributions for enhancing the quality of generated HOI sequences.

*   **Significance:** This work is significant because it tackles the crucial challenge of generalizing HOI synthesis to unseen objects, a major obstacle to deploying HOI systems in real-world scenarios.  The demonstrated ability to create realistic and coherent 4D HOI sequences with diverse objects has considerable potential for applications in AR/VR, robotics, and animation. The methodology is sound, and the extensive evaluations, including comparisons to SOTA and ablation studies, strengthen its impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong generalization capabilities:** The key contribution is demonstrably improved generalization to unseen objects.
    *   **Effective two-stage architecture:** The decoupling of spatial and temporal modeling is a smart design choice, addressing data limitations.
    *   **Contact-Aware Diffusion Model:**  The architecture is sound, and the method makes use of encoders that allow a level of detail that would not be available without.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Keyframe quality:** The performance is dependent on the accuracy of the Object-AnchorNet in the first stage. The paper acknowledges limitations regarding multi-object interactions, which restricts complexity.
    *   **Dataset limitations:** Although the paper addresses the lack of large-scale 4D HOI datasets, the generation of the data itself still has its own limitations which is noted in the paper.
    *   **Qualitative results are still not "perfect":** While improved over prior methods, the generated motions still have room for improvement in realism and physical accuracy (e.g., small penetrations might still be visible in some cases).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a promising direction for HOI synthesis, particularly by focusing on generalization. Its approach of decoupling spatial and temporal modeling, combined with contact-aware mechanisms, could inspire future research in this area.  The code and models being publicly available will also increase its impact.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper's novelty and significance are evident, limitations related to the reliance on the first stage and remaining challenges in perfect motion fidelity prevent a higher score. The advancements in generalization and the sound architectural design make it a meaningful contribution that can significantly impact the field. A score of 7 reflects the solid advancements made while acknowledging remaining challenges and opportunities for future improvements.

### SPARE: Single-Pass Annotation with Reference-Guided Evaluation for Automatic Process Supervision and Reward Modelling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15498v1)
- **Authors**: Md Imbesat Hassan Rizvi, Xiaodan Zhu, Iryna Gurevych
- **Abstract**: Process or step-wise supervision has played a crucial role in advancing complex multi-step reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, efficient, high-quality automated process annotation remains a significant challenge. To address this, we introduce Single-Pass Annotation with Reference-Guided Evaluation (SPARE), a novel structured framework that enables single-pass, per-step annotation by aligning each solution step to one or multiple steps in a reference solution, accompanied by explicit reasoning for evaluation. We show that reference-guided step-level evaluation effectively facilitates process supervision on four datasets spanning three domains: mathematical reasoning, multi-hop compositional question answering, and spatial reasoning. We demonstrate that SPARE, when compared to baselines, improves reasoning performance when used for: (1) fine-tuning models in an offline RL setup for inference-time greedy-decoding, and (2) training reward models for ranking/aggregating multiple LLM-generated outputs. Additionally, SPARE achieves competitive performance on challenging mathematical datasets while offering 2.6 times greater efficiency, requiring only 38% of the runtime, compared to tree search-based automatic annotation. The codebase, along with a trained SPARE-PRM model, is publicly released to facilitate further research and reproducibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPARE (Single-Pass Annotation with Reference-Guided Evaluation), a novel framework for automatic process supervision of large language models (LLMs) during multi-step reasoning. SPARE aims to improve the efficiency and quality of process annotation by aligning candidate solution steps with steps in a reference solution and providing explicit reasoning for each evaluation. This allows for single-pass annotation and multi-step aligned comparisons. The paper demonstrates SPARE's effectiveness across mathematical reasoning, multi-hop question answering, and spatial reasoning tasks. SPARE is shown to improve reasoning performance when used for fine-tuning models via offline reinforcement learning and for training reward models for ranking/aggregating LLM-generated outputs. SPARE also demonstrates computational efficiency compared to tree search-based annotation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the structured framework for single-pass, reference-guided evaluation. Previous work has explored process supervision, but SPARE's explicit focus on aligning solution steps with reference steps and providing justifications for the evaluation appears to be a distinct contribution.  The framework addresses the limitations of outcome-only rewards and expensive human annotation of process rewards.  The utilization of LLMs for evaluation is not inherently new, but the application within the SPARE framework and the resulting performance gains are novel.

**Significance:** SPARE addresses a crucial challenge: creating high-quality, efficient process supervision for LLMs to enhance their reasoning capabilities. This is significant for several reasons:

*   **Improved Reasoning:** The paper demonstrates that SPARE improves LLM reasoning performance across diverse tasks.
*   **Efficiency:**  The computational efficiency of SPARE relative to MCTS-based methods is a valuable practical benefit. This makes process supervision more accessible, especially when using paid LLM APIs for inference.
*   **Data Efficiency:** SPARE reuses reference solutions already available for supervised fine-tuning, avoiding the need for additional expensive annotation efforts.
*   **Broad Applicability:** The framework's performance across multiple domains suggests good generalizability.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Defined Framework:** SPARE's structured evaluation scheme with explicit reasoning and multi-step alignment is clearly defined and well-motivated.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on several datasets. The comparisons against baselines (self-consistency, outcome supervision, and MCTS-based methods) demonstrate the effectiveness of SPARE.
*   **Computational Efficiency:** The comparison against MCTS highlights a significant practical advantage.
*   **Reproducibility:** Releasing the code and trained models promotes further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on Reference Solutions:** The framework depends on the availability of reference reasoning traces, which might not always be available in every scenario. Although, the paper argues that these references are already needed for SFT, which is generally a first step in most training methodologies.
*   **LLM as Evaluator:** Like other LLM-based evaluation methods, SPARE is susceptible to noise and biases within the evaluation LLM. While the paper attempts to mitigate this through careful prompt design and in-context learning, this remains a potential limitation. Further investigation into the robustness of SPARE against different evaluator LLMs is warranted.
*   **Over-Parameterization:** The paper could benefit from further analysis on how varying the size of the LLM affects the overall result. Would the method remain computationally efficient if a larger model was used? Would it lead to even better results?
*   **Limited comparison with other reference-guided methods:** While the paper argues its differences with other works like GenRM, a more in-depth comparison would strengthen the paper.

**Impact and Potential Influence:**

SPARE has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM training and reasoning. Its efficiency and effectiveness could make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners seeking to improve LLM reasoning capabilities without incurring excessive annotation costs or computational overhead. The reference-guided approach might also inspire new methods for incorporating domain knowledge and constraints into LLM training.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper presents a novel and well-executed framework for automatic process supervision, demonstrating strong empirical results and computational efficiency. The core idea of reference-guided evaluation is a valuable contribution. However, the dependence on reference solutions and the inherent limitations of using LLMs as evaluators are limitations.  A more thorough comparison to existing reference-guided methods would bolster the paper further. The lack of analysis on LLM model size is a critical point to consider and would strengthen the result, especially given the computational efficiency claim. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, but there is still room for improvement and further investigation.

### Optimizing Web-Based AI Query Retrieval with GPT Integration in LangChain A CoT-Enhanced Prompt Engineering Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15512v1)
- **Authors**: Wenqi Guan, Yang Fang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models have brought a radical change in the process of remote learning students, among other aspects of educative activities. Current retrieval of remote learning resources lacks depth in contextual meaning that provides comprehensive information on complex student queries. This work proposes a novel approach to enhancing remote learning retrieval by integrating GPT-based models within the LangChain framework. We achieve this system in a more intuitive and productive manner using CoT reasoning and prompt engineering. The framework we propose puts much emphasis on increasing the precision and relevance of the retrieval results to return comprehensive and contextually enriched explanations and resources that best suit each student's needs. We also assess the effectiveness of our approach against paradigmatic LLMs and report improvements in user satisfaction and learning outcomes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel framework for enhancing web-based AI query retrieval, specifically for remote learning resources.  It integrates GPT models within the LangChain framework and leverages Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning and prompt engineering to improve the precision and relevance of retrieval results. The approach aims to provide comprehensive and contextually enriched explanations and resources tailored to individual student needs.  The authors evaluate their approach against existing methods using two datasets: the Paul Graham Essays Questions-Answers dataset and the Google Natural Questions dataset.  They demonstrate improved performance in terms of accuracy, precision, and mAP compared to baseline models like GPT-3.5 Turbo, GPT-4o, and BERT. The system incorporates the ability to query arXiv, webpages, and handle sophisticated requests. It utilizes a cache mechanism with Google Drive for more efficient query response and reduced API calls.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of existing technologies and techniques for a particular application.  While LangChain, GPT models, CoT reasoning, and prompt engineering are well-established, their integrated application to remote learning retrieval, with an emphasis on comprehensive context and tailored resources, represents a useful combination. The architecture also leverages Google Drive as a caching solution to make the system more efficient. This is more of an engineering-focused paper.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is moderate.  It addresses a relevant problem (improving remote learning resource retrieval) and demonstrates tangible improvements over baseline models on standard datasets. However, the gains are incremental rather than groundbreaking. The use of CoT and careful prompt engineering is a common technique, so the application, although beneficial, is not revolutionary.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing retrieval systems in handling complex and context-dependent student queries.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework is described in sufficient detail, outlining the components and their interactions.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on two standard datasets provide evidence of the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper addresses practical concerns such as response time, throughput, and API call reduction.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvements over baseline models are noticeable, but not dramatically large. The novelty is incremental and building upon existing work.
    *   **Limited Datasets:** The datasets used are standard, but somewhat limited in scope.  A more diverse and comprehensive set of educational datasets would strengthen the evaluation.  Also, the Paul Graham dataset is a single author dataset, which might skew results.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:**  The paper lacks ablation studies to isolate the individual contributions of CoT reasoning, prompt engineering, and the LangChain framework. It is unclear which components are most responsible for the observed improvements.
    *   **Modest Originality:** While the integration is useful, the individual components (GPT, Langchain, CoT, Prompt Engineering) are established. The application is more about system integration and engineering.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more effective and personalized remote learning retrieval systems. The framework could be adopted and adapted by other researchers and practitioners in the field. It highlights the importance of combining LLMs with knowledge retrieval and tailored prompts. However, the incremental nature of the contribution limits its potential to significantly transform the field.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed engineering effort to improve remote learning resource retrieval. The integration of GPT models within LangChain, combined with CoT reasoning and prompt engineering, leads to measurable improvements in accuracy and relevance. However, the improvements are incremental rather than transformative. The dataset selection could be strengthened. The novelty is not exceptionally high, as it relies on a combination of existing techniques. The lack of ablation studies makes it difficult to attribute the improvements to specific components. Overall, it is a worthwhile contribution that adds to the body of knowledge in the field but doesn't represent a major breakthrough. The score reflects the paper's moderate novelty, practical value, and potential influence, balanced against the limitations in dataset diversity, lack of ablation studies, and incremental improvement over established methods.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Lessons from Training Grounded LLMs with Verifiable Rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15522v1)
- **Authors**: Shang Hong Sim, Tej Deep Pala, Vernon Toh, Hai Leong Chieu, Amir Zadeh, Chuan Li, Navonil Majumder, Soujanya Poria
- **Abstract**: Generating grounded and trustworthy responses remains a key challenge for large language models (LLMs). While retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with citation-based grounding holds promise, instruction-tuned models frequently fail even in straightforward scenarios: missing explicitly stated answers, citing incorrectly, or refusing when evidence is available. In this work, we explore how reinforcement learning (RL) and internal reasoning can enhance grounding in LLMs. We use the GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) method to train models using verifiable outcome-based rewards targeting answer correctness, citation sufficiency, and refusal quality, without requiring gold reasoning traces or expensive annotations. Through comprehensive experiments across ASQA, QAMPARI, ELI5, and ExpertQA we show that reasoning-augmented models significantly outperform instruction-only variants, especially in handling unanswerable queries and generating well-cited responses. A two-stage training setup, first optimizing answer and citation behavior and then refusal, further improves grounding by stabilizing the learning signal. Additionally, we revisit instruction tuning via GPT-4 distillation and find that combining it with GRPO enhances performance on long-form, generative QA tasks. Overall, our findings highlight the value of reasoning, stage-wise optimization, and outcome-driven RL for building more verifiable and reliable LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Lessons from Training Grounded LLMs with Verifiable Rewards":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of training large language models (LLMs) to generate grounded and trustworthy responses, especially in the context of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). It proposes a two-stage reinforcement learning framework called Ground-GRPO, based on Group Relative Policy Optimization, which uses verifiable outcome-based rewards to improve answer correctness, citation quality, and refusal skills. The framework first optimizes answer and citation behavior using rewards focused on accuracy and sufficient citations. Then, it trains the model to refuse answering unanswerable questions by introducing a refusal-specific reward. The experiments on multiple QA benchmarks (ASQA, QAMPARI, ELI5, ExpertQA) show that reasoning-augmented models, especially with Ground-GRPO, outperform instruction-tuned models. The paper further explores the combination of distillation (using GPT-4) with Ground-GRPO, demonstrating complementary benefits for grounded response generation, especially for long-form QA tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds on existing work in RAG and reinforcement learning for LLMs, but introduces a novel hierarchical reward system and two-stage training approach specifically designed to address grounding. The combination of GRPO with a focus on verifiable rewards targeting answer correctness, citation sufficiency, and refusal is a significant contribution. The insights on the interplay between reasoning capabilities and RL effectiveness are also noteworthy. However, the use of GRPO itself is not entirely novel, as it is an existing RL method.
*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to significantly impact the development of more reliable and trustworthy LLMs. The identified benefits of reasoning models and the effectiveness of Ground-GRPO in improving grounding metrics are valuable contributions to the field. The findings regarding the complementary effects of distillation and RL, along with the insights on model-specific reward tuning, provide practical guidance for training grounded LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper provides extensive experimental results across multiple datasets and model architectures, supporting its claims.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of grounded response generation and provides a well-structured methodology.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings offer practical guidelines for training grounded LLMs, including the benefits of reasoning, staged optimization, and outcome-driven RL.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper offers a detailed analysis of the results, discussing the impact of different training strategies and reward designs on various aspects of grounding.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements are statistically significant, the absolute gains in some metrics may be considered incremental, especially when combining all techniques.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The reinforcement learning process can be computationally expensive, and the paper does not explicitly address the practical challenges of scaling the proposed framework to larger models and datasets.
    *   **Limited Ablation on Reasoning:**  It would have been valuable to include a more detailed ablation study specifically dissecting different reasoning approaches.
    *   **Reliance on Existing RL Method:** The core RL method, GRPO, isn't novel. The paper's contribution lies in its application and reward engineering.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings can influence the development of future LLM training techniques, leading to more reliable and trustworthy models for information-seeking applications.  The combination of distillation and RL with a focus on verifiable rewards could become a standard practice in the field. The distinction between short-form and long-form QA tasks when applying different techniques is a valuable observation.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper contains thorough experimental work to back the suggested method of reinforcement learning with a staged approach and careful reward shaping to enhance both factual groundedness and also refusal capabilities, in order to avoid making up an answer. While the core RL algorithm is not a novelty, the practical insights offered by the paper regarding the training of models in a retrieval-augmented context make it a useful piece.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of grounded LLMs by proposing a practical and effective training framework (Ground-GRPO) and providing valuable insights into the role of reasoning, staged optimization, and distillation. While the improvements are somewhat incremental and the core RL method is not new, the comprehensive experiments, clear problem definition, and practical guidelines make it a significant addition to the existing literature.  The identified weaknesses are relatively minor and do not significantly detract from the overall value of the paper.  It is a substantial research contribution with solid practical implications for building more reliable LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diff-TONE: Timestep Optimization for iNstrument Editing in Text-to-Music Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15530v1)
- **Authors**: Teysir Baoueb, Xiaoyu Bie, Xi Wang, Gaël Richard
- **Abstract**: Breakthroughs in text-to-music generation models are transforming the creative landscape, equipping musicians with innovative tools for composition and experimentation like never before. However, controlling the generation process to achieve a specific desired outcome remains a significant challenge. Even a minor change in the text prompt, combined with the same random seed, can drastically alter the generated piece. In this paper, we explore the application of existing text-to-music diffusion models for instrument editing. Specifically, for an existing audio track, we aim to leverage a pretrained text-to-music diffusion model to edit the instrument while preserving the underlying content. Based on the insight that the model first focuses on the overall structure or content of the audio, then adds instrument information, and finally refines the quality, we show that selecting a well-chosen intermediate timestep, identified through an instrument classifier, yields a balance between preserving the original piece's content and achieving the desired timbre. Our method does not require additional training of the text-to-music diffusion model, nor does it compromise the generation process's speed.
- **Summary**: The paper "Diff-TONE: Timestep Optimization for iNstrument Editing in Text-to-Music Diffusion Models" proposes a novel approach for instrument editing in text-to-music diffusion models. The core idea is to leverage the observation that diffusion models progressively refine the audio: first, overall structure, then instrument characteristics, and finally audio quality. The method identifies an optimal intermediate timestep to swap the instrument name in the text prompt. This is done by monitoring an instrument classifier's predictions and substituting the prompt when the classifier's prediction stabilizes. This allows the model to retain the original content while modifying the instrument timbre, without retraining the diffusion model or significantly impacting generation speed. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach, Diff-TONE, compared to baseline methods like random or midpoint timestep selection, and a related approach, MusicMagus.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper exhibits a moderate level of novelty. The idea of leveraging the timestep dimension to control the refinement process of diffusion models has been touched upon in related fields, such as image editing. However, its application to instrument editing in text-to-music diffusion models, especially using an instrument classifier to dynamically identify the optimal timestep for prompt manipulation, presents a unique contribution.

The significance lies in improving the controllability and flexibility of text-to-music generation. While text-to-music models are rapidly improving, precise control over specific aspects like instrumentation remains a challenge. Diff-TONE offers a computationally efficient way to achieve this without retraining or extensive modifications to the existing diffusion model architecture.  This makes it a practical approach for musicians and composers to refine and experiment with their generated music.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and Intuitive Approach:** The concept of using an instrument classifier to identify the point at which instrument information is infused is well-motivated and easy to understand.
*   **Computational Efficiency:** The method doesn't require retraining or complex optimizations, making it readily applicable with existing pre-trained text-to-music diffusion models.
*   **Demonstrated Effectiveness:** The experiments show that Diff-TONE outperforms basic baselines in terms of content preservation (Chroma) and overall generation quality (KAD).
*   **Practical Relevance:** The approach directly addresses the challenge of controlling specific aspects of music generation, increasing its value for real-world creative applications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Classifier Quality:** The performance of Diff-TONE heavily relies on the accuracy and robustness of the instrument classifier. The paper acknowledges this but does not explore the limitations extensively.
*   **Limited Exploration of Failure Cases:** The paper mentions the cases where the classifier may never change its prediction, preventing editing. While this is touched upon, a more thorough analysis of such failure cases would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Limited Comparison:** While MusicMagus is a relevant baseline, more extensive comparisons against alternative techniques for timbre transfer or music editing (even if requiring more complex implementations) would provide a stronger assessment of Diff-TONE's competitive standing.
*   **Lack of Qualitative Examples:** Although the authors state sound examples are provided at the demo page, qualitative results within the paper could support the explanation of the results.

**Potential Influence:**

Diff-TONE has the potential to influence research in controllable music generation and editing. The concept of using timestep optimization combined with external classifiers could inspire further exploration of controlling various aspects of generated music. The simplicity and computational efficiency of the approach make it a viable starting point for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a moderately novel and practically relevant approach for instrument editing. While it has some weaknesses, the strengths in terms of simplicity, efficiency, and demonstrated effectiveness warrant a reasonably positive score. The reliance on classifier quality and the relatively limited exploration of failure cases prevent a higher score. It doesn't represent a groundbreaking paradigm shift, but contributes a solid, useful method to the field. The fact that it avoids retraining of large generative models is an important consideration, making this potentially useful for practitioners.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 6/10

### Intrinsic and Extrinsic Organized Attention: Softmax Invariance and Network Sparsity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15541v1)
- **Authors**: Oluwadamilola Fasina, Ruben V. C. Pohle, Pei-Chun Su, Ronald R. Coifman
- **Abstract**: We examine the intrinsic (within the attention head) and extrinsic (amongst the attention heads) structure of the self-attention mechanism in transformers. Theoretical evidence for invariance of the self-attention mechanism to softmax activation is obtained by appealing to paradifferential calculus, (and is supported by computational examples), which relies on the intrinsic organization of the attention heads. Furthermore, we use an existing methodology for hierarchical organization of tensors to examine network structure by constructing hierarchal partition trees with respect to the query, key, and head axes of network 3-tensors. Such an organization is consequential since it allows one to profitably execute common signal processing tasks on a geometry where the organized network 3-tensors exhibit regularity. We exemplify this qualitatively, by visualizing the hierarchical organization of the tree comprised of attention heads and the diffusion map embeddings, and quantitatively by investigating network sparsity with the expansion coefficients of individual attention heads and the entire network with respect to the bi and tri-haar bases (respectively) on the space of queries, keys, and heads of the network. To showcase the utility of our theoretical and methodological findings, we provide computational examples using vision and language transformers. The ramifications of these findings are two-fold: (1) a subsequent step in interpretability analysis is theoretically admitted, and can be exploited empirically for downstream interpretability tasks (2) one can use the network 3-tensor organization for empirical network applications such as model pruning (by virtue of network sparsity) and network architecture comparison.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the internal and external organization of the self-attention mechanism within transformer networks. It presents theoretical evidence, supported by computational examples, for the invariance of the self-attention mechanism to the softmax activation function under certain regularity assumptions. The paper utilizes paradifferential calculus and proposes a methodology based on hierarchical tensor organization to analyze the structure of attention heads. This organization allows for tasks like network sparsity analysis and model pruning. The authors demonstrate the utility of their findings using both vision (ViT) and language (Transformer-XL) transformers, showcasing potential implications for interpretability and network applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of paradifferential calculus for theoretically establishing softmax invariance with the questionnaire method for empirical tensor organization represents a novel approach. Applying these tools to the specific problem of understanding self-attention in transformers is also a fresh perspective. However, the individual techniques (paradifferential calculus, hierarchical tensor organization) are not entirely new; the novelty lies in their application and integration in this context. Also, Theorem 2 and Corollary 1 have a restriction that requires the attention heads to be organized to have mixed a-Holder regularity and it also requires the attention head to be equivalent up to large constants.

*   **Significance:** The potential implications for interpretability and model compression are significant. The theoretical foundation for softmax invariance could simplify interpretability analysis by suggesting that certain transformations don't fundamentally alter the learned representations. Furthermore, analyzing network sparsity using the proposed methods could lead to more efficient model pruning strategies. The paper makes a strong argument for its significance in these fields, but it also acknowledges that the questionnaire algorithm can be sensetive to certain parameters, so further evaluation and development of the methodology is necessary to fully realize its practical advantages.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical grounding with the use of paradifferential calculus.
    *   The proposed hierarchical tensor organization method offers a structured way to analyze attention heads.
    *   Empirical validation on established transformer models (ViT and Transformer-XL).
    *   Clear articulation of potential applications in interpretability and model pruning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical result of softmax invariance relies on restrictive assumptions, which may limit its general applicability.
    *   Computational examples needed the attention head to be a power of 2, requiring to be cropped from 197 to 128. This constraint may also limit the general applicability and also may lose data.
    *   The questionnaire algorithm relies on parameters that can be sensetive.
    *   The leap from the theoretical results and the empirical setup of network applications may require refinement
    *   The empirical results, while supportive, are somewhat preliminary and could benefit from more extensive experiments and comparisons with existing techniques.
    *   Practical implementation of model pruning techniques based on the sparsity analysis is not fully demonstrated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach of combining theoretical and empirical tools could influence future research on transformer interpretability and optimization. The focus on tensor organization and sparsity analysis provides a valuable framework for understanding and manipulating these models. If the practical benefits for model pruning and efficient inference can be convincingly demonstrated in further work, the paper's impact could be substantial.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper exhibits a good level of novelty through the combined application of paradifferential calculus and hierarchical tensor organization to understand attention mechanisms. The theoretical insights into softmax invariance, while reliant on assumptions, offer a valuable perspective. The potential implications for interpretability and model pruning are promising, but the limited empirical validation and lack of concrete implementation of those applications hold back the paper's immediate impact. Despite these weaknesses, the work presents a sound and original contribution, making it worthy of publication.
